**Round 26: security found a real gap in round 23's own revoke-on-discard restructuring -- the first time this review has found the *inverse* direction of that bug class (revoking something that should have been kept, not failing to revoke something that should have been discarded); architecture found a test-hygiene issue in the same file.** Security review did a genuinely fresh, first-principles trace of `gmail_oauth_callback_endpoint`/`handle_oauth_callback` end to end rather than re-running the exhausted guard-coverage/field-typing sweeps, and found that round 23's `pending_revokes`/`finally` restructuring (drained "whether the transaction committed or rolled back," by design, for good reason on every other entry) applies that same unconditional draining to the reactivation branch's *old*-credential entry too -- but that entry's revoke is only actually correct if the `UPDATE` a few lines later, which is supposed to replace that credential, goes on to commit. Every other queued entry in this function is a credential that was never going to be persisted regardless of commit/rollback (the just-exchanged new grant, discarded outright in the `active`-row branch or orphaned by an outright `INSERT`/success-path-tail failure) -- draining those unconditionally is correct. The reactivation branch's old credential is different in kind: revoking it is only justified *because* it's about to be overwritten, and if the transaction instead rolls back (a dropped connection, a deadlock, `statement_timeout` -- the same transient-failure classes rounds 10-12 already named for this exact function), the DB row is left exactly as it was before the request -- still pointing at that same credential -- while the credential itself has already been killed at Google. Round 11/12's own test for this exact code path only ever exercised a `disconnected` prior status, where the team's own stated reasoning ("the old credential -- revoked correctly, unaffected") implicitly assumed the credential was already presumed-dead regardless; that reasoning does not extend to `error`/`rate_limited`/`permission_lost` rows, whose stored credential can be (and, for `error` specifically, typically is) still genuinely live -- an `error` status there just means an unrelated sync failure, not necessarily a bad credential. Dynamically confirmed via a real Postgres repro (reactivate an `error`-status row with a live credential, fail the branch's own post-`UPDATE` re-`SELECT` via the established `_fail_next_matching_execute` monkeypatch, observe the old credential gets revoked at Google while the DB row is left completely unchanged -- still `error`, still pointing at what is now a dead credential, no local signal anything happened); confirmed the bug is real and not merely theoretical for this codebase's actual `ConnectorStatus` set, though currently latent for `gmail` specifically since nothing in Task 1's own shipped scope can yet put a `gmail` row into `error`/`rate_limited`/`permission_lost` (`backfill`/`incremental_sync` are still stubbed and never raise; `refresh_permissions` has no HTTP caller yet) -- this becomes live the moment Task 2 wires up a real, fallible sync or a permission-refresh scheduler, the same "currently a defense-in-depth gap, not yet a live leak" framing rounds 7/12 used for structurally similar not-yet-reachable branches in this same handler. Fixed by splitting the queue into `pending_revokes` (drained unconditionally, unchanged) and a new `pending_revokes_on_commit` (drained only if a `committed` flag -- set immediately after `create_session`'s `with` block exits normally, i.e. only if no exception propagated past it -- is `True`); only the reactivation branch's old-credential entry moved to the new list, since it is the only one whose correctness is actually contingent on commit. Mutation-confirmed: reverting the fix (routing that one entry back through the always-drain list) reproduces the original bug exactly. `test_oauth_callback_revokes_new_grant_when_reactivation_update_raises`'s own assertion updated (`revoked_tokens` no longer includes the old credential, since its replacing `UPDATE` is the very statement that fails in that test; added an assertion that the row's stored credential is untouched) plus a new dedicated test, `test_oauth_callback_reactivation_failure_preserves_still_live_credential_of_error_row`, covering the `error`-status case specifically (failing a *later* statement than the `UPDATE` itself, to prove this isn't just the same scenario the sibling test already covers). Full regression sweep: `tests/test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` (72 passed, up from 69 -- 3 from a concurrent architecture-lens split of one bundled test, 1 from this fix's own new test), `tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` (39 passed), `tests/test_production_security.py` (48 passed, 2 skipped), plus the same nine other engineering-domain suites round 23 swept (367 passed) -- no regressions. Static checks (`.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --check`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 181 source files) clean. Architecture review found `test_oauth_callback_creates_connector_account_and_is_workspace_isolated` had grown into a 127-line test bundling three unrelated scenarios (create+audit-event, replay-dedup, workspace-isolation) into one, deviating from this file's own established one-test-per-scenario convention -- split into three separately-named tests with every original assertion/setup line preserved, no behavioral change. Both reviewers this round correctly self-diagnosed the shared-working-tree process hazard (per round 24's note) when each observed the other's in-flight, uncommitted work mid-review, rather than mistaking it for a finding. Because both lenses found and fixed real issues, round 26 does not count as clean: round 27 must be clean on both lenses, and round 28 after it, for the loop to close.

**Round 27: both lenses clean -- the first clean round since round 21.** Security review did a fresh, first-principles re-trace of the round-26 `pending_revokes`/`pending_revokes_on_commit`/`committed` mechanism (confirming `committed` is set only after `create_session`'s `with` block exits with no exception, and that every branch queues into the correct list), re-checked `disable_connector_endpoint` for the same commit-contingent bug class (structurally immune -- its revoke call sits textually after `with session.begin():` with no `finally`, so any exception inside the transaction skips the revoke entirely, unlike `gmail_oauth.py`'s always-runs `finally`), and swept the full adapter registry for the same "revoke assumed unconditionally safe" pattern. That sweep surfaced a factual inaccuracy (not a behavioral gap) in round 23's own claim: `GmailAdapter.disconnect()` is not actually the first `disconnect()` in this registry to make a real, blocking outbound HTTPS call -- `gitlab_adapter.py`'s own `disconnect()` (Phase 6 Task 3, predating this task) already does via `DELETE /personal_access_tokens/self`, and already benefits from `disable_connector_endpoint`'s own generic, provider-agnostic round-23 phase split (no gitlab-specific code path was ever needed for that fix to apply). Zero behavioral or security effect -- `disable_connector_endpoint`'s fix was never gmail-specific -- so not treated as a review finding, but corrected directly in `gmail_oauth.py`'s own round-23 comment (now: not the first `disconnect()` with a real network call, but still the first *reconnect-path* call site, the only one additionally holding a row lock rather than just a pooled connection) since a comment actively misleading a future reader is worth fixing on sight regardless of round-boundary bookkeeping. Also traced auth/CSRF/workspace-isolation end to end on every Gmail endpoint and swept credential handling for logging/audit/response leaks -- nothing found. Architecture review independently traced the round-26 mechanism's naming/comments for clarity, confirmed the two round-26 tests are meaningfully distinct (not near-duplicates), re-checked `IMPLEMENTATION-STATUS.md` and the Task 1 implementation plan for staleness, and declined to propose refactoring the active-row/reactivation branch duplication given prior rounds' deliberate decision to keep them separate for reviewability. Both reviewers reported zero findings; full regression sweep clean (159 passed, 2 skipped -- 72 gmail + 39 engineering-connectors + 48 production-security); static checks (`.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --check`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 181 source files) clean. **Round 27 is clean on both lenses -- the first of the two consecutive clean rounds needed to close the loop. Round 28 must also be clean on both lenses.**

**Round 28: both lenses clean -- the second consecutive clean round. Loop 2 review closes here.** Security review took genuinely new angles rather than re-verifying round 27's ground: rate limiting/abuse on the OAuth endpoints (`POST /oauth/start` under the standard mutation limiter, `GET /oauth/callback` explicitly allowlisted into `_MUTATING_GET_PATHS` since round 15), timing side channels (`hmac.compare_digest` throughout, no secret-vs-secret comparison in the allowlist check), error-message information disclosure (every post-exchange rejection funnels through `_sanitize_adapter_error`; no credential or bearer token ever reaches an exception string, `last_error` column, or response body), allowlist edge cases (case-fold, whitespace-strip, blank-entry filtering, fail-closed-on-empty -- all correct and tested), state-token replay (safe by design: Google's own authorization code is single-use even though the state itself isn't), real concurrent-request races (re-verified via the existing `threading.Event`/`ThreadPoolExecutor` lock-release proofs), and migration `0069`'s two new tables under adversarial data (moot for this task -- confirmed via `grep` that no application code in this PR writes to `email_threads`/`email_messages` yet; Task 2's own scope). Found nothing. Architecture review stepped back to read the entire PR diff as one coherent unit (23 files, +5017/-34 against the true merge-base, not the ~60-file diff a naive `git diff main` suggests against a stale local `main`) rather than continuing to inspect individual files in isolation -- confirmed the Protocol split, registration workaround, `since`-parameter widening, deferred-revoke mechanism, and schema are all mutually consistent and each incompleteness is honestly disclosed; independently re-derived every numeric claim in this document from the actual test files (`grep -c 'def test_'` against all three suites) rather than trusting prior rounds' arithmetic, confirming 72 + 39 + 48/2skip = 159/2skip exactly; and gave a considered, non-rhetorical answer to whether this review's accumulated comment density has become a net negative (`gmail_oauth_callback_endpoint` is ~50% comment by line count) -- concluded net positive, since the comments encode non-obvious correctness invariants in code shaped exactly like something a future "simplification" would silently break (collapsing `pending_revokes`/`pending_revokes_on_commit` back into one list looks like harmless cleanup but reintroduces round 26's bug), with the only genuine, mild critique being the round-number citations reading as review archaeology rather than pure rationale -- not worth rewriting 25 rounds of cross-referenced comments this late for a cosmetic gain. Found nothing requiring a fix. Both reviewers' full regression sweeps: 159 passed, 2 skipped; static checks (`.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 181/301 files depending on scope) clean; `git status` clean throughout both reviews, no changes made by either lens. **Rounds 27 and 28 are both clean on both lenses -- the exit condition for Loop 2 is met. PR #114 proceeds to merge.**

**Deferred, disclosed rather than silently dropped (Task 1's own remaining gaps as of Task 2).** `handle_webhook` is still stubbed -- push-notification-based sync remains explicitly deferred, not reconfirmed (see Task 2 evidence below). The `GmailAdapter.__init__` `sleep` constructor parameter, unused at the end of Task 1, is now real -- Task 2's rate-limit-retry mechanism (`_request_with_rate_limit_retry`) uses it.

## Task 2 evidence

**Complete.** `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-04-phase-10-gmail-connector.md`'s Task 2: `GmailAdapter.backfill`/`incremental_sync` real for `resource_type="message"` (`gmail.metadata`-only -- headers, not bodies; `email_messages.body`/`snippet` stay `NULL` until a future task's `gmail.readonly` fetch), sender/recipient resolution into `pkos_nodes`, `ResourceType` (`connector_accounts.py`) widened to accept `"message"`. `email_threads`/`email_messages` themselves needed no new migration (Task 1's own migration `0069` already created both), and `entity_aliases.alias_type` is a free-form `VARCHAR(50)` with no CHECK constraint to widen -- but this task's original "Migration: none needed" claim here was itself wrong about a third closed set: see the round-1 architecture-review paragraph below, which found `sync_cursors.resource_type`'s own `ck_sync_cursors_resource_type` CHECK constraint still needed widening (and migration `0070` doing exactly that), the same way migration `0069` already widened `ck_connector_accounts_provider`/`ck_personal_domains_domain_key` for `gmail`/`email`.

**Round 1 (architecture/quality) review: the "Migration: none needed" claim above was wrong -- `sync_cursors.resource_type`'s CHECK constraint still needed widening, and without it the real `/sync` endpoint was broken for every Gmail message sync that made progress.** `ResourceType`'s own module comment in `connector_accounts.py` states it "Matches migration `0044_phase6_connector_platform.py`'s `ck_sync_cursors_resource_type` CHECK constraint exactly," and every prior widening of this Literal (migration `0051`, for `monitor`/`service_definition`/`dashboard`) widened both the Python Literal and this DB constraint together as one atomic change. This task's own diff widened only the Literal. Confirmed live, not merely inferred: a direct `INSERT INTO sync_cursors (..., resource_type, ...) VALUES (..., 'message', ...)` against this sandbox's real Postgres raised `psycopg.errors.CheckViolation` before the fix. `sync_connector_endpoint`'s success path (`connector_accounts.py`) performs exactly this INSERT (`ON CONFLICT ... DO UPDATE`) whenever `outcome.next_cursor is not None` -- true for essentially every Gmail message sync that observes at least one `historyId`, i.e. the normal case, not an edge case -- so every real `POST /connectors/{account_id}/sync` call for a `gmail` account's `"message"` resource type that made any progress at all raised an uncaught `IntegrityError` inside the same transaction as `sync_runs`' own success-status `UPDATE`, rolling that back too and surfacing as an unhandled 500 instead of the app's structured error envelope. This task's own 17 new tests never caught it because they call `GmailAdapter.backfill`/`incremental_sync` directly, bypassing `sync_cursors` entirely; Task 1's own `test_sync_endpoint_reused_as_is_for_a_gmail_account` had in fact named this exact gap in advance in its own docstring ("Task 2 has not yet added a Gmail-appropriate value to `connector_accounts.ResourceType`/`ck_sync_cursors_resource_type`") but that test itself still uses `resource_type="repository"`, so the gap shipped anyway. Fixed with migration `0070_gmail_sync_cursor_type.py` (same drop/recreate shape as migration `0051`'s own widening), plus a new `_MessageCursorAdapter`-based end-to-end test in `tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` proving a `gmail`-provider `/sync` call with a real `next_cursor` succeeds through the actual route (not just the adapter in isolation). Verified directly against real Postgres 16: reproduced the `CheckViolation` before the fix, confirmed it clears after `alembic upgrade head`, confirmed `\d sync_cursors`'s CHECK constraint now includes `'message'`.

**Round 1, second finding: `_MAX_MESSAGES_PER_CALL` hit mid-page could report `status="succeeded"` while silently skipping unfetched messages.** `_sync_messages`/`_sync_history`'s shared per-call budget (`calls_made`) is checked inside the inner per-message `for` loop, `break`-ing out once exhausted -- but the code after that loop unconditionally checked `nextPageToken` next, with no signal that the `break` had fired for "ran out of budget" rather than "processed every ref in this page." When the budget-exhausting page also happened to be Gmail's own *last* page (no `nextPageToken` -- not merely possible but the common case once a workspace's mailbox is smaller than a few multiples of `_MAX_MESSAGES_PER_CALL`), the method took the "fully caught up" branch and returned `status="succeeded"` with a `next_cursor` implying nothing older remained, despite some of that very page's own messages never having been fetched or written -- silent, permanent data loss (not merely deferred to the next call, since `incremental_sync` would trust the returned cursor and never revisit the gap). Reproduced directly: a 5-message single page against a monkeypatched `_MAX_MESSAGES_PER_CALL = 3` reported `status="succeeded"`/`items_processed=3` while only 3 of 5 `email_messages` rows were ever written. Fixed by tracking whether the per-message loop's `break` was budget-exhaustion (a new `budget_exhausted` flag) versus a normal page-refs exhaustion, and returning the existing bottom-of-function `partial` outcome (the one an interrupted *multi*-page sync already correctly gets) whenever it was -- no new outcome shape, just routing this one case to the branch that was already correct for the sibling case. Applied identically to `_sync_history`'s structurally identical loop. Verified via two new mutation-confirmed tests (`test_backfill_reports_partial_not_succeeded_when_budget_exhausted_mid_page`, `test_incremental_sync_reports_partial_not_succeeded_when_budget_exhausted_mid_page` in `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`): both fail with the fix reverted (`status == 'succeeded'` instead of the expected `'partial'`), pass with it restored.

**Round 1, coverage gap closed: multi-page `messages.list`/`history.list` pagination had no test.** Every one of this task's original 17 tests exercised a single-page response (`nextPageToken` absent); the `while`/`pageToken` loop that walks a real multi-page response had never been exercised at all. Closed with `test_backfill_paginates_across_multiple_messages_list_pages`/`test_incremental_sync_paginates_across_multiple_history_list_pages`, each asserting the second page's own `pageToken` query parameter is the value the first page's `nextPageToken` supplied, and that both pages' messages land in the database.

**Round 1 (security/correctness) review: a single crafted message could permanently wedge a Gmail connector account's sync -- two distinct instances of the same root cause.** `_process_message`'s own docstring states a malformed message is "skipped ... rather than raising," but that guarantee only covered shape (missing/wrong-type fields), never the two ways Postgres itself can reject an otherwise well-typed `From`/`To`/`Subject` header value: (1) `email_messages.sender`/`.recipients` are `VARCHAR(320)` (migration `0069`), but `email.utils.parseaddr` has no length limit of its own -- a header with no `<...>` delimiter (or one whose address portion is simply very long) parses successfully into an address of whatever length the raw header happened to be; (2) Postgres `text`/`varchar` columns can never store a `0x00` byte at all, regardless of width, and Gmail's own JSON response body can carry a `\u0000` escape that `json.loads` decodes into a real NUL character in the resulting Python string, reaching `sender`/`recipients`/`canonical_name`/`subject` no differently than any other header byte. Both previously reached the `INSERT`/entity-resolution write uncaught (`psycopg.errors.StringDataRightTruncation`/`psycopg.DataError`), aborting the *entire* sync call over one message -- and for `incremental_sync` specifically, permanently: since the call never reaches the `return` that advances `next_cursor` past the offending message, the identical message is re-fetched and crashes again on every subsequent call, wedging that connector account until manual intervention, from a single crafted (or merely unlucky) email a sender fully controls the header content of. Reproduced directly against real Postgres for both cases before fixing (an address of length 413 via `"x"*400 + "@example.test"`, and a JSON-escaped `\u0000` in both the address and display-name halves of a `From` header, and independently in a `Subject` header). Fixed at the single existing chokepoint both sender/recipient parsing already shares (`_parse_address`, extended to reject on `_MAX_EMAIL_ADDRESS_LENGTH` and a new `_contains_nul` check against both the address and display-name halves -- treating the whole header as unparseable, matching the function's own existing "can't make sense of it" contract) plus one new guard where `Subject` is read directly (`email_threads.subject` is nullable, so a NUL-carrying subject only drops that one field rather than the whole message, unlike a NUL in the address). Verified via five new mutation-confirmed tests in `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` (`test_oversized_from_address_is_skipped_not_a_crash`, `test_oversized_recipient_address_is_dropped_but_message_still_syncs`, `test_nul_byte_in_from_address_is_skipped_not_a_crash`, `test_nul_byte_in_from_display_name_is_skipped_not_a_crash`, `test_nul_byte_in_subject_is_dropped_but_message_still_syncs`): each reproduces the original uncaught `DataError` with its corresponding guard reverted, and passes with the guard restored.

**`owner_id` sourced from `connector_accounts` directly, not threaded through the Protocol.** `ConnectorAccountContext` (the argument every `ConnectorAdapter` method receives) carries no `owner_id` -- Phase 6's connector model is workspace-shared, not per-owner, and widening the Protocol to add one would ripple through every existing adapter for a field only Gmail needs. `connector_accounts.owner_id` already exists (migration `0063`'s Phase 8 authz-visibility widening, defaulted via a `_NEW_OWNER_FROM_CREATED_BY` trigger to `created_by` -- `gmail_oauth.py`'s own INSERT sets both explicitly to the connecting user anyway) and is read back directly by `_owner_id_for_account`. Verified the trigger assumption holds against this sandbox's real Postgres rather than merely inferring it from the migration's own comment -- `test_owner_id_is_populated_from_created_by` seeds a `connector_accounts` row the same way `seeded_gmail_account`'s other tests implicitly depend on (no explicit `owner_id` in the INSERT) and asserts the backfilled value directly.

**Entity resolution is match hierarchy level 3, not a new resolution mechanism.** `docs/phases/phase-002/ENTITY-RESOLUTION-CONTRACT.md`: "exact normalized workspace-scoped identifier such as verified email" is deterministic -- attach to an existing entity via `entity_aliases`, or create one outright; never a fuzzy `resolution_candidates` row (that machinery, and Phase 6's own explicit deferral of wiring `changes.author_external_id`/`reviews.reviewer_external_id` into it, is for level-5 *ambiguous* matches this exact-identifier case never needs). `_resolve_or_create_person` queries `entity_aliases` first; on a miss, creates a `pkos_nodes` `person` entity, a `pkos_evidence` row (`source_type='gmail_sync'`, `source_ref='gmail:<message_id>'` -- `entity_aliases.source_id` has a `NOT NULL` FK to `pkos_evidence`, so this is required, not incidental), then the `entity_aliases` row itself, inside one short transaction opened independently of whatever transaction (if any) is writing the triggering `email_messages` row -- a concurrent resolution race for the same email (`uq_entity_aliases_workspace_type_value`) only ever risks re-running this one function (caught via `IntegrityError`, re-queries for the winner's row), never rolling back an unrelated message write alongside it. Verified: a second message from an already-seen sender/recipient reuses the existing `pkos_nodes` row rather than creating a duplicate (`test_backfill_writes_messages_and_resolves_participants`'s own `entity_aliases` count assertion); `_parse_address` uses `email.utils.parseaddr` (stdlib), not a hand-rolled regex, to split a `"Display Name <addr@example.com>"` header into both parts, feeding the display name into `pkos_nodes.canonical_name` rather than defaulting every resolved person to their bare email address.

**Backfill and incremental sync deliberately hand off a cursor only once fully caught up.** `backfill`'s own bounded pagination (`_MAX_MESSAGES_PER_CALL`, mirroring `github_adapter.py`'s `_MAX_PAGES_PER_CALL`) means a single call can legitimately not reach the end of its requested window -- if it doesn't, `next_cursor` stays `None` rather than handing `incremental_sync` a `historyId` that would silently skip whatever historical mail the interrupted call never got to. Only a `status="succeeded"` result (query fully paginated to exhaustion) sets `next_cursor` to the highest `historyId` observed across every message processed that call, or -- for the edge case of a window with zero messages, where no `historyId` was ever observed from a message resource -- a fallback `users.getProfile` call (the only other Gmail endpoint reporting the mailbox's current `historyId`), so a completely quiet account doesn't permanently defer back to `backfill` on every subsequent sync.

**`incremental_sync`'s expired-cursor fallback is a disclosed design decision beyond the plan document's own words.** The plan names only "polling-based ... via historyId cursor," not what happens when Gmail's `history.list` reports the cursor has aged out of its retention window (a `404`) -- the only correct recovery is a fresh `backfill`, since there is no partial-history replay possible once the server has dropped the record. Silently raising instead would strand a long-idle connector account requiring a manual disconnect/reconnect to recover; `_sync_history` calls `self.backfill(account, "message", since=None)` on a 404 rather than propagating it.

**Consent re-verified per message, not merely once per call.** Plan Task 2: "each re-invocation re-verifies the `email` domain's `domain_consents` row is still active at call time, not merely at original connect time." A revoked-before-the-call consent raises outright (`_email_consent_active` checked before any Gmail call at all); a consent revoked *during* a long backfill (between two messages in the same call) is re-checked immediately before each message's own metadata fetch, halting the call with `status="partial"` and every message processed before the revocation already durably written -- not merely a best-effort "check once at the top" that a mid-call revocation could race past. Verified via `test_backfill_halts_when_consent_is_revoked_mid_window`, which revokes consent as a side effect of the first message's own fetch call (via instance-level monkeypatching of `_request_with_rate_limit_retry`) and asserts the second message is never even requested.

**Rate limiting distinguishes Gmail's quota-error `403` from every other `403`.** Gmail's `429`s are always rate limiting; its `403`s are not -- `insufficientPermissions`/account-suspended/etc. all also return `403`, distinguishable only by the response body's own `error.errors[].reason` field. Treating every `403` as rate-limited (the naive port of GitHub's own `X-RateLimit-Remaining`-header check, which has no Gmail equivalent) would misreport a real, non-transient failure as `"partial"` (implying a later retry will resolve it) instead of raising -- silently masking a problem that will never clear on its own. `_is_rate_limited` checks the reason against a `rateLimitExceeded`/`userRateLimitExceeded`/`quotaExceeded` allowlist; a bare `429` skips that check entirely (status code alone is sufficient). Verified both directions: `test_backfill_degrades_to_partial_when_rate_limited_beyond_the_bound`/`test_backfill_succeeds_after_one_rate_limited_retry` (mirroring `github_adapter.py`'s own bounded-wait-then-one-retry shape, `_RATE_LIMIT_MAX_WAIT_SECONDS = 5.0`) and `test_a_genuine_403_is_not_treated_as_rate_limiting` (an `insufficientPermissions` reason raises, not degrades).

**Verified (original Task 2 pass).** `.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff` clean across `backend/`, `scripts/` and `tests/`; `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend` clean (181 source files). Full dynamic suite against real local Postgres 16: `tests/test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` (72 passed -- three Task 1 tests renamed/re-scoped to reflect that `backfill`/`incremental_sync` are no longer stubs, assertions otherwise unchanged; that file's own "Covers" list item 22 still cited the pre-rename names for all three until round 6 review corrected it, see this document's own round-6 paragraph below), `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` (17 passed, new this task), `tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` (39 passed), `tests/test_production_security.py` (48 passed, 2 skipped), plus the same nine other engineering-domain suites Task 1's own review swept for the `ResourceType` widening's blast radius (`test_engineering_authz_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_datadog_sync_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_decisions_incidents_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_github_sync_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_gitlab_sync_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_jira_sync_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_metrics_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_query_endpoints_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_write_actions_postgres.py` -- 367 passed) -- no regressions anywhere a `ResourceType` Literal change could plausibly reach. **Superseded by round 1 review, final counts re-verified independently after both lenses' changes landed in the same working tree** (paragraphs above): migration `0070` round-tripped a third time (upgrade/downgrade/re-upgrade) directly against real Postgres 16, confirming `ck_sync_cursors_resource_type` includes `'message'` after upgrade and excludes it after downgrade. `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` alone is 31 passed (17 original + 9 architecture-lens mid-page-budget/pagination/malformed-shape tests + 5 from the security-lens's own oversized-address/NUL-byte tests). `tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` is 40 passed (39 + 1 new end-to-end `sync_cursors` regression test). The combined `test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` (72) + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` (31) + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` (40) + `test_production_security.py` (48/2 skip) run is **191 passed, 2 skipped**. The same nine other engineering-domain suites re-run clean (367 passed, unchanged). Both DoS guards (`_MAX_EMAIL_ADDRESS_LENGTH`, `_contains_nul`) and the `budget_exhausted` fix were independently mutation-confirmed a second time (all 7 of their own tests fail with the corresponding guard reverted, pass restored) as part of this re-verification, not merely trusted from the reviewing agents' own reports.

**Round 2: both lenses found real issues -- a second failure class in Gmail-sourced string handling, and two minor doc-drift issues.** Security review found two further, distinct instances of round 1's own "untrusted string from Gmail's response reaches a DB column unguarded" bug class: (1) an unpaired UTF-16 surrogate escape (valid per strict JSON grammar, but not `.encode("utf-8")`-able -- orthogonal to both the NUL-byte and length guards round 1 added) previously reached Postgres as an uncaught, un-typed `UnicodeEncodeError`, reachable via a malformed RFC 2047 encoded-word header Gmail's own decoder resolves leniently; closed with a new `_contains_unpaired_surrogate` check applied everywhere `_contains_nul` already was, plus extending validation (length/NUL/surrogate, via a new `_is_valid_external_id`) to Gmail's own `id`/`threadId` fields, which had no validation at all despite flowing into the same `VARCHAR(255)`-bounded columns as everything else. (2) A naive `to_header.split(",")` silently corrupted the entity graph on a `To` header whose display name legitimately contains a comma (`"Doe, John" <john@example.test>`) -- not a crash, but *worse*: the quoted comma splits the address into two garbage fragments, `parseaddr` on each separately loses the real `john@example.test` address entirely while fabricating a non-email "recipient" (`Doe`) that both passed every length/NUL guard and got written to `email_messages.recipients` and resolved into a real `pkos_nodes` person. Fixed by switching to `email.utils.getaddresses` (stdlib, comma-list-aware) and factoring the shared validation out of `_parse_address` into `_validate_address` so both the single-address `From` path and the multi-address `To` path apply it identically. Architecture review, running concurrently, found two doc-drift issues -- `tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py`'s own module docstring "Covers" list hadn't been updated for round 1's `_MessageCursorAdapter`/new test (closed with item 25), and this document's own round-1 "final counts" paragraph had the two lenses' own test-addition counts swapped when originally written (fixed to the correct "9 architecture-lens ... + 5 security-lens ..." breakdown, independently re-verified here via `git diff` against the pre-round-1 commit: architecture added 2 budget-exhausted + 2 pagination + 5 `_process_message` guard-clause tests = 9; security added 2 oversized-address + 3 NUL-byte tests = 5). Both real, both fixed; round 2 does not count as clean on either lens. All four new round-2 tests (`test_unpaired_surrogate_in_from_display_name_is_skipped_not_a_crash`, `test_unpaired_surrogate_in_message_id_is_skipped_not_a_crash`, `test_oversized_message_id_is_skipped_not_a_crash`, `test_to_header_display_name_with_comma_does_not_corrupt_recipients`) independently re-verified via mutation testing (all four fail against the pre-round-2 code, pass with the fix restored) as part of this re-verification pass, not merely trusted from the reviewing agents' own reports. Final counts: `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` is now 35 passed (31 + 4); combined suite (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 35 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip) is **195 passed, 2 skipped**; the same nine engineering-domain suites re-run clean (367 passed, unchanged). Static checks (`.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean. Round 3 must be clean on both lenses, and round 4 after it, for the loop to close.

**Round 3: both lenses found real issues -- a third, subtler instance of the Gmail-string-handling bug class (this time introduced by round 2's own fix), and two doc-drift findings.** Security review found that `email.utils.getaddresses` (round 2's own fix for the comma-in-display-name corruption bug, above) defaults to `strict=True` -- CPython's own defense against a *different* spoofing shape (`'alice@x.com<bob@y.com>'`, no comma, one field parsing ambiguously into two addresses): it validates the parsed address count against `1 + (top-level comma count)` and, on a mismatch, discards the *entire* parse as a single `("", "")` sentinel rather than only the offending part. A `To` header ending in a bare trailing comma (`"alice@x.test, bob@x.test,"` -- real mail systems produce this, and a sender fully controls their own outgoing headers) is consumed without leaving a padding empty tuple behind, so the actual parsed count comes in one short of the formula's expectation and the whole header is treated as unparseable: `recipients` ends up `[]`, and `_process_message`'s own `if not recipients: return None` then silently drops the *entire* message -- sender, subject, thread, all of it -- with no error and no `error_summary`, indistinguishable from a message that was never fetched at all. An attacker (or just an ordinary misbehaving mail system) needs only a trailing comma on their own `To` header to make a message permanently vanish from the synced entity graph. Fixed with a new `_getaddresses_resilient` helper: retries `getaddresses` once with exactly the bare trailing comma (and surrounding whitespace) stripped when the sentinel is hit, recovering the real recipients without weakening the actual spoofing protection -- a header with no trailing comma to strip (e.g. the ambiguous no-comma spoofing shape itself) is a no-op, so that case is still correctly rejected. Architecture review, running concurrently, found two doc-drift issues, both test/doc-only with no production-code change of their own: this document's own Task 2 "Entity resolution" paragraph cited a function, `` `_extract_email_address` ``, that has never existed anywhere in `gmail_adapter.py`'s git history (confirmed via `git log --all -p`) -- present since the original Task 2 commit, missed by both round 1 and round 2's own doc-accuracy passes, corrected to cite only the real `_parse_address`; and `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`'s own module docstring item 11 claimed unpaired-surrogate coverage for "a `From`/`Subject` header" that didn't actually exist as a dedicated test for `Subject` (or for Gmail's own `threadId`, item 12's other named field) -- a real, if narrow, coverage gap for an already-shipped, already-correct guard (`_process_message`'s `subject`/`_is_valid_external_id(external_thread_id)` checks already called `_contains_unpaired_surrogate` correctly since round 2; only the test proving it was missing), closed with two new tests (`test_unpaired_surrogate_in_subject_is_dropped_but_message_still_syncs`, `test_unpaired_surrogate_in_thread_id_is_skipped_not_a_crash`). Both real, both fixed; round 3 does not count as clean on either lens. All four new round-3 tests independently re-verified as part of this re-verification pass, not merely trusted from the reviewing agents' own reports: the two trailing-comma tests (`test_to_header_trailing_comma_does_not_drop_the_whole_message`, `test_to_header_ambiguous_no_comma_address_is_still_safely_dropped`) mutation-confirmed by reverting `gmail_adapter.py` to its pre-round-3 (post-round-2) state via `git stash` -- the trailing-comma test fails exactly as the bug report predicts (the message is silently dropped, so no `historyId` is ever observed and the adapter falls through to an unmocked `users.getProfile` call), the ambiguous-no-comma companion test passes unchanged (that message was already correctly dropped before this round, confirming the fix doesn't weaken real spoofing protection), then restored and both pass; the underlying `getaddresses` behavior the fix and its report rely on was independently re-derived from a standalone interpreter session before trusting the report at all (`getaddresses(['alice@x.test, bob@x.test,'])` -> `[('', '')]`; the same header without the trailing comma -> both addresses correctly; the ambiguous no-comma spoofing shape -> `[('', '')]`, protection intact; a leading comma -> parses fine via the existing empty-tuple padding path, not this bug at all; the round-2 quoted-comma case -> still parses correctly, confirming no regression). The two surrogate-coverage tests were confirmed to require no corresponding production-code mutation to fail, since (per the report) the guards they cover were already shipped and already mutation-tested in round 2 -- these two tests close a coverage gap, not a live bug, and were reviewed for correctness against the already-verified `_contains_unpaired_surrogate`/`_is_valid_external_id` call sites in `_process_message` instead. Final counts: `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` is now 39 passed (35 + 2 architecture + 2 security); combined suite (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 39 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip) is **199 passed, 2 skipped**; the same nine engineering-domain suites re-run clean (367 passed, unchanged). Static checks (`.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean. Round 4 must be clean on both lenses, and round 5 after it, for the loop to close.

**Round 4: both lenses found real issues -- a resource-exhaustion gap in per-recipient entity resolution, a fourth (and structurally distinct) instance of the `getaddresses` count-collision bug class, and a recurring "Covers" list doc-drift gap.** Security review found that `email_messages.recipients` is an unbounded `ARRAY(VARCHAR(320))` (migration `0069`) and nothing capped how many addresses a single `To` header could carry -- each distinct recipient makes `_resolve_or_create_person` open its own independent `SessionFactory` transaction, so a single crafted message with an extreme recipient count could make one `backfill`/`incremental_sync` call do an effectively unbounded amount of synchronous DB work (and open that many short-lived connections) regardless of `_MAX_MESSAGES_PER_CALL`'s own bound -- a resource-exhaustion angle distinct from every prior malformed-input crash/corruption finding in this file. Fixed with `_MAX_RECIPIENTS_PER_MESSAGE = 500` (matching Gmail's own documented per-message recipient limit for a consumer account), keeping only the first N valid recipients in header order rather than rejecting the whole message. Verified via `test_to_header_recipient_count_is_capped_to_bound_entity_resolution_work`, mutation-confirmed against the constant reverted to effectively unbounded.

Architecture review, running concurrently, found that `email.utils.getaddresses`'s own aggregate `1 + comma_count` address-count check (see `_getaddresses_resilient`'s own docstring, and rounds 2-3's paragraphs above) can be fooled in the *opposite* direction from round 3's own trailing-comma finding: round 3 proved a header with real recipients could be wrongly discarded when the count comes in one *short*; round 4 found a header can also be wrongly *accepted* when the count coincidentally comes in exactly right despite the header being genuinely malformed. `'bob@example.test<carol@example.test>'` -- the exact ambiguous, no-comma shape `test_to_header_ambiguous_no_comma_address_is_still_safely_dropped` (round 3) proves is correctly rejected -- is *not* still safely dropped once a single trailing comma is appended to it (`'bob@example.test<carol@example.test>,'`): the comma raises the expected count from 1 to 2, the malformed field alone still parses into exactly those two addresses via the legacy grammar both strict and non-strict modes share, and `2 == 2` satisfies the check on the very first, un-retried `getaddresses` call (never even reaching `_getaddresses_resilient`'s own retry path). Both fabricated addresses pass every existing length/NUL/surrogate guard and would be written to `email_messages.recipients` and resolved into two real `pkos_nodes` person entities -- the identical "attacker-controlled `To` header corrupts the entity graph" impact round 2 already fixed once (via a naive-split bug), here via a different mechanism (a comma-count collision `getaddresses`' own heuristic cannot distinguish from a genuine two-recipient list, by construction), reachable at the same one-character attacker cost round 3's own bug report used for the inverse direction. Confirmed by mutation: reverting the fix and re-running `test_to_header_ambiguous_address_with_trailing_comma_is_still_safely_dropped` fails with both fabricated addresses actually present in `email_messages.recipients` and resolved into `pkos_nodes`, restoring it passes. Fixed with `_to_header_has_grammar_defect`, a cross-check using `email.headerregistry.HeaderRegistry`'s from-scratch RFC 5322 grammar parser (rather than `getaddresses`' aggregate-count heuristic) -- its `.defects` tuple is empty for every legitimate multi-address shape this module already accepts (a plain list, the quoted-comma display name, and round 3's own trailing-comma recovery case, independently confirmed against all three before relying on it) and non-empty for the comma-collision case, applied ahead of `_getaddresses_resilient` so a defect anywhere in the header drops the whole `To` header rather than only the colliding field. Wrapped in a narrow `except` for `UnicodeEncodeError` (which `HeaderRegistry` itself raises, uncaught, for a header carrying an unpaired UTF-16 surrogate -- the same failure class round 2's `_contains_unpaired_surrogate` exists to catch, reachable here one call earlier) plus `ValueError`/`TypeError`/`LookupError`/`IndexError`, deferring to `_getaddresses_resilient`'s own existing parse and `_validate_address`'s own NUL/surrogate checks on any exception rather than this cross-check taking responsibility for a failure class it isn't needed for. A smaller, secondary doc-drift fix bundled with this finding: `_getaddresses_resilient`'s own docstring claimed its retry strips "exactly one bare trailing comma," but the implementation is a `while` loop that strips every consecutive trailing comma (`"alice@x.test,,"` recovers to a single recipient, not a second, still-unparseable sentinel) -- corrected to describe the actual (more permissive, not less correct) behavior.

Architecture review separately found a third, purely doc-only gap in the same recurring pattern rounds 2-3 already found once each in different files: `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`'s own module docstring "Covers" list had stopped at item 13 (round 2's own last addition) despite round 3 shipping a substantial new fix (`_getaddresses_resilient`'s trailing-comma recovery) and two dedicated tests for it that were never given their own numbered entry -- the same "a round ships a fix and tests but the file's own header goes stale" gap round 2's architecture lens found once for `IMPLEMENTATION-STATUS.md`'s own test-count arithmetic and round 3's found once for a different test file's docstring, recurring here a third time, in this file, for the first time. Closed by adding item 15 (round 3's trailing-comma recovery, retroactively) and item 16 (this round's own comma-collision fix) to the "Covers" list. Final counts: `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` is now 41 passed (39 + 1 security + 1 architecture); combined suite (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 41 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip) is **201 passed, 2 skipped**; static checks (`.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean. Both real, both fixed; round 4 does not count as clean on either lens. Round 5 must be clean on both lenses, and round 6 after it, for the loop to close.

**Round 5 (security/correctness) review: `_to_header_has_grammar_defect` (round 4's own fix, above) had a false-positive gap that silently dropped an otherwise well-formed message.** Round 4 closed the `getaddresses`-count-collision bug class with `bool(HeaderRegistry("To", to_header).defects)` -- any defect at all, gating the entire recipient loop off if tripped. `HeaderRegistry` reports several defect classes for a `To` header, not just the `InvalidHeaderDefect` the round 4 collision actually raises: a benign interior double comma or a comma-plus-whitespace empty entry (`"bob@example.test,,carol@example.test"` -- real mail-merge tooling and forwarded headers produce this; Gmail forwards a sender's raw header verbatim) parses via `getaddresses` into the two real addresses plus a spurious `("", "")` entry `_validate_address` already drops on its own -- the exact same benign shape `_getaddresses_resilient`'s own trailing-comma recovery (round 3) exists to handle -- but `HeaderRegistry` reports this shape as `ObsoleteHeaderDefect('empty element in address-list')`/`ObsoleteHeaderDefect('address-list entry with no content')`, never `InvalidHeaderDefect` (independently confirmed in a standalone interpreter, alongside every other legitimate multi-recipient shape this module already accepts and every collision variant round 4 found, all of which do raise `InvalidHeaderDefect`). `bool(.defects)` could not tell that apart from the genuine collision, so it silently dropped the *whole message* -- sender, subject, thread, all of it, via `_process_message`'s own `if not recipients: return None` -- over a header that was never actually ambiguous: the identical "one comma away from an otherwise well-formed message vanishing with no error" impact `_getaddresses_resilient`'s own round 3 fix closed for a different comma placement, reintroduced here by round 4's own fix for a different one. Fixed by narrowing the check to `isinstance(defect, InvalidHeaderDefect)` specifically rather than `bool(.defects)` against the tuple as a whole; the round 4 collision case (and every no-comma/trailing-comma-appended ambiguous variant it was written to catch) still raises `InvalidHeaderDefect` and is unaffected. Verified via `test_to_header_interior_double_comma_recovers_recipients_not_dropped_by_grammar_check`, mutation-confirmed (fails against the pre-round-5 code -- the message is silently dropped and the adapter falls through to an unmocked `users.getProfile` call, the same failure signature round 3's own trailing-comma bug produced -- passes with the fix restored); the existing `test_to_header_ambiguous_address_with_trailing_comma_is_still_safely_dropped` (round 4's own collision-protection test) re-run unchanged and still passes, confirming the narrowing doesn't reopen that collision. Also reviewed and dismissed as not a gap: `_parse_address` (the `From`-header path) does not call `_to_header_has_grammar_defect` at all, but `parseaddr` -- unlike `getaddresses(strict=True)` -- has no count-based heuristic to fool in the first place; independently confirmed `parseaddr("bob@example.test<carol@example.test>")` (and the same shape with a trailing comma appended) both return `('', '')`, safely dropped by `_validate_address`'s existing emptiness check, not fabricating a plausible-but-wrong single address the way `getaddresses`' count arithmetic could. Final counts: `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` is now 42 passed (41 + 1); combined suite (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 42 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip) is **202 passed, 2 skipped**. Static checks (`.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean. Because a real issue was found and fixed, round 5 does not count as clean on the security lens.

**Round 5 (architecture/quality) review: `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`'s own "Covers" list never had an entry for its NUL-byte guard tests.** Round 1's security/correctness lens added five tests in one pass -- two for `_MAX_EMAIL_ADDRESS_LENGTH` (oversized address) and three for `_contains_nul` (an embedded NUL byte, in a `From` address, a `From` display name, and a `Subject` header) -- but the file's own "Covers" list (item 7, added at the same time) only ever described the oversized-address half of that pass; the three NUL-byte tests (`test_nul_byte_in_from_address_is_skipped_not_a_crash`, `test_nul_byte_in_from_display_name_is_skipped_not_a_crash`, `test_nul_byte_in_subject_is_dropped_but_message_still_syncs`) were never given their own numbered entry -- present since the original round-1 commit, missed by every doc-drift pass rounds 2-4 each ran across this exact file (round 2 closed a coverage-description gap for item 11's surrogate claim, round 3 closed one for item 15's trailing-comma fix, round 4 closed one for its own item 16 -- each looked for a list that had *stalled* after a later round's fix, not a gap dating to the list's own first entries). The same "a round ships tests but the file's own header goes stale" pattern round 4's own doc-drift paragraph named, just older and previously unnoticed rather than freshly introduced. Closed by adding item 18, describing the guard and citing all three tests by name; no test or production-code change, a documentation-accuracy fix only. Cross-checked every one of the file's 42 tests (as of the fix above) against the numbered list afterward, rather than trusting a single spot-check: `test_owner_id_is_populated_from_created_by` remains deliberately unlisted, a fixture-precondition sanity check rather than adapter-behavior coverage, consistent with the list's own stated scope ("Covers, per this task's own scope") and not a further gap. Verified via the same full combined suite and static checks the paragraph above already reports (**202 passed, 2 skipped**; `.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`/`.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files, all clean) -- this fix changes no test count on its own. Because a real (if narrow) doc-drift issue was found and fixed, round 5 does not count as clean on the architecture lens either. Round 6 must be clean on both lenses, and round 7 after it, for the loop to close.

**Round 6 (security/correctness) review: unbounded RFC 5322 comment-nesting recursion crashes `From`/`To`-header parsing with an uncaught `RecursionError`, a failure mode outside every exception type the prior five rounds' guards already anticipated.** `email.utils.parseaddr`, `email.utils.getaddresses`, and `email.headerregistry.HeaderRegistry` -- the entire stdlib parsing chain this file's `From`/`To`-header handling has been built on and hardened across rounds 2-5 -- all share the same underlying `_AddressList`/`_parseaddr` grammar, which recurses one Python interpreter stack frame per level of RFC 5322 `(...)` comment nesting, with no depth cap of its own; RFC 5322 permits comments to nest arbitrarily. Independently confirmed in a standalone interpreter: a header of 497 nested, *matched* parentheses around an ordinary address (`"(" * 497 + "a@example.test" + ")" * 497`, produced directly against the installed Python's default recursion limit) raises an uncaught `RecursionError` from all three of `parseaddr`, `getaddresses`, and `HeaderRegistry` alike -- not `ValueError`/`TypeError`/`UnicodeEncodeError`/a Postgres `DataError`, the failure classes every prior round's guard already catches. Gmail forwards a sender's raw `From`/`To` header verbatim and a sender fully controls their own outgoing headers, so this is directly attacker-reachable, and it hits the identical "single crafted message crashes the entire sync call, and for `incremental_sync` permanently wedges that connector account since the cursor never advances past it" failure class every guard in this file already exists to close -- reached here via stdlib recursion depth rather than a length/NUL/surrogate check. Three call sites needed the fix: `_parse_address` (via `parseaddr`, the `From` header, returns `None` on `RecursionError` -- unparseable, same as any other malformed `From`), `_getaddresses_resilient` (via `getaddresses`, both the initial call and the trailing-comma retry, returns the `[("", "")]` sentinel `_validate_address` already discards), and `_to_header_has_grammar_defect` (via `HeaderRegistry`, which runs *before* `_getaddresses_resilient` in `_process_message` -- so this would have crashed before the other guard's own catch ever ran; `RecursionError` added to its existing `except` tuple, returns `False`, deferring to `_getaddresses_resilient`'s own independent catch on the same input). Verified via two new tests, `test_from_header_comment_nesting_bomb_is_skipped_not_a_crash` and `test_to_header_comment_nesting_bomb_is_safely_dropped_not_a_crash` (item 19 in the test file's own "Covers" list), both mutation-confirmed (reverting just `gmail_adapter.py` via `git stash` reproduces the raw uncaught `RecursionError` propagating out of the stdlib for both; restoring passes both). One inaccuracy independently caught and corrected during this re-verification pass: the reviewing agent's own docstrings claimed the 497-deep bomb (1008 bytes total) fit "comfortably inside a single RFC 5322 unfolded header line's 998-byte hard limit" -- it does not (1008 > 998) -- corrected in both `gmail_adapter.py` and the test file to note the discrepancy is moot regardless, since this module receives Gmail's response as an already-decoded JSON string field, never raw SMTP line-folded bytes subject to that (in any case advisory, not enforced) limit. Not a live production bug in the correction itself -- the underlying `RecursionError` finding and its fix are unaffected; only the docstrings' own stated byte-count justification was inaccurate.

**Round 6 (architecture/quality) review: a coverage gap in `_to_header_has_grammar_defect`'s own narrow `except`, and a doc-drift finding in a sibling test file the prior five rounds' doc-accuracy passes never re-swept.** `_to_header_has_grammar_defect` (round 4's fix) parses the raw `To` header through `email.headerregistry.HeaderRegistry` *before* `_getaddresses_resilient`/`_validate_address` ever run, and has caught `UnicodeEncodeError` (among others) in its own narrow `except` since round 4 -- correctly, an unpaired UTF-16 surrogate escape in the `To` header raises exactly that, uncaught, independently reproduced against a standalone `HeaderRegistry` call. But no test in the file had ever actually driven a surrogate-carrying `To` header through this specific function: every existing surrogate test (`test_unpaired_surrogate_in_from_display_name_is_skipped_not_a_crash` and its `Subject`/`id`/`threadId` siblings) puts the surrogate somewhere that never reaches `_to_header_has_grammar_defect` at all. Mutation-confirmed real: narrowing the guard's `except` tuple down to `RecursionError` alone (removing `UnicodeEncodeError`/`ValueError`/`TypeError`/`LookupError`/`IndexError`, i.e. reverting to before round 4's own fix) left every other test in the file passing -- only this gap's absence went undetected. Closed with `test_to_header_unpaired_surrogate_does_not_crash_the_grammar_defect_check` (item 20 in the file's own "Covers" list), reusing the existing `_surrogate_json_response` marker-substitution helper on the `To` header's address portion instead of `From`/`Subject`/`id`. Not a live production bug -- the guard itself needed no code change, this closes a coverage gap only.

Separately, and independently, this round found that `tests/test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` (Task 1's own test file, not Task 2's) has carried a doc-drift gap since the very first Task 2 commit: that file's own "Covers" list item 22 credits `test_backfill_is_a_documented_stub`/`test_backfill_ignores_since`/`test_incremental_sync_is_a_documented_stub` with closing Task 1's round-22 stub-coverage gap -- but Task 2's own commit (`3d6ad18`, confirmed via `git show -- tests/test_gmail_connector_postgres.py`) renamed all three (to `test_backfill_no_ops_for_a_resource_type_other_than_message`, `test_backfill_ignores_since_for_a_resource_type_other_than_message`, `test_incremental_sync_no_ops_for_a_resource_type_other_than_message` respectively) once `backfill`/`incremental_sync` stopped being stubs, and item 22's own text was never updated to match -- none of the three old names exist anywhere in the file today. This document's own "Verified (original Task 2 pass)" paragraph above had a second, smaller instance of the identical miscount: it credited the rename to only "two Task 1 tests," not three (also corrected, in place, above). Every one of rounds 1-5's own doc-drift passes checked `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`'s own "Covers" list repeatedly (finding real gaps there four separate times) but never re-swept this sibling file's, the same "a fix lands and the nearest file's own header is checked, but a second file the same commit touched isn't" blind spot, just in a new location. Both fixed; no test count changes from either fix (the second is documentation-only). Final counts: `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` is now 45 passed (42 + 2 round-6-security-lens `RecursionError` tests + 1 round-6-architecture-lens surrogate-coverage test); combined suite (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 45 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip) is **205 passed, 2 skipped**. Static checks (`.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean. Because real issues were found and fixed, round 6 does not count as clean on the architecture lens. Round 7 must be clean on both lenses for the loop to close.

**Round 7 (security/correctness) review: two new, previously-unseen hazards in the same `To`-header parsing chain rounds 2-6 already hardened five times over -- an uncaught `AttributeError` from RFC 5322 `group` syntax, and unbounded (roughly quadratic) CPU cost in `HeaderRegistry` itself, independent of round 6's own recursion-depth finding.** First: RFC 5322's `group` construct (`"name: member@x.test;"`) is grammar `HeaderRegistry` otherwise parses cleanly (as a `Group` object, distinct from an ordinary `Mailbox`), but a `To` header made of two such groups -- or a group immediately followed by a bare address -- with no comma between them (invalid grammar `HeaderRegistry`'s own legacy `_AddressList`-based recovery path still attempts rather than rejects outright) makes that recovery path try to read `.local_part` off the `Group` object, which has none, raising an uncaught `AttributeError: 'Group' object has no attribute 'local_part'` from inside `HeaderRegistry.__call__` itself -- independently confirmed in a standalone interpreter, reproducible with as little as `"g:a@x.test;b@x.test"` (18 characters, no nesting, nothing exotic). None of the six exception types `_to_header_has_grammar_defect`'s `except` clause already caught (`UnicodeEncodeError`/`ValueError`/`TypeError`/`LookupError`/`IndexError`/`RecursionError`, accumulated across rounds 4-6) are raised for this input -- the identical "single crafted message reaches an uncaught crash, aborting the entire sync call, and for `incremental_sync` wedging that account permanently since the cursor never advances past it" failure class every prior round's own guard exists to close, reached here via a seventh, distinct stdlib exception type. Fixed by adding `AttributeError` to the caught tuple; `email.utils.getaddresses` (`_getaddresses_resilient`'s own underlying call) independently confirmed to handle the identical input without raising, returning its usual `("", "")` sentinel, so the message still ends up safely dropped (zero valid recipients), not silently accepted as a genuine group-syntax parse. Second, and structurally unrelated: `HeaderRegistry` is a from-scratch recursive-descent parser (`email._header_value_parser`) that reslices its remaining input on every token consumed rather than tracking an index into it -- independently profiled with `cProfile` directly against `HeaderRegistry()("To", ...)`: parse time grows roughly quadratically with the number of comma-separated fields in the header (a 2,000-address header costs ~0.1s of pure CPU in one call, 8,000 addresses ~2.4s, 16,000 ~10s), a cost `_MAX_RECIPIENTS_PER_MESSAGE`'s own guard (round 4) cannot bound since it only applies *after* this call has already returned -- no crash, no exception, just unbounded synchronous CPU per crafted message, a resource-exhaustion angle distinct from `_MAX_RECIPIENTS_PER_MESSAGE`'s own downstream-DB-cost framing and from round 6's stack-depth framing alike. The same quadratic-ish cost independently reproduces for comma-free shapes too (an extremely long single-mailbox display-name phrase, or an extremely long dot-atom domain), so a comma-count heuristic alone would not have closed every avenue; a length cap on the whole raw header does, since every shape costly enough to matter is necessarily a long string. Fixed with `_MAX_TO_HEADER_LENGTH = 50_000` (~100 characters per recipient at `_MAX_RECIPIENTS_PER_MESSAGE`'s own 500-recipient cap, generous headroom above a typical real entry) and `_to_header_exceeds_length_limit`, checked in `_process_message` ahead of both `_to_header_has_grammar_defect` and `_getaddresses_resilient` so an oversized header never reaches either parser; the worst measured shape's cost at exactly that length is ~0.64s, independently measured, rather than unbounded. Verified via two new tests, `test_to_header_group_syntax_collision_does_not_crash_the_grammar_defect_check` and `test_to_header_exceeding_length_limit_is_dropped_not_a_cpu_stall` (items 21-22 in the test file's own "Covers" list), both mutation-confirmed: reverting the `AttributeError` addition alone reproduces the raw uncaught `AttributeError` from inside `email.headerregistry`; reverting the length guard alone (via a stub `_to_header_exceeds_length_limit` returning `False`) reproduces the ~3,000-recipient message being fully processed and written rather than dropped; both pass with the corresponding fix restored. Final counts: `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` is now 47 passed (45 + 2); combined suite (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 47 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip) is **207 passed, 2 skipped**. Static checks (`.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`) clean. Because real issues were found and fixed, round 7 does not count as clean on the security lens; round 8 must be clean on both lenses for the loop to close.

**Round 8: both lenses clean -- the first clean round of Task 2.** Security review re-traced the full malformed-Gmail-input guard chain rounds 1-7 built up (`_MAX_EMAIL_ADDRESS_LENGTH`, `_contains_nul`, `_contains_unpaired_surrogate`, `_is_valid_external_id`, `_getaddresses_resilient`'s trailing-comma recovery, `_to_header_has_grammar_defect`'s collision/false-positive/`RecursionError`/`AttributeError` handling, `_MAX_RECIPIENTS_PER_MESSAGE`, `_MAX_TO_HEADER_LENGTH`), the consent-revocation/budget-exhaustion/rate-limit-degrade logic, and `_resolve_or_create_person`'s own race handling, with fresh evidence against real HEAD rather than trusting prior rounds' conclusions -- found nothing new. Architecture review independently re-swept `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`'s own "Covers" list (items 1-22) against every actual test function, `tests/test_gmail_connector_postgres.py`'s item 22 (the post-Task-2-rename test names), this document's own round-1-through-7 prose against current code, and migration `0070`'s docstring against the actual constraint -- found nothing stale. Both reviewers reported zero findings; full regression sweep (`tests/test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 47 + `tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `tests/test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip) is **207 passed, 2 skipped**, unchanged from round 7's own final count; static checks (`.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`) clean; `git status` clean throughout, no changes made by either lens. **Round 8 is clean on both lenses -- the first of the two consecutive clean rounds needed to close the loop. Round 9 must also be clean on both lenses.**

**Round 9 (security/correctness) review: a participant-resolution failure -- realistically a transient DB error, not a crafted message -- crashed the entire sync call and, on retry, silently and permanently skipped entity resolution for that message.** `_process_message`'s entity-resolution loop calls `_resolve_or_create_person` once per participant, uncaught, after `_insert_message_if_new` has already committed the `email_messages`/`email_threads` write in its own, separate transaction (`_resolve_or_create_person`'s own docstring explains why entity resolution deliberately runs in independent transactions, one per participant, rather than reusing the message write's own -- so a resolution race only ever risks re-running that one function, never rolling back the unrelated message write alongside it). That independence cuts both ways: an uncaught exception from *any* participant's resolution -- a deadlock, a dropped connection, the app's own `statement_timeout` under load, none of which requires malformed input at all, unlike every one of rounds 1-7's own findings -- propagated straight out of `_process_message`, aborting the whole `_sync_messages`/`_sync_history` loop: every message still queued behind it in the same call went unprocessed, and for `incremental_sync`, since no `return` past that point was ever reached, `next_cursor` never advanced, so the identical failure would repeat on every subsequent call. Confirmed by direct reproduction (mocking `_resolve_or_create_person` to raise for one specific participant, real Postgres, no test framework involved): the first call raised uncaught as predicted; the message row was nonetheless durably committed; and once the exception was cleared and the call retried, `_insert_message_if_new`'s own `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` returned `None` for the now-already-existing row, causing the *entire* `if inserted_id is not None:` entity-resolution block -- including the one participant that failed the first time -- to be skipped on every future call, permanently: the sync call reports `"succeeded"`, the message row exists, and nothing anywhere signals that one of its participants was never linked into `pkos_nodes`. Silent, permanent data loss in the entity graph, distinct from and reachable independently of every prior malformed-Gmail-input finding in this file, since it requires no attacker-controlled header content at all. Fixed by wrapping each participant's `_resolve_or_create_person` call in its own `try`/`except Exception: continue`, isolating a single participant's failure to just that one message-participant link (verified by direct reproduction that the person entity itself is not permanently lost -- a later message naming the same participant still resolves them normally, since `_resolve_or_create_person` runs for every message's participants, not merely new ones) rather than letting it cascade into every other message and account still queued in the same call. Verified via two new tests, `test_participant_resolution_failure_does_not_crash_the_sync_call` (a second, independent message elsewhere in the same call is unaffected) and `test_participant_resolution_failure_still_resolves_other_participants_of_the_same_message` (the *other* participants of the *same* message as the failing one are still resolved, and the failing participant is later resolved once a subsequent message names them) -- item 23 in the test file's own "Covers" list -- both mutation-confirmed: reverting just the `try`/`except` (via `git stash` on `gmail_adapter.py` alone) reproduces the raw uncaught `RuntimeError` propagating out of `backfill` for both, restoring it passes both. Final counts: `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` is now 49 passed (47 + 2); combined suite (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 49 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip) is **209 passed, 2 skipped**. Static checks (`python -m py_compile`, `.venv/bin/ruff check`, `.venv/bin/ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean. Because a real issue was found and fixed, round 9 does not count as clean on the security lens.

**Round 9 (architecture/quality) review: round 8's own clean result was never recorded in this document, breaking the per-round documentation convention Task 1's own review loop established.** Task 1's `IMPLEMENTATION-STATUS.md` section explicitly gives a clean round its own paragraph three separate times (rounds 17, 27, 28 above) rather than leaving a silent gap -- each states plainly what was re-verified and re-confirms the exit-condition counter, since that paragraph is itself part of the mechanism a reader (or a future review round) uses to know whether the streak is genuinely at one clean round or two. Round 7's own paragraph above ends "...round 8 must be clean on both lenses for the loop to close," but the very next paragraph in this document, before this round's own fix, was "Deferred, disclosed rather than silently dropped" -- round 8 having happened, and having been clean (per this task's own governing context: independently re-verified with a fresh static/dynamic check matching baseline exactly, 207 passed/2 skipped, no changes made), was nowhere written down. A reader of this file alone, with no access to that external context, would have no way to tell round 8 ever ran, let alone that it was the loop's first clean round -- indistinguishable, from this document's own text, from round 8 simply not having happened yet. Fixed by adding the round-8 paragraph above, describing what each lens re-verified and restating the exit-condition counter, matching the style already established for rounds 17/27/28. No production code, test, or migration change -- documentation-only. Full regression sweep unchanged from round 8's own count (**207 passed, 2 skipped**); static checks (`python -m py_compile`, `.venv/bin/ruff check`, `.venv/bin/ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean. Because a real (documentation) gap was found and fixed, round 9 does not count as clean: round 10 must be clean on both lenses, and round 11 after it, for the loop to close.

**Round 10: both lenses clean -- the first clean round since round 9 reset the streak.** Security review independently reproduced round 9's own `_resolve_or_create_person` IntegrityError-retry race live against real Postgres (pausing the adapter's own call mid-transaction via a monkeypatched `Session.execute`, forcing a genuine `uq_entity_aliases_workspace_type_value` collision from a competing writer) to confirm round 9's `except Exception: continue` cannot mask that recovery path's own success case, and traced every `ON CONFLICT` site in the file to confirm no other "durable partial commit, then an uncaught exception, then a retry silently skips work via `ON CONFLICT`" pattern exists elsewhere in `_process_message`/`_sync_messages`/`_sync_history` -- found nothing new. Architecture review re-checked round 9's own new content specifically (its `try`/`except` block's inline comment against the actual code and against `_resolve_or_create_person`'s own docstring, item 23 in the test file's "Covers" list against the two new test bodies, and both round-9 `IMPLEMENTATION-STATUS.md` paragraphs against current reality) and independently re-ran round 9's own mutation test (removing the `try`/`except`, confirming both round-9 tests fail with the raw uncaught `RuntimeError`, restoring it) -- found nothing stale.
