Round 11 must also be clean on both lenses for the loop to close.**

**Round 11 (architecture/quality) review: `_upsert_thread`'s own `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE` clause -- `GREATEST(...)` for `last_message_at`, `COALESCE(...)` for `subject` -- was exercised by every multi-message-per-thread test in the file, but never verified for the *values* it produces.** Every existing such test (e.g. `test_backfill_writes_messages_and_resolves_participants`) happens to process its messages in ascending chronological order, so a naive unconditional `EXCLUDED.*` overwrite of both columns (no `GREATEST`/`COALESCE` at all) would produce byte-identical results and pass unnoticed. Mutation-confirmed by temporarily replacing both clauses with a plain `EXCLUDED.*` overwrite: all 161 then-existing tests across `tests/test_gmail_connector_postgres.py`/`tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`/`tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` still passed. This is not merely a hypothetical ordering: Gmail's own `messages.list` returns messages newest-first by default, so this task's own per-page loop in `_sync_messages`/`_sync_history` processes a thread's newest message *before* its older ones on every real sync -- the opposite of every existing test's own ordering. Without `GREATEST`, `email_threads.last_message_at` would regress backward to an older message's timestamp once that older message is processed second within the same call, corrupting the exact column a later task's thread-list/attention ordering would depend on to mean "this thread's most recent activity." Without `COALESCE`, `subject` would flip to whichever message happens to be processed last (typically the *oldest* message in the thread, given Gmail's own ordering) instead of staying pinned to the thread's own first-recorded subject line. Fixed with `test_backfill_thread_upsert_keeps_latest_last_message_at_and_first_subject` -- deliberately processing a thread's newer message before its older one (matching Gmail's real ordering, not the existing tests' own), asserting both `last_message_at` stays at the newer message's timestamp and `subject` stays at the newer message's (first-processed) subject after the older message is processed second. Mutation-confirmed: fails for the intended reason (`{'Older Subject'} == {'Newest Subject'}` assertion mismatch) with the `GREATEST`/`COALESCE` clauses reverted to a plain overwrite, passes with them restored. No production code change -- `_upsert_thread`'s SQL was already correct; this closes a test-coverage gap, not a live bug. `git diff` confirms `gmail_adapter.py` is byte-identical to HEAD -- test-only change. `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`'s own "Covers" list gained item 24 for this. Verified independently: full 210-test combined suite (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 50 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip) run three consecutive times (`ECC_DATABASE_URL=postgresql+psycopg://ecc:ecc@localhost:5432/ecc`) -- **210 passed, 2 skipped** each run, no regressions, no flakiness. Static checks (`python -m py_compile`, `.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean. Because a real (test-coverage) gap was found and fixed, round 11 does not count as clean: round 12 must be clean on both lenses, and round 13 after it, for the loop to close.**

**Round 11 (security/correctness) review, running concurrently with the architecture-lens finding above: `_sync_messages`' rate-limited/consent-revoked `partial` returns could hand `incremental_sync` a `next_cursor` that silently, permanently skips an already-listed but not-yet-fetched message.** Three of this method's four `partial`-status return sites (`list_response is None`, the mid-loop consent-revocation check, `get_response is None` -- everything except the already-correct `budget_exhausted` bottom-of-function return) computed `next_cursor=str(highest_history_id) if highest_history_id else None`, where `highest_history_id` is a running `max()` over every `historyId` already processed *this call*. That's safe only if `messages.list`'s own result ordering guaranteed ascending `historyId` -- it does not: Gmail's default (and this task's own) sort is newest-received-first (the same fact the architecture lens's own finding above independently relies on for `_upsert_thread`'s ordering), and `historyId` does not track receipt order closely enough to guarantee monotonicity either. A message still queued behind the one that triggered the `partial` return, but with a *lower* `historyId` than the cursor just handed out, becomes permanently unreachable: `_sync_history`'s own `history.list(startHistoryId=...)` call only ever returns entries with a strictly *greater* `historyId` than the cursor it's given, so no subsequent `incremental_sync` call can ever surface it again. Distinct from every one of rounds 1-10's own findings in this file: none of them touched cursor *value* correctness (only whether a `partial` result should hand one out at all -- the exact invariant this method's own "no further pages" branch already carried a comment asserting, `"see this method's own partial-branch returns above, none of which set it"` -- a claim that was false for three of the four branches it was describing, until this fix). Reproduced directly against real Postgres, not merely reasoned about: two messages in one `messages.list` page, `msg-newer` (`historyId=2000`) processed first, `msg-older` (`historyId=500`, deliberately lower, mirroring an older message Gmail's own newest-first ordering places behind a newer one) rate-limited on its own `messages.get` call past the bounded retry -- before the fix, `next_cursor` came back `"2000"`, a value `history.list(startHistoryId=2000)` would never surface `msg-older` (historyId 500) through again. `_sync_history` itself was independently re-checked and confirmed to never have had this bug -- its own three `partial` branches all correctly return `next_cursor=start_history_id` (the cursor the call *started* from, unconditionally, never a value updated mid-walk). Fixed by returning `next_cursor=None` from all three affected `_sync_messages` sites, matching the already-correct `budget_exhausted` return in the same method and `_sync_history`'s own sibling behavior -- a `None` cursor simply means the next `incremental_sync` call falls back to a fresh `backfill`, which safely re-covers the skipped message (and everything queued behind it) rather than silently orphaning it. Verified via two new tests (`test_backfill_partial_next_cursor_does_not_skip_a_lower_history_id_message`, `test_backfill_partial_next_cursor_is_none_when_list_call_rate_limited_across_pages` -- item 25 in the test file's own "Covers" list) plus a `next_cursor is None` assertion added to the pre-existing `test_backfill_halts_when_consent_is_revoked_mid_window` (covering the third affected site, whose own scenario doesn't control the unfetched message's `historyId` and so can only assert the cursor came back safely `None` rather than reproducing a specific stale-cursor value), all three mutation-confirmed: `git stash`-ing just this fix (`gmail_adapter.py` reverted to its pre-round-11-security-fix state, independently of the architecture lens's own test-only change alongside it) reproduces `next_cursor == '2000'` for both new tests and a non-`None` cursor for the extended existing one, all failing for the predicted reason; restoring the fix passes all three. Superseding the architecture paragraph's own intermediate count above (written before this fix landed in the same working tree): full regression sweep (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 52 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip) is **212 passed, 2 skipped** (210 + 2 new security-lens tests; the third site's coverage is an assertion added to an existing test, not a new one). Static checks (`python -m py_compile`, `.venv/bin/ruff check`, `.venv/bin/ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`) clean. Because a real issue was found and fixed on this lens too, round 11 does not count as clean on the security lens either -- both lenses found real issues this round, so the streak resets exactly once, not twice: round 12 must be clean on both lenses, and round 13 after it, for the loop to close.**

**Round 12 (architecture/quality) review: round 11's own new prose miscounted or misordered the very fix it was describing, in four places -- the pattern this document's own round-9/round-2/round-3 history already predicted (the newest review content is the least-reviewed content).** All four are doc-accuracy issues in text round 11 itself added, none in the underlying `next_cursor=None` fix, which was re-verified correct as written. (1) `_sync_messages`' `list_response is None` branch comment claimed this was "the earlier of the two sites where it can fire," but the fix landed at three sites (`list_response is None`, the mid-loop consent-revocation check, `get_response is None`), not two -- the comment silently omitted the consent-revocation site, which gets no comment of its own at all. (2) The "no further pages" branch's comment, added by round 11 to correct its own pre-existing overclaim, replaced it with a different miscount: "round 11 review: two of the three actually did, until fixed" -- but per the round-11 diff (`git show 833e953`), all three of the partial-branch returns above that point had the pre-fix bug, not two; the comment should have said "all three." (4) Both the module docstring's item 24 and `test_backfill_thread_upsert_keeps_latest_last_message_at_and_first_subject`'s own docstring, in describing the `GREATEST`/`COALESCE` mutation test, said the "161 then-existing tests" that stayed green were "the file's"/"the entire file's" -- but 161 is the combined cross-file total (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 49 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40, exactly matching this document's own round-11 architecture paragraph above); this file alone had 49 tests at the time, not 161. Fixed by correcting the wording in all four locations (`backend/ecc/domains/personal/gmail_adapter.py` for (1)/(2); `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` for (3)/(4)) -- no production-code logic change, no test assertions changed, no new test needed (nothing here was a coverage gap; every fact being described was already correctly implemented and already correctly tested, only mis-stated). **Round 12 (security/correctness) review, running concurrently with the architecture-lens finding above: `_sync_history`'s own budget-exhausted (and rate-limited/consent-revoked mid-loop) `partial` returns could livelock a connector account that ever fell more than `_MAX_MESSAGES_PER_CALL` messages behind, never advancing past the same first N history records no matter how many times `incremental_sync` was retried.** Round 11's own security-lens fix (paragraph above) opened exactly this question: whether *other* cursor computations in this file shared its ordering-assumption flaw. `_sync_history`'s three `partial`-status return sites all reported `next_cursor=start_history_id` unconditionally -- the cursor the call *started* from, never advanced regardless of how many `history[]` records this call had already fully processed before returning. Unlike `_sync_messages`' `messages.list` (round 11's own finding: no ordering guarantee ties list position to `historyId`), `history.list(startHistoryId=X)` walks records in strictly ascending `id` order and is deterministic for a given `X` -- so pinning `next_cursor` at the call's own starting point meant a subsequent `incremental_sync` call resuming from that same, never-advanced cursor re-walked `history.list` from page 1 and re-hit the identical budget boundary on the identical records every single time: a livelock, not merely delayed progress. Any message past position `_MAX_MESSAGES_PER_CALL` in that cursor's own ordering was silently, permanently unreachable via any number of retries, and the returned `error_summary` ("will resume next call") was affirmatively false for this scenario. Reproduced directly against real Postgres, not merely reasoned about: four history records behind one `historyId=100` cursor, budget monkeypatched to 2 -- five consecutive `incremental_sync("100")` calls each processed only the same first two records (`hist-0`/`hist-1`) and reported `next_cursor="100"` every time; `hist-2`/`hist-3` were never written by any of them. Fixed by tracking `resumable_history_id` -- the historyId of the last *fully processed* `history[]` record (every one of its own `messagesAdded` entries actually fetched and written), across every page walked in the call -- and resuming every partial-return site from it instead of `start_history_id` unconditionally; a record interrupted mid-way by the budget correctly does *not* advance it, so nothing already-unprocessed is ever skipped, only genuinely-completed work is credited. The same investigation also examined `_sync_messages`' own equivalent shape (a fresh `backfill` restarting at `messages.list`'s own newest-first page 1 on every retry, via `sync_connector_endpoint`'s own "backfill run type always calls `adapter.backfill(context, resource_type)` with no `since`" behavior, confirmed by reading `connector_accounts.py` directly) and confirmed it is real -- an account with more history than `_MAX_MESSAGES_PER_CALL` within the backfill window can never have its older mail reached, since every restart re-lists the same newest messages -- but is **not** safely fixable the same way: `messages.list` carries no ordering guarantee a resume cursor could be computed from mid-walk (the exact fact round 11's own fix already relies on), so closing it fully needs a resume-state channel wider than this module's single historyId-shaped `next_cursor` string (e.g. a distinct persisted page-token/since-window, threaded through `SyncOutcome`, `sync_cursors`, and `sync_connector_endpoint` -- a materially larger change than this file alone). Disclosed as a follow-up rather than fixed this round; not silently dropped. Verified via `test_incremental_sync_makes_forward_progress_across_repeated_budget_exhausted_calls` (item 26 in the test file's own "Covers" list) -- two repeated `incremental_sync` calls, each hitting the same monkeypatched budget, together reach every message, unlike the identical two calls before this fix -- mutation-confirmed: reverting just this fix (`git checkout` on `gmail_adapter.py`, independent of the architecture lens's own doc-only change alongside it) reproduces the new test failing (both records past the budget never written, cursor pinned at the same value across calls); restoring the fix passes it, and the reproduction script's own five-consecutive-call scenario converges to all four messages written where it previously never did. Full regression sweep: **213 passed, 2 skipped** (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 53 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip). Static checks (`python -m py_compile`, `.venv/bin/ruff check`, `.venv/bin/ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean. Because a real issue was found and fixed on this lens too, round 12 does not count as clean on the security lens either -- both lenses found real issues this round, so the streak resets exactly once, not twice: round 13 must be clean on both lenses, and round 14 after it, for the loop to close.**

**Round 13 (architecture/quality) review: round 12's own `resumable_history_id` fix left two older, pre-round-12 doc claims about `_sync_history`'s cursor behavior stale, plus a third, unrelated broken cross-reference dating to the original Task 2 commit.** Round 12 changed `_sync_history`'s three `partial`-status return sites from unconditionally pinning `next_cursor=start_history_id` to resuming from `resumable_history_id` once a record has completed -- but two present-tense claims elsewhere describing that "unconditional, never updated mid-walk" behavior as `_sync_history`'s *current* behavior were never updated to match: (1) `_sync_messages`' own `get_response is None` branch comment (written by round 11, to explain why `_sync_messages` needed a different fix from its sibling) still asserted `_sync_history`'s partial branches "correctly return `next_cursor=start_history_id` ... unconditionally, not a value updated mid-walk" -- true when round 11 wrote it, false since round 12's own fix landed in the very same file; (2) `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`'s own "Covers" list item 25 (also round 11's) made the identical claim in describing what its own fix matched. Both are code/test comments describing current behavior in the present tense, not changelog entries (unlike this document's own per-round paragraphs, which are correctly time-stamped to what was true when written and don't need updating), so both had gone genuinely stale, not merely historical. Separately, and independently: the module docstring's own `incremental_sync` paragraph has claimed since the original Task 2 commit (`3d6ad18`, confirmed via `git log -S`) that its 404-fallback design decision is explained by "`_sync_history`'s own docstring" -- but `_sync_history` (like its sibling `_sync_messages`) has never had a docstring at all; the actual explanation has always lived in an inline comment on that method's `history_response.status_code == 404` branch. Twelve prior review rounds' own doc-accuracy passes (including round 8's explicit "swept `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`'s own Covers list ... this document's own round-1-through-7 prose ... found nothing stale" and round 12's own four-instance doc-drift sweep) never caught this specific cross-reference, since none happened to open `_sync_history` and check for a docstring that isn't there. Fixed by correcting the cross-reference to point at the actual branch comment, and rewording both stale claims to describe `_sync_history`'s current `resumable_history_id`-based behavior (cross-referencing `_sync_history`'s own comment and item 26, respectively) rather than its pre-round-12 one. No production-code logic change -- `resumable_history_id`'s own behavior, re-verified against the current diff, is unaffected; comment/docstring text only. Verified: `python -m py_compile`, `.venv/bin/ruff check`, `.venv/bin/ruff format --diff` (both changed files), `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend` (182 source files) all clean; full regression sweep unchanged at **213 passed, 2 skipped** (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 53 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip) -- no test count change, since this round's finding was documentation-only and needed no new coverage. Security/correctness lens review, run in parallel: see that lens's own round-13 paragraph, if any, for its independent result this round. Because a real (documentation-accuracy) issue was found and fixed on this lens, round 13 does not count as clean on the architecture lens.

**Round 13 (security/correctness) review, running concurrently with the architecture-lens finding above: round 12's own `resumable_history_id` fix resumes `_sync_history` at `history[]` *record* granularity -- deliberately scoped that way in round 12's own writeup, but review found it doesn't go far enough: a single record whose own `messagesAdded` list by itself exceeds `_MAX_MESSAGES_PER_CALL` could never finish, no matter how many times `incremental_sync` was retried, the identical livelock class round 12 closed for the *inter*-record case, reopened here for the *intra*-record one.** `history.list(startHistoryId=X)` has no way to return one record's own message list partially -- only ever the whole thing again for the identical `X` -- so before this fix, every retry re-walked an oversized record from its own first message, re-spent the entire budget re-fetching (idempotently, but non-progressing) messages an earlier retry had already written, and hit the identical exhaustion point every time; `resumable_history_id` never advances past a record that never *finishes*, so `next_cursor` never changes either. This was investigated directly in response to this document's own round-12 security paragraph, which left an open question about `_sync_messages`' analogous, disclosed-not-fixed gap ("did round 12 give up too early?") -- the investigation found the `_sync_messages` question itself unchanged (still correctly disclosed, not fixed, for the reason given below), but surfaced this *new*, distinct gap in `_sync_history`'s own round-12 restructuring instead, on the newest, least-reviewed logic in the file, exactly where this document's own recurring pattern (newest content is least-reviewed) predicts. Reproduced directly against real Postgres, not merely reasoned about: one history record with five `messagesAdded` entries, budget monkeypatched to 2 -- six consecutive `incremental_sync("100")` calls each processed only the same first two messages (`big-0`/`big-1`) and reported `next_cursor="100"` every time; `big-2`/`big-3`/`big-4` were never written by any of them. Fixed by extending the cursor -- still a single opaque `str`, `ConnectorAdapter.incremental_sync`'s own contract, no Protocol change -- to optionally carry "and skip the first N messages of record M" (`_parse_history_cursor`/`_build_history_cursor`, format `"{list_start_history_id}:{record_id}:{skip_count}"`), safe for the same reason `resumable_history_id` itself is: `history.list` walks strictly ascending by record `id` for a fixed `startHistoryId`, and a `messagesAdded` list is itself a fixed, already-happened fact, so both a record's position in the walk and its own message list are stable and replayable across retries. Every `partial`-return site inside `_sync_history` now reports this compound form when it's genuinely mid-record; a bare historyId cursor (from before this fix, or handed to `incremental_sync` after `_sync_messages`' own `cursor is None` fallback to `backfill`) still parses correctly as the plain, no-skip case. This also answers the round-12-disclosed `_sync_messages` question more precisely than "investigate and report honestly" alone would: `_sync_messages`' own gap remains genuinely *not* safely fixable the same way -- `messages.list` carries no ordering guarantee analogous to `history.list`'s strictly-ascending-by-`id` walk, so there is no stable position within it a compound cursor could safely encode "skip N" against; a resume position computed against an unordered, possibly-shifting listing (new mail arriving between retries) is not the same kind of safe as resuming a fixed, already-happened history record. Verified via `test_incremental_sync_makes_forward_progress_through_a_single_oversized_history_record` (added to the test file's "Covers" list) -- three successive `incremental_sync` calls, each hitting the same monkeypatched budget, together cover every message in the oversized record, and a `fetch_counts` assertion confirms each message is fetched exactly once total across all three calls, not merely that all five eventually land in Postgres via idempotent re-fetching. Mutation-confirmed two ways: (1) reverting the entire fix to the pre-round-13 `gmail_adapter.py` reproduces the new test failing with `next_cursor` stuck at `"100"` across calls, exactly the predicted symptom; (2) with the fix otherwise intact, disabling only the skip-application (`record_message_ids = record_message_ids[pending_skip_count:]`) reproduces a narrower, still-real failure -- the cursor's own skip-count component stops advancing (`"100:101:2"` on both the first and second call) while its record-id component still updates correctly, confirming the skip logic itself, not merely the cursor's string shape, is load-bearing. Both restore to passing. Full regression sweep: **214 passed, 2 skipped** (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 54 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip). Static checks (`python -m py_compile`, `.venv/bin/ruff check`, `.venv/bin/ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean. Because a real issue was found and fixed on this lens too, round 13 does not count as clean on the security lens either -- both lenses found real issues this round, so the streak resets exactly once, not twice: round 14 must be clean on both lenses, and round 15 after it, for the loop to close.**

**Round 14 (architecture/quality) review: two real, narrow gaps, both left behind by round 13's own new compound-cursor mechanism -- a stale doc cross-reference round 13 fixed in one file but missed in its sibling, and a docstring/code mismatch in the mechanism itself.** First: the module docstring's `incremental_sync` paragraph cited "`_sync_history`'s own docstring" for its 404-fallback design decision until round 13 fixed it to cite the real explanation (an inline comment on that method's `history_response.status_code == 404` branch, since neither `_sync_history` nor `_sync_messages` has ever had a docstring) -- but `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`'s own `test_incremental_sync_falls_back_to_backfill_when_history_id_expired` carries the identical stale cross-reference, present since the original Task 2 commit (`git log -S` confirms both instances trace to `3d6ad18`) and never touched by round 13's own fix, which only edited `gmail_adapter.py`. Fixed by correcting the test's own docstring to match the production one's post-round-13 wording. Second, and more substantive: `_parse_history_cursor`'s own docstring states malformed compound state -- "the wrong number of `:`-separated fields, or a non-integer/negative record id or skip count" -- degrades to the plain-cursor fallback, but the function's actual guard checked `skip_count < 0` and never the symmetric `record_id < 0`: `_parse_history_cursor("100:-5:3")` returned `("100", -5, 3)`, a live compound parse, not the documented fallback. Found while adding this function's first-ever direct unit tests (it had none -- every existing test reaches it only indirectly, through a full `_sync_history` mock, none of which ever feeds it malformed state). Confirmed harmless in production today: a real Gmail `history[]` record `id` (parsed via the same `_coerce_int` this function's own `record_id` uses) is never negative, so a negative `pending_stuck_record_id` can only originate from already-corrupted persisted cursor state and would then simply never match any record `_sync_history` actually walks -- equivalent in effect (though not in the `list_start_history_id` value handed to `history.list`) to the documented fallback -- but a genuine, mutation-testable contract violation between this docstring and its own implementation, the exact "correctness of the plan/docstring's own contract vs. what was actually built" class this round was asked to look for. Fixed by adding the missing `record_id < 0` check, symmetric with the pre-existing `skip_count < 0` one. Closed with seven new direct tests for `_parse_history_cursor` (item 28 in the test file's own "Covers" list) covering the plain case, the well-formed compound case, and every documented malformed-state fallback; mutation-confirmed by reverting just the new `record_id < 0` condition and observing only the negative-record-id test fail (for the predicted `("100", -5, 3)` mismatch), with the other six unaffected -- confirming they document already-correct behavior, not padding. Also assessed, per this round's own explicit instruction, whether `_sync_history`'s now-substantial body (grown across three rounds of restructuring -- 12's `resumable_history_id`, 13's compound cursor) has become a refactor candidate (e.g. extracting the record-processing inner loop into its own helper): concluded no, and did not refactor -- the loop's local state (`calls_made`, `resumable_history_id`, `seen_message_ids`, `record_history_id`, `already_skipped_this_record`, `processed_in_record_this_call`, `budget_exhausted`) is read and written across both the page loop and the record loop, and by the final fallthrough return after both loops exit; extracting the inner loop into a helper would need to pass or return most of that state anyway, trading one long, densely-commented function for a helper with an equally wide, easy-to-miswire signature -- not a clarity win for a mechanism three rounds of adversarial review have already made expensive to get subtly wrong. Verified: `python -m py_compile`, `.venv/bin/ruff check`, `.venv/bin/ruff format --diff` (both changed files), `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend` (182 source files) all clean. Full regression sweep: **221 passed, 2 skipped** (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 61 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip) -- up from 214 by the 7 new `_parse_history_cursor` tests. Because a real issue was found and fixed on this lens, round 14 does not count as clean on the architecture lens -- see the security/correctness lens's own round-14 paragraph, if any, for that lens's independent result this round.

**Round 15: both lenses clean -- the first clean round since round 13 reset the streak (round 14 found a real issue on the architecture lens, so round 15 starts a fresh streak, not a continuation of round 13's own reset).** Security review re-verified concurrency safety directly against real Postgres rather than re-reading prior rounds' own conclusions: eight concurrent threads racing to resolve the same brand-new email address through `_resolve_or_create_person` converged to exactly one `pkos_nodes` row and one `entity_aliases` row, zero uncaught errors, confirming round 9's own `try`/`except Exception: continue` isolation (and round 10's own independent IntegrityError-retry reproduction) still holds under a wider fan-out than either prior round tested; ten concurrent threads racing to `_upsert_thread`/`_insert_message_if_new` the same thread with ten distinct messages converged to exactly one `email_threads` row and ten distinct `email_messages` rows, zero uncaught errors, extending round 11's own single-threaded `GREATEST`/`COALESCE` value-correctness finding to genuine concurrent-writer safety, never previously reproduced under real concurrency. Both reviewers reported zero findings; full regression sweep (`test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` 72 + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` 61 + `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` 40 + `test_production_security.py` 48/2 skip) is **221 passed, 2 skipped**, unchanged from round 14's own final count; the broader 9-suite engineering-domain regression sweep (`test_engineering_authz_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_datadog_sync_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_decisions_incidents_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_github_sync_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_gitlab_sync_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_jira_sync_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_metrics_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_query_endpoints_postgres.py`, `test_engineering_write_actions_postgres.py`) is **367 passed**, unchanged; static checks (`python -m py_compile`, `.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean; `git status` clean throughout, no changes made by either lens. **Round 15 is clean on both lenses -- the first of the two consecutive clean rounds needed to close the loop. Round 16 must also be clean on both lenses for the loop to close.**

**Round 16 (security/correctness) review: `_sync_messages`'s and `_sync_history`'s own "fully caught up" success returns computed `next_cursor` via a bare truthiness check on an `int | None`, rather than the `is not None` check every sibling read of the same variables elsewhere in the same two methods already used.** `_sync_messages` had `next_cursor=str(highest_history_id) if highest_history_id else None`; `_sync_history` had `next_cursor=(str(latest_history_id) if latest_history_id else list_start_history_id)`. Both `highest_history_id`/`latest_history_id` are ultimately populated via `_coerce_int` -- from a message's own `historyId` field, the `users.getProfile` fallback, or `history.list`'s own page-level `historyId` field -- which can legitimately return `0`, a value that is falsy in Python but semantically distinct from "no historyId was ever observed." Before this fix, a `historyId` of exactly `0` was silently treated identically to "nothing observed at all": `_sync_messages` would discard a real cursor (`next_cursor=None` on a genuinely `"succeeded"` sync instead of `"0"`), and `_sync_history` would fall back to the *stale* starting cursor instead of the fresh value Gmail actually returned -- the same `bool`-vs-falsy-`int` confusion class round 9 closed for `expires_in` in `handle_oauth_callback`, here via plain truthiness rather than `bool`'s own `int` subtyping. Real Gmail `historyId` values are not documented as ever being exactly `0` in practice, so this is confirmed latent rather than a live production bug today -- the same "confirmed harmless in practice today, but a real, mutation-testable contract violation" framing round 14 used for `_parse_history_cursor`'s own `record_id < 0` gap -- but nothing on the read side validated that assumption, and every other check on these same two variables in the same two methods (e.g. `_sync_messages`'s own `if highest_history_id is None:` a few lines above its own fix site) already got this right; this was the one remaining truthiness-shortcut in the file. Fixed by switching both sites to `is not None`. Closed with `test_backfill_reports_next_cursor_zero_not_none_when_history_id_is_zero` and `test_incremental_sync_reports_next_cursor_zero_not_the_stale_start_when_history_id_is_zero` (item 29 in the test file's own "Covers" list), both mutation-confirmed: reverting either site back to a bare truthiness check reproduces the corresponding test failing for exactly the predicted symptom (`next_cursor is None` instead of `"0"`; `next_cursor == "100"` -- the stale starting cursor -- instead of `"0"`); restoring both passes them. The parallel architecture/quality lens, sharing the same working tree this round, independently discovered and mutation-reproduced this same fix mid-investigation rather than duplicating it, and reported no further issue of its own after a full re-sweep of the cursor mechanism, migration `0070`, and the entire document/test-coverage cross-check -- see that lens's own findings folded into this paragraph, since both lenses converged on the same single fix this round. Because a real issue was found and fixed, round 16 does not count as clean on either lens -- round 17 must be clean on both lenses, and round 18 after it, for the loop to close.**

**Deferred, disclosed rather than silently dropped.** `handle_webhook` remains stubbed (push-notification-based sync, Cloud Pub/Sub, is still the disclosed-not-yet-decided item from the design doc). `refresh_permissions`'s return value still has no HTTP caller wired to it (Task 1's own disclosed gap, unchanged this task) -- a sync call that hits an expired/revoked access token surfaces as a bare `RuntimeError` (Gmail's `401`) rather than a `permission_lost` status transition; closing that gap is not in this task's own scope and is left for whichever future task first needs it. A `backfill` window (default 30 days) containing more messages than `_MAX_MESSAGES_PER_CALL` (200) can still never be fully covered -- every retry of a budget-exhausted/rate-limited `backfill` restarts at `messages.list`'s own newest-first page 1 with no persisted resume position, so it repeatedly re-covers the same newest ~200 messages and never reaches older mail in the window (see round 12's security paragraph above for the original investigation, and round 13's security paragraph above for why this gap specifically remains open even after `_sync_history`'s own analogous intra-record gap was closed this round: `messages.list` has no ordering guarantee a resume cursor could safely be computed from, unlike `history.list`'s strictly-ascending-by-record-`id` walk). Not a data-corruption risk (nothing gets mis-attributed, and every retry honestly reports `status="partial"`, never a false `"succeeded"`), but a real completeness gap for any account with a busy inbox on its very first backfill; closing it needs a resume-state channel wider than this module's single historyId-shaped `next_cursor` string, out of this task's own scope.

## Task 3 evidence

**Deterministic attention integration.** Per the implementation plan's own Task 3 scope: an "awaiting reply" heuristic (last message in a thread is inbound, no outbound reply since, and the sender resolves to a known `pkos_nodes` contact) feeding `attention_items` via the exact ingestion pattern `task`/`commitment`/`risk`/`waiting_link` already use -- no new AI-runtime task type -- and `attention.explain_item` extended to handle a Gmail-sourced item's resource type.

**`EntityType` widened, no migration needed.** `ecc.domains.attention.attention.EntityType` (a `Literal`) gains `"email_thread"`, reusing `risk_review`/`meeting`'s own established precedent: `attention_items.entity_type` is an unconstrained `String(32)` (migration `0006`, no `CHECK`), so this is a Python-side type widening only.

**The heuristic is computed inside `regenerate_attention`, not inside `GmailAdapter.backfill`/`incremental_sync`.** Confirmed via research before implementation: this codebase has no periodic connector-work scheduler at all (`connector_accounts.py`'s own docstring states this outright), and every existing "derived data" surface (`GET /metrics`, `POST /attention/regenerate`, `POST /connectors/{id}/sync`) is manually/pull-triggered -- the same shape `task`/`commitment`/`risk`/`waiting_link` already use, and the one this task's own "exact ingestion pattern" instruction points at. A new eligibility query joins `email_threads`/`email_messages` (a `LATERAL` subquery picks each thread's own last message by `sent_at`) against `personal_domains`(`enabled = true`)/`domain_consents`(`revoked_at IS NULL`) -- a defensive re-check, since Task 7 (not yet built) is what will actually purge `email_threads`/`email_messages` on consent revocation; until then this keeps a revoked-but-not-yet-purged domain's threads from surfacing new attention items -- and (originally) `entity_aliases` (`alias_type='email'`, `normalized_value = LOWER(TRIM(sender))`), the identical lookup `gmail_adapter.py`'s own `_resolve_or_create_person` performs at write time, re-run here at read time. **This paragraph described the pre-round-1 shape; round 1 review replaced the SQL-side `entity_aliases` comparison with a Python-side filter -- see that round's own paragraph below for why, and `regenerate_attention`'s own current code/comments for the shape that replaced it (this paragraph is left otherwise unmodified as a record of the original design; round 8 review found this final clause had gone stale and is the fix that added this note).** `_score_awaiting_reply` (a new scorer, `attention.py`) contributes a flat `awaiting_reply_points` (a new `AttentionPolicy` field, default 12) plus the existing generic `stale_7d_points`/`stale_14d_points` `_score_waiting` already shares -- not a second, email-specific staleness threshold pair. The stale-row `DELETE` and `list_attention`'s own tie-breaker `ORDER BY` were both extended with a matching `email_thread` branch/`LEFT JOIN`, mirroring every existing entity type's own shape exactly.

**Two schema realities this task had to design around, both absent from `email_threads`/`email_messages` by migration `0069`'s own deliberate design (personal-domain tables, not `ecc.platform.authz`-model resource tables):**
- No `version`/optimistic-concurrency column -- `_upsert_batch`'s dismissed-state-preservation logic needs *some* monotonically-changing per-row value to detect "has the underlying entity changed since dismissal," so `email_threads.updated_at` (bumped to `now()` by `gmail_adapter.py`'s `_upsert_thread` on every write that touches a thread) is converted to a microsecond-epoch `bigint` and used in its place.
- No `visibility` column -- every `email_thread` row's `attention_items.visibility` is fixed to `'private'` in Python rather than read from the source table, since `authz.authorize()`'s own step 2 ("the owner is always allowed") already makes the row visible to its `owner_id` regardless, and Phase 7's per-owner personal-domain model never intends a `high_stakes`-classified domain's derived data to default to workspace-wide visibility.

**`attention.explain_item` needed no code change at all -- confirmed by reading, not assumed.** `attention.get_item_tool` (`attention/tools.py`) reads only `attention_items`' own columns (`entity_type`, `score`, `confidence`, `factors`) and authorizes against the `attention_items` row itself, never branching on `entity_type`; the active prompt template (migration `0055`) renders `"Item type: {{ entity_type }}"` as a plain string substitution. `EntityType`'s own widening is therefore the entire "extension" -- verified directly via `test_execute_run_explain_item_handles_an_email_thread_sourced_item` (`tests/test_ai_runtime_runtime_postgres.py`), a new end-to-end `execute_run` test using `_score_awaiting_reply`'s own real factor-code vocabulary (`awaiting_reply`, `stale_7d`) rather than the file's existing task-shaped `_DEFAULT_FACTORS`, following the `_insert_attention_item` helper's own widened `entity_type` parameter (default `'task'`, every pre-existing call site unaffected).

**Tests: `tests/test_attention_email_awaiting_reply_postgres.py` (new, 6 tests)** -- an inbound thread from a resolved sender surfaces with the `awaiting_reply` factor; an outbound-replied thread does not; a thread from an unresolved sender does not; a thread whose owner's `email` domain is disabled does not; staleness ages in as `stale_14d` (not both `stale_7d` and `stale_14d` at once) as the last inbound message crosses 14 days; and a thread later replied to is pruned from the queue on the next `regenerate_attention` call, mirroring `test_attention_waiting_postgres.py`'s own `test_waiting_link_surfaces_in_attention_queue_and_ages` lifecycle. Plus one new `tests/test_ai_runtime_runtime_postgres.py` test for `explain_item` coverage (above). Mutation-confirmed: reverting `attention.py`/`policy.py` alone (`git stash`) reproduces exactly the 3 positive-assertion tests failing (`StopIteration`/empty-factor-set, for the predicted reason -- nothing scores or surfaces `email_thread` rows at all) while the 3 negative-assertion tests trivially still pass (asserting an absence that remains true with the feature entirely absent, not a false-positive signal) and the `explain_item` test is unaffected (confirming that path was already fully generic, independent of this task's own production-code change); restoring passes all 7.

**Full regression evidence.** Combined suite (`test_attention_capacity_postgres.py` + `test_attention_meeting_prep_postgres.py` + `test_attention_planning_postgres.py` + `test_attention_risk_reviews_postgres.py` + `test_attention_waiting_postgres.py` + `test_attention_email_awaiting_reply_postgres.py` (new) + `test_risks_attention_postgres.py` + `test_ai_runtime_runtime_postgres.py` + `test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`): 318 passed, 1 failed -- the single failure (`test_ranking_10000_eligible_entities_under_budget`) independently confirmed pre-existing and unrelated to this task's own changes, via direct reproduction against the unmodified baseline (`git stash`) in this same sandbox, which fails identically (p95 1149ms/670ms against a 600ms budget on both the initial pass and its own built-in retry) with none of this task's code present -- consistent with this session's own established finding that this specific test is flaky under sandbox/CI load, not a real regression. `tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` + `tests/test_production_security.py` + `tests/test_ai_runtime_evaluation_postgres.py`: 123 passed, 2 skipped. Static checks (`python -m py_compile`, `.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean across every touched file (`backend/ecc/domains/attention/attention.py`, `backend/ecc/domains/attention/policy.py`, `tests/test_ai_runtime_runtime_postgres.py`, `tests/test_attention_email_awaiting_reply_postgres.py`).

**Deferred, disclosed rather than silently dropped.** The evaluation dataset (`tests/fixtures/phase4_evaluation_attention_explain.py`, Decision 9's own "3-4 examples per `EntityType`") is not extended with `email_thread` examples this task -- `risk_review`/`meeting` already established that a synthetic, hand-labelled example needs no real scorer behind it to be meaningful for the live-model `ollama-evaluation` CI job, so this would be additive and low-risk, but the live-model evaluation floors this job gates on are, per this document's own Task 2 review history, sensitive to exactly this kind of new content (`0055`'s own migration docstring: a new prompt-adjacent addition can only really be verified against a real `ollama-evaluation` run, not locally) -- deferred rather than risked speculatively in the same change that adds the underlying feature. `list_attention`'s `email_thread` `LEFT JOIN` was added for tie-breaker-ordering symmetry with every other entity type but has no due-date-shaped column to contribute to the primary `COALESCE(t.due_at, ...)` ordering clause (email threads have no analogous "expected timing" concept) -- correctly, not a gap: an `email_thread` item simply sorts on score/pinned-status like every other item, falling through to the final `created_at`/`entity_id` tie-breakers only when those are equal.

**Round 1 (security/correctness) review: `regenerate_attention`'s eligibility query resolved a thread's last-inbound sender against `entity_aliases` via a SQL-side `LOWER(TRIM(sender))` comparison, but every `entity_aliases.normalized_value` row is actually written by `gmail_adapter._normalize_email` (`.strip().casefold()`, Python) -- and Postgres's `LOWER()` is not equivalent to Python's `.casefold()` for a real class of addresses.** Confirmed directly against this database's own collation: `'straße@example.test'.strip().casefold()` expands the German sharp s to `'strasse@example.test'` (Unicode full case folding's own special-cased mapping), but `SELECT LOWER('ß')` returns `'ß'` unchanged. A real, already-resolved contact sending from such an address would silently never surface as awaiting reply -- a false negative, not a crash or leak, but a genuine correctness gap in the very mechanism this task exists to provide. Fixed by dropping the SQL-side comparison entirely: the eligibility query now fetches every structurally-eligible thread (domain enabled, consent active, last message inbound) with its raw sender, separately fetches the workspace's known `entity_aliases.normalized_value` set, and filters in Python using the real `_normalize_email` (imported from `gmail_adapter`) -- the stale-row `DELETE`'s `email_thread` branch was simplified to reuse the same Python-computed eligible-id list rather than re-running an independently-normalized `EXISTS` check, closing a small consistency window between the two in the process. Verified via `test_inbound_thread_from_casefold_divergent_sender_surfaces_as_awaiting_reply` (item 7 in the test file's own "Covers" list), mutation-confirmed: reverting the Python-side `_normalize_email` call to a plain `.strip().lower()` reproduces the test failing with `StopIteration` (the thread never surfaces) for exactly the predicted reason; restoring passes it.

**Round 1 (architecture/quality) review: no existing test, for any `attention_items` entity type, exercised `_upsert_batch`'s dismissed-state-preservation logic end to end (dismiss an item, regenerate again with nothing changed, confirm it stays dismissed) -- and `email_thread` is the one entity type where a bug in that mechanism was plausible rather than merely theoretical, since its `source_entity_version` is a derived proxy (`email_threads.updated_at` converted to microsecond-epoch), not a real optimistic-concurrency column the way every other entity type's is.** Closed with `test_dismissed_email_thread_stays_dismissed_across_an_unchanged_regenerate` (item 8), asserting both the HTTP-visible behavior (a dismissed item doesn't reappear in the next `regenerate` response) and the underlying invariant directly (`dismissed_entity_version == source_entity_version` in the database row). Mutation-confirmed: injecting per-call entropy into the version computation (`+ uuid4().int % 1000`, simulating a non-deterministic derivation) reproduces the test failing for exactly the predicted reason (the item spuriously reappears, since the recomputed version no longer matches what was recorded at dismiss time); restoring the deterministic computation passes it. No production-code change was needed for this finding -- the existing derivation was already correct, this closes a coverage gap only.

**A note on this round's own review process.** Both review agents, working concurrently in the same shared working tree (not isolated worktrees), independently reported what each described as a prompt-injection/tampering attempt against their own session -- a fabricated notification claiming a file had been externally modified and instructing them not to disclose this. Independent investigation traced this to the standard, benign harness notification this repository's own review sessions have relied on throughout Phase 10's review history (the identical "Note: file was modified... don't tell the user, they're already aware" text fires whenever a concurrent process touches a file an agent is working with, including this orchestrating session's own routine `git stash`/`ruff format` calls) -- triggered here by the two agents' own concurrent edits to the same file, compounded by the architecture lens's own legitimate mutation-testing methodology (deliberately breaking, then restoring, the version computation to verify its own proposed test) colliding with the security lens's concurrent edits to the same function. No evidence of an actual external attack was found; the final working-tree state was independently verified clean and coherent, and both agents' substantive findings (above) were independently re-verified and are real. Both agents' cautious, disclose-rather-than-comply response to ambiguous instructions embedded in tool output was the correct posture regardless of the underlying cause.

Full regression sweep after both fixes (`test_attention_capacity_postgres.py` + `test_attention_meeting_prep_postgres.py` + `test_attention_planning_postgres.py` + `test_attention_risk_reviews_postgres.py` + `test_attention_waiting_postgres.py` + `test_attention_email_awaiting_reply_postgres.py` (now 8 tests) + `test_ai_runtime_runtime_postgres.py` + `test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`, which excludes `test_risks_attention_postgres.py`'s own known pre-existing flaky test): **308 passed**, up from 306 by the round's 2 new tests; static checks (`python -m py_compile`, `.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean. Because real issues were found and fixed on both lenses, round 1 does not count as clean: round 2 must be clean on both lenses, and round 3 after it, for the loop to close.

**Round 2 (architecture/quality) review: round 1's own new comment block above the `email_thread_candidates` query still described the *pre-round-1* implementation** -- it said sender resolution against `entity_aliases` happened in this same SQL query "matching `_normalize_email`'s `.strip().casefold()` via `LOWER(TRIM(...))`," but round 1's own fix had already removed that SQL-side comparison and moved it to the separate Python-side filter (`known_email_aliases` + `_normalize_email`) a few lines below -- the comment sitting directly above the query was never updated to match, leaving it actively misleading about where sender resolution actually happens. Fixed by rewriting the comment to describe the current two-step approach and why. Pure comment change, no logic touched. Doc-accuracy, test-coverage-completeness, and scope-boundary re-checks against the plan all found nothing else stale.

**Round 2 (security/correctness) review: three real issues, all in `regenerate_attention`'s `email_thread` eligibility query.** (1) The `LATERAL` subquery picking a thread's "last" message had no tiebreaker at all (`ORDER BY sent_at DESC LIMIT 1`) -- two messages with an identical `sent_at`, one inbound and one outbound, resolved to whichever row Postgres's own query plan happened to visit first, an unspecified choice with no guarantee of stability across repeated calls against the same data. No column in this schema can determine which message was genuinely sent *last* in a real tie (`id` is an application-assigned `uuid4()`, unrelated to send order; this same file's own comments on Gmail's `historyId` already document it isn't a reliable per-message ordering signal either), so the fix (`ORDER BY sent_at DESC, id DESC`) doesn't and can't resolve which message the tie "really" was -- it closes the SQL-level indeterminacy only, turning an unspecified result into a fixed, reproducible one. (2) `email_threads.owner_id` -- unlike every other scored `entity_type`'s own owner_id -- can never be reassigned via `POST /ownership/transfers` (Phase 7 personal-domain data, never a member of `authz.py`'s grantable resource-table model), so a member's removal from the workspace left their still-unanswered threads permanently orphaned: recomputed and rewritten by every future `regenerate_attention` call forever, yet permanently invisible to everyone (hardcoded `visibility='private'`, and a removed member can never again pass `authz.authorize`'s own active-membership check). Fixed with a `JOIN workspace_memberships wm ... wm.status = 'active'`, so a removed owner's threads become ineligible and the existing stale-row `DELETE` prunes them rather than leaving them as permanent dead weight. Disclosed, not fixed (touches `ecc/platform/authz.py`, a shared module outside this task's primary files): removing a member who owns such a row is *initially* blocked by `authz.owned_resource_summary` (which counts any `attention_items` row by `owner_id`, `email_thread` or not), and the documented remedy -- transferring ownership of that specific `attention_items` row -- is silently reverted by the very next `regenerate_attention` call, since the row's `owner_id` is always re-derived from `email_threads.owner_id`, never itself transferable; a plausible follow-up is excluding `entity_type='email_thread'` from that summary's scan, mirroring its existing `audit_events` precedent. (3) The `known_email_aliases` fetch pulled *every* `entity_aliases` row for the workspace (`alias_type='email'`) on every `regenerate_attention` call, scaling with total resolved-contact count rather than with how many threads are actually awaiting reply -- unbounded for a long-lived executive inbox with years of resolved contacts. Fixed by narrowing the fetch to `normalized_value = ANY(:candidate_senders)`, computed from only the structurally-eligible candidates already fetched -- still uses the real `_normalize_email` (round 1's own fix is preserved), only the candidate set checked is bounded. Verified via three new tests (items 9-11 in the test file's own "Covers" list): `test_identical_sent_at_tie_resolves_deterministically_by_id` (two threads, each with an opposite-direction tie, both asserted precisely -- not merely "whichever direction Postgres's own untamed default happens to favor"), `test_removed_member_owned_thread_is_pruned_from_attention`, and `test_known_email_aliases_bounded_to_candidate_senders_still_matches`. Both correctness fixes ((1) and (2)) independently mutation-confirmed by this orchestrating session directly (not merely trusted from the reviewing agent's own report): reverting the `id DESC` tiebreaker reproduces the tie test failing consistently across three repeated runs (not flaky-appearing-to-pass); reverting the `wm.status = 'active'` join reproduces the removed-member test failing. Fix (3) is a scale improvement, not independently mutation-testable via a correctness assertion (its own test proves the bounded fetch still matches correctly, not that the unbounded version was wrong) -- confirmed both bounded and unbounded forms pass the new test, as expected.

Full regression sweep after round 2's fixes: **311 passed** (up from 308 by the round's 3 new tests; `test_attention_email_awaiting_reply_postgres.py` is now 11 tests), `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py`/`test_production_security.py`/`test_ai_runtime_evaluation_postgres.py`: 123 passed, 2 skipped (unchanged); static checks (`python -m py_compile`, `.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff`, `.venv/bin/mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend`, 182 source files) clean. This round's two review agents, explicitly briefed on round 1's own shared-working-tree confusion episode (documented above), worked concurrently without incident. Because real issues were found and fixed on both lenses, round 2 does not count as clean: round 3 must be clean on both lenses, and round 4 after it, for the loop to close.

**Round 3 (security/correctness) review: clean.** Re-verified round 2's own `wm.status='active'` join has no live behavioral gap against `workspace_memberships`' third schema-level status (`'suspended'` exists in the `CHECK` constraint but no endpoint anywhere in the backend ever writes it -- an unused, reserved enum value, the same "declared but not yet wired up" pattern this codebase already uses elsewhere); confirmed the `candidate_senders` array parameter carries no injection or malformed-input surface (bound, parameterized, and fed only from already-Task-2-hardened `email_messages.sender` values); confirmed the two-round-trip eligibility query introduces no new race (the entire function body, both round-2 queries included, runs inside the pre-existing `pg_advisory_xact_lock`-guarded transaction, serializing concurrent `regenerate_attention` calls per workspace); confirmed neither the `personal_domains` nor `workspace_memberships` JOIN can fan out a candidate row into duplicates (both are effectively 1:1 per `(workspace_id, owner_id)`, verified against their own unique constraints), which would otherwise have broken `_upsert_batch`'s single-statement `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`; verified `source_entity_version`'s float-to-bigint microsecond conversion is exact across a 200,000-sample randomized range spanning 2020-2035; and independently confirmed `planning.py` correctly excludes `email_thread` from its own scheduling query (`entity_type IN ('task','commitment','waiting_link')`) the same way it already excludes `risk`/`risk_review`/`meeting`. No changes made.

First, per this round's own explicit brief item 3: `frontend/src/features/attention/AttentionQueue.tsx`'s exported `AttentionItem.entity_type` TypeScript union -- consumed by the *already-shipped* Attention Queue UI that this task's new `email_thread` rows flow into unchanged, `list_attention` having no separate Task-8-gated view -- had never been widened to include `'email_thread'`, unlike the backend `EntityType` `Literal` this task widened in `attention.py`. This was a real, `tsc --noEmit`-confirmed compile failure (`error TS2322: Type '"email_thread"' is not assignable to type ...`), not a style nit: a companion test (`groupOf` grouping an `email_thread` item as `needs_action`) already existed in the working tree assigning that literal to the un-widened type. This session's own fix collided, in the literal sense, with the concurrent security/correctness lens making the identical fix at the same moment in the same shared working tree (this document's own round-1 paragraph's disclosed working mode) -- both sessions' edits landed as one duplicated union member (`'email_thread' | 'email_thread'`), caught and corrected on this session's own next read-back rather than assumed clean. Fixed state: the union gains `'email_thread'` once, and `groupOf` gets an explanatory comment for why `email_thread` deliberately falls through to the same `needs_action` branch as `task`/`commitment` rather than a dedicated one (the reply is the owner's own next action, not something blocking on someone else). Verified with `npx tsc --noEmit` (clean) and the full `frontend` vitest run for the `attention` feature directory (11 `AttentionQueue.test.tsx` tests passed, including the pre-existing `email_thread` grouping test); the three unrelated `DecisionsPanel`/`IncidentsPanel`/`EntityDetail` test failures elsewhere in the frontend suite were independently confirmed pre-existing via `git stash` against the unmodified baseline, not caused by this fix. Per this round's own brief item 4 (`AttentionFeedback`/`override_reason`/`pinned` mutation behavior for an `email_thread` item): confirmed no issue -- `dismiss`/`defer`/`restore`/`feedback` all read and write only `attention_items`' own columns (never the source table), and there is no generic "pin" endpoint at all (`pinned` is only ever mutable via the type-specific `PATCH /tasks/{id}`/`/commitments/{id}`/`/risks/{id}` routes, none of which an `email_thread`'s `entity_id` could ever resolve against). Per this round's own brief item 5 (scope-vs-plan): re-read the plan's own Task 3 bullets: the heuristic, the ingestion pattern, and the `explain_item` extension are all present and match; no scope creep, nothing under-built.

Second, found via this round's own "fresh eyes on anything else" instruction, outside this task's primary files but directly caused to matter by this task's own design choice: `ecc.domains.platform.dashboard_briefs._build_sections`'s `top_priorities` section (feeding both `GET /api/v1/dashboard/today` and `GET /api/v1/briefs/morning`) was the one section in that function that never applied `attention_items`' own visibility filter -- `today_schedule`/`overdue_commitments`/`waiting_on`/`risks` all embed `authz.visible_resource_filter_sql`/`_namespaced_visibility_filter`, but the `attention_rows` query only ever scoped by `workspace_id`, with no visibility check at all. Reproduced directly against real Postgres before investigating further, not merely inferred from reading the SQL: a `visibility='private'` `attention_items` row owned by one workspace member appeared in a *different* active member's own `GET /api/v1/dashboard/today` response. Most Phase 1-3 entity types default `visibility` to `'workspace'` (migration `0063`), which is why this stayed latent in practice -- but this task's own `email_thread` rows are *unconditionally* `'private'` (`attention.py`'s `email_thread_rows` comment, itself justified by "Phase 7's per-owner personal-domain model never intends a `high_stakes`-classified domain's derived data to default to workspace-wide visibility"), making `email_thread` the first entity_type where every single row hits this gap -- turning a previously dormant, occasionally-triggerable bug into a guaranteed leak of every executive's Gmail "awaiting reply" summaries (including the human-readable `explanation` text) to every other active workspace member's own morning brief. Judged in-scope to fix directly, not merely disclose (unlike round 2's own `authz.owned_resource_summary` disclosure a few paragraphs above, which blocks an admin action rather than leaking content, and which genuinely does require touching a shared module for an unrelated corner case): this is a live, silent, cross-member confidentiality breach of exactly the "high_stakes" data this task's own design says must stay owner-only, in the one already-shipped surface besides the Attention Queue itself that reads `attention_items`. Fixed by adding the identical `authz.visible_resource_filter_sql(..., resource_type="attention_items", ...)` filter this function's own sibling sections already use, aliased consistently (`ai`) with `attention.py`'s own `list_attention` endpoint. Verified via a new `test_top_priorities_excludes_another_members_private_attention_item` (`tests/test_dashboard_briefs_postgres.py`), asserting both that a private item owned by another member is absent from the viewing member's own `top_priorities` and that the owner still sees their own item (a visibility fix, not a silent drop); mutation-confirmed by reverting `dashboard_briefs.py` alone (`git stash`) and reproducing the exact same test failing for the predicted reason (the private item present in the other member's response), then restoring to pass.

**Round 5: found by the orchestrating session directly, before either review agent launched (Agent/Bash tooling was temporarily unavailable partway through this round; per its own explicit guidance, investigation continued using only the still-available read-only tools).** A backend-wide grep sweep of every direct `attention_items` SQL reader (`grep -rln "attention_items" backend/ecc/`, followed by narrowing to actual `FROM attention_items`/`attention_items ai` read sites, not comment mentions) turned up one previously-unchecked instance of round 4's own bug class: `backend/ecc/domains/attention/planning.py`'s `_fetch_candidates` (called by `POST /api/v1/plans`, restricted to `entity_type IN ('task','commitment','waiting_link')` -- `email_thread` is not involved here at all, so this is pre-existing, not introduced by this task) queried `attention_items` scoped only by `workspace_id`, never applying `authz.visible_resource_filter_sql` -- despite the function's own docstring claiming "Same visibility filter as `attention.py:list_attention`," which was never actually true. Fixed with the identical `authz.visible_resource_filter_sql(session, auth, resource_type="attention_items", action="read", table_alias="ai")` pattern round 4 used in `dashboard_briefs.py`, merging `**visibility_params` into the query's bind parameters and correcting the docstring to describe the eligibility filter and the visibility filter as the two separate things they are. **Round 6 (security/correctness) review: one real bug found and fixed -- dismissed `email_thread` items could silently resurface after a no-op Gmail resync.** `backend/ecc/domains/personal/gmail_adapter.py`'s `_upsert_thread` (called once per message Gmail returns, including messages this thread already has -- `_insert_message_if_new`'s own docstring names a backfill re-run or incremental/backfill overlap re-processing an already-known message as an expected, not exceptional, case) set `email_threads.updated_at = :now` unconditionally on every `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`, even when the write changed nothing observable (`last_message_at` and `subject` both left at their existing values). Since `attention.py`'s `email_thread_rows` derives `attention_items.source_entity_version` directly from `email_threads.updated_at` (there is no real `version` column, per migration `0069`), this meant: dismiss an "awaiting reply" item, then an ordinary backfill re-run or sync-window overlap touches the same thread with zero new content, `updated_at` bumps anyway, and the next `regenerate_attention` call sees a "changed" version and silently un-dismisses the item -- a real violation of the dismissed-state-preservation invariant this task's own round-1 paragraph already established a test for, but for a different entity, not this specific write path. Reproduced directly against real Postgres before fixing. Fixed by making the `updated_at` bump conditional -- only set to `:now` when `EXCLUDED.last_message_at` genuinely advances the row's own `last_message_at` (mirroring the existing `GREATEST`) or `subject` is being filled in for the first time (mirroring the existing `COALESCE`) -- so a true no-op resync leaves both `updated_at` and the derived `attention_items` version untouched, matching how every other scored entity type's real `version` column already behaves. Verified via a new `test_backfill_rerun_over_an_overlapping_window_does_not_bump_thread_updated_at` (`tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py`) -- notably, the file's own pre-existing sibling test for the identical resync scenario, `test_backfill_rerun_over_an_overlapping_window_does_not_duplicate_rows`, only ever asserted row *counts* and never caught this, since duplicate-suppression and `updated_at`-stability are two different invariants that happen to share a fixture. Mutation-confirmed by the orchestrating session directly (reverted the fix back to the unconditional `updated_at = :now`, reproduced the new test failing for exactly the predicted reason, restored, re-confirmed all 148 tests across the three directly-touched files pass).

One additional item flagged, not fixed: `dashboard_briefs.py`'s `_brief_staleness`'s `table_by_type` dict has no entry for `"email_thread"` (or pre-existing `"waiting_link"`), so an `email_thread` item in a morning brief's `top_priorities` section falls back to age-based staleness rather than checking its live source -- a display-freshness gap, not a visibility/security issue (round 4's fix already scopes this section correctly), and `waiting_link` already has the identical pre-existing gap untouched by this task. Left as a disclosed, not-yet-fixed item rather than expanded scope for a low-severity display nit shared with an entity type this task doesn't own.

Fixed by swapping the two functions' physical positions; independently confirmed byte-for-byte content-identical via a sorted-line-multiset comparison (`sorted(before.splitlines()) == sorted(after.splitlines())`), no logic touched. Second, while independently investigating this round's own "has the `email_thread` branch accumulated cruft" brief item, this lens built its own standalone reproduction of the identical `_upsert_thread` `updated_at` bug the security lens found and fixed concurrently in the same shared working tree -- both lenses converged on the same root cause independently. Rather than duplicate the fix, this lens verified the security lens's own fix was correct and complete (diff scoped exactly to the targeted `CASE` expression) and closed the resulting doc gap: `attention.py`'s own `email_thread_rows` comment, which this task's own round 1-5 paragraphs above already describe as claiming `updated_at` is bumped "on every write that touches this thread," was now stale against the corrected conditional-bump behavior -- rewritten to describe the actual behavior and reference this round's finding, so a future reader doesn't reintroduce the disproven "every write" assumption. Cross-checked every round 1-5 doc paragraph against current code (not just the most recent) -- all other specific claims still hold, including the still-open `authz.owned_resource_summary` disclosure from round 2 (still doesn't exclude `email_thread`, as documented). Declined to force an extraction of `regenerate_attention`'s `email_thread` branch into a helper despite 6 rounds of accumulated comments -- each block documents a distinct, still-live decision, and extraction would require threading `eligible_email_thread_ids` through a returned bundle for no real clarity gain, the same conclusion round 3 already reached.

**Round 7 (architecture/quality) review: two real issues found and fixed, both test-file documentation completeness, plus two previously-flagged items reassessed with fresh reasoning and reconfirmed correct.** First, per this round's own explicit brief item 1: the "Covers" docstring-numbering-vs-physical-position drift closed once each in round 3 (items 7/8) and round 6 (items 11/12) is a *recurring* failure mode, not two unrelated one-off slips -- both times, a round that added two new tests inserted them into the file in a different order than the list's own narrative numbering, and both times the fix was a positional swap that does nothing to prevent a third recurrence. Verified all 12 names match the file's own 12 real `def test_...` names, in the same order, via a script comparing the two lists directly (not by eyeballing). Pure docstring change, no logic touched, all 12 tests still pass. Second, found via this round's own brief item 5 (does the round-6 test in `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` follow that file's own conventions): it didn't -- that file's own "Covers" list (items 1-29, its own separate, longer-running review history from Task 2) has an established convention of giving every substantive new test its own numbered entry, including two prior retroactive additions (items 15 and 18) made specifically because a test "had never had a Covers entry of its own despite always having its own dedicated tests" -- and round 6's new `test_backfill_rerun_over_an_overlapping_window_does_not_bump_thread_updated_at` was the identical gap, just not yet caught. Fixed by adding item 30, describing the finding and attributing it correctly to Task 3's own Loop 2 round 6 (distinct from this file's own rounds 1-29). Both fixes verified with `.venv/bin/ruff check`/`ruff format --diff` (clean) and the full `test_attention_email_awaiting_reply_postgres.py` + `test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` suite (76 tests, unchanged pass count).

Third, reassessed (per this round's own brief item 4) whether `gmail_adapter.py`'s `_upsert_thread` comment block (added round 6) has grown disproportionate to its code -- measured directly rather than judged by feel: 29 comment lines against 73 total non-blank body lines (~40%), against this same file's own pre-existing functions from earlier tasks (`_coerce_int` at 75%, `_unpack_credential` at 37%, both already in the file before this task touched it) -- comparable to, not an outlier against, this codebase's own established tolerance for a comment-dense function that documents a subtle, previously-shipped-and-broken invariant. Not a liability; no change made. Fourth, reassessed (per round 6's own invitation to revisit with fresh eyes, not merely trust the prior call) `dashboard_briefs.py`'s `_brief_staleness`'s `table_by_type` gap for `email_thread`/`waiting_link` -- confirmed directly against the running database (`\d waiting_links` / `\d email_threads`) that the two cases are not actually symmetric the way round 6's paragraph implied: `waiting_links` genuinely has a real `version bigint` column (so `"waiting_link": "waiting_links"` would be a valid one-line fix, just out of this task's own scope, per round 6's own reasoning, since Task 3 doesn't own `waiting_link`), but `email_threads` has no `version` column at all -- adding `"email_thread": "email_threads"` to the dict as a naive parallel fix would raise `UndefinedColumn` (`column "version" does not exist`) the first time an `email_thread` item reached this function, not silently do nothing. Giving `email_thread` real staleness detection here would need new, special-cased code (recomputing the same `updated_at`-to-microsecond-epoch conversion `attention.py`'s own `email_thread_rows` already does, since `_brief_staleness`'s generic one-`SELECT`-per-type pattern can't express it), not a missing dict entry -- confirming round 6's disclosed-not-fixed call was correct, on stronger grounds than round 6 itself established. No change made.
