## Task 4 evidence

**`recommendations` create-path extension.** Per the implementation plan's own Task 4 scope: `execute_target()` gains an `operation="create"` branch per `target_type` (`task`/`commitment`/`risk`), `RecommendationCreate.target_id` becomes `UUID | None`, and the frontend renders/confirms a create-type recommendation.

**"No second insert path" required a real refactor, not just a new branch -- researched before implementing, not assumed.** `execute_target`'s existing `set_status`/`set_priority`/etc. branches were already raw parametrized SQL `UPDATE`s against `tasks`/`commitments`/`risks`, never calling into `POST /api/v1/tasks`/`/commitments`/`/risks`'s own route handlers at all -- so there was no existing precedent inside `execute_target` for reusing route logic, and `create_task`/`create_commitment`/`create_risk` were each monolithic route handlers with their own `session.begin()` (calling one as-is from inside `confirm_recommendation`'s already-open transaction would raise `InvalidRequestError`, since SQLAlchemy refuses a second top-level `Session.begin()`), their own `Request`/`Idempotency-Key` HTTP dependencies, and their own private `_`-prefixed audit/outbox helpers. Honoring the plan's own explicit "no second insert path" instruction meant extracting a real internal helper from each: `insert_task`/`insert_commitment`/`insert_risk` (public exports, not `_`-prefixed -- cross-domain reuse from `recommendation_targets.py`, the identical `attention.py`/`gmail_adapter.py` lesson Task 3's own round 11 review already established) now hold the INSERT + `_write_audit`/`_write_outbox` logic, taking no `session.begin()`/idempotency-key locking of their own; each route handler's own `with session.begin(): ...` block was rewritten to call its own `insert_*` helper, then run its own idempotency-key storage exactly as before (verified byte-identical behavior via the pre-existing `test_task_postgres.py`/`test_commitment_postgres.py` concurrency/idempotency-replay tests, unchanged and passing). `execute_target`'s new create branch calls the same three helpers directly inside `confirm_recommendation`'s own already-open transaction.

**Schema: `RecommendationCreate` widened, `proposed_fields` reuses the existing `*Create` models per the plan, one migration for one nullable JSONB column.** `target_id: UUID | None`, `expected_version: int | None` (both were already nullable at the DB level -- migration `0009`'s own `recommendations` table never declared `nullable=False` on either, and `RecommendationResponse` already typed both nullable in the response direction, so no column-level migration was needed for them). `proposed_fields: dict[str, Any] | None` is genuinely new (migration `0071_phase10_recs_create_path.py`, one nullable `proposed_fields JSONB` column -- the revision id itself had to be shortened from the plan-derived `0071_phase10_recommendations_create_path` to `0071_phase10_recs_create_path`, 29 characters, after the first migration attempt failed with `StringDataRightTruncation` against `alembic_version.version_num`'s own `varchar(32)` column, caught and fixed by actually running the migration against real Postgres rather than assuming a descriptive revision id would fit). A `model_validator(mode="after")` on `RecommendationCreate` enforces the two shapes are mutually exclusive and internally consistent: `operation="create"` requires `target_id`/`expected_version` both absent and `proposed_fields` present, eagerly re-validated against `TaskCreate`/`CommitmentCreate`/`RiskCreate` (via a `target_type -> Create model` map) at generation time -- a malformed create-type recommendation is rejected with `422` at `POST /api/v1/recommendations`, the same point every other malformed recommendation already is, not deferred to confirm-time; every other operation requires `target_id`/`expected_version` both present and `proposed_fields` absent. `execute_target`'s own create branch re-validates `proposed_fields` against the same `*Create` model a second time at confirm-time (defense in depth against a stored JSONB payload predating a later, stricter model version -- not redundant with the generation-time check, which only ever ran once, at generation).

**`ConfirmAction.target_expected_version` also widened to `int | None`, with its own explicit presence/absence enforcement in `confirm_recommendation` -- not left to a generic "just pass what you have."** A create-type recommendation has no target row to have a version of (`row["expected_version"]` is always `None` for it, per the model validator above), so `confirm_recommendation` branches: `operation="create"` requires `target_expected_version` to be `None` (`422 TARGET_EXPECTED_VERSION_NOT_ALLOWED` if present -- a caller can't accidentally pin a create-type confirm to a version that doesn't exist), every other operation requires it to be present and matching the stored `expected_version` exactly as before (`422 TARGET_EXPECTED_VERSION_REQUIRED` if absent, `409 TARGET_VERSION_CONFLICT` if mismatched, both pre-existing checks, now made conditional rather than removed).

**Superseding a prior pending recommendation for the same target is deliberately NOT extended to create-type recommendations -- a considered scope decision, not an oversight, and it required no code change at all.** `generate_recommendation`'s existing supersede query (`UPDATE recommendations SET status='superseded' ... WHERE target_type=:target_type AND target_id=:target_id AND status IN (...)`) already no-ops correctly for `target_id=NULL` under SQL's own three-valued logic (`x = NULL` is never `TRUE`, even for two rows that are both `NULL`) -- so two independent `operation="create"` recommendations for the same `target_type` (e.g. two different email threads each detecting a distinct action item) simply coexist as two separate `proposed` rows, rather than the second silently replacing the first the way it would for two updates proposed against the identical existing row. Verified via `test_two_pending_create_recommendations_of_the_same_type_do_not_supersede_each_other`.

**`generate_recommendation`'s advisory lock / `target_version` lookup / expected-version match are all skipped for `operation="create"`**, guarded by a single `is_create` branch -- there is no existing target row to serialize concurrent generation against or to check a version for.

**Frontend: `RecommendationPanel.tsx`'s pre-existing confirm button was already, by coincidence, disabled whenever `expected_version == null`** -- exactly the shape a create-type recommendation's `expected_version` always has, so the naive pre-existing guard would have permanently blocked confirming any create-type recommendation. Fixed with an explicit `isCreateRecommendation(item)` check (`proposed_action.operation === 'create'`) so the button is enabled for create-type recommendations regardless of `expected_version`, and `actionPayload`'s own `confirm` case sends `target_expected_version: null` for a create-type item rather than echoing back the always-`null` `item.expected_version` (which happened to produce the same JSON value either way, but the explicit branch documents the reason rather than relying on that coincidence). Added a `<dl>` "Proposed new {target_type}" preview rendering `proposed_fields` for a create-type recommendation, alongside the existing evidence/risk-factor previews (`RiskFactorsPreview`'s own `item.target_id` guard already no-ops correctly for `null`, unchanged).

**Tests.** Backend (`tests/test_recommendations_postgres.py`, +9 tests): schema-validation rejection of `target_id`/`expected_version` present alongside `operation="create"` and of `proposed_fields` present/absent on the wrong side of that split; rejection of `proposed_fields` that fail the target's own `*Create` schema; full generate -> publish -> confirm -> execute for each of task/commitment/risk create-type recommendations, asserting the real new row's fields, `owner_id`, and its own `*.created` audit event; `target_expected_version` presence/absence enforcement in both directions; the no-supersession case above. Frontend (`RecommendationPanel.test.tsx`, +2 tests): `actionPayload`'s `target_expected_version: null` for create-type; the rendered proposed-fields preview and enabled confirm button. Every backend fix mutation-tested by the orchestrating session directly: reverted the `target_id is not None` check in `RecommendationCreate`'s own model validator, confirmed `test_generate_rejects_create_with_target_id_or_expected_version` fails for exactly the predicted reason (`201` instead of `422`), restored, re-confirmed the full 14-test file passes; reverted the frontend confirm-button's `isCreateRecommendation` guard, confirmed the new rendered test fails for exactly the predicted reason (button stays disabled), restored, re-confirmed the full 13-test file passes.

## Task 5 evidence

**AI-runtime action-detection tool.** Per the implementation plan's own Task 5 scope: a new Phase 4 AI task type `email.detect_action` (Pydantic output schema, prompt, evaluation dataset), a deterministic tool giving it grounded access to a thread's own content, and a sync-pipeline hook that runs newly-fetched inbound messages through it and turns a positive detection into a `source="ai"` recommendation via Task 4's create-path -- never a direct write to `tasks`/`commitments`/`risks`.

**Precedent research done before implementing, not assumed.** A foreground research pass mapped this task's exact shape onto Phase 7 Task 5's `personal.generate_insight` -- the only prior AI task type wired end to end through `router.py`/`runtime.py`/`validator.py`/`prompts.py`/`evaluation.py`. It confirmed one structural gap this task would be the first to hit: no internal (non-HTTP) function existed for creating a recommendation -- every existing caller was `POST /api/v1/recommendations` itself. Task 5 is therefore the first non-HTTP caller of the recommendations create-path, one layer up from the identical "no second insert path" discipline Task 4's own `insert_task`/`insert_commitment`/`insert_risk` extraction already established.

**`validator.py`: `EmailDetectActionOutput` is fail-closed in both directions, not just the usual "reject if missing."** `{has_action, target_type, operation, proposed_fields, rationale, confidence, cited_message_ids}` -- a `model_validator(mode="after")` (`_validate_action_shape`) requires `target_type`/`operation`/`proposed_fields`/`cited_message_ids` all present and non-empty when `has_action` is `true`, and requires all four absent/empty when `has_action` is `false`; a model that claims no action but still smuggles a `target_type` fails schema validation just as surely as one that claims an action with no citations. `proposed_fields` is deliberately `dict[str, Any]`, not a re-declaration of `TaskCreate`/`CommitmentCreate`/`RiskCreate` inside the `ai_runtime` domain -- the real, authoritative shape validation against the right `*Create` model already happens exactly once, at `RecommendationCreate`'s own model validator, so duplicating it here would be a driftable second copy; every other task type's own output schema follows the identical "don't import another domain's models" convention. `check_email_detect_action_grounding` mirrors `check_explain_item_grounding`'s citation-grounding pattern against `email_messages.id`s (as returned by the new tool) instead of factor codes, and is a no-op for `has_action: false` (nothing to ground a negative determination against).

**`cited_message_ids` enforcement is schema-level, not grounding-level -- deliberately, and proven end to end, not just asserted.** `_validate_action_shape` requires `cited_message_ids` non-empty whenever `has_action` is `true`; a response claiming an action with an empty citation list fails Pydantic validation (`schema_invalid`) before `check_email_detect_action_grounding` ever runs (whose own `ungrounded` set is computed by intersecting the cited list against valid ids -- trivially empty-passes an empty list, which is exactly why the schema-level guard has to exist first). Proven with a dedicated evaluation-harness test (`test_run_evaluation_has_action_true_with_no_citations_fails_schema_validity`) rather than only unit-tested against the validator in isolation.

**`runtime.py` wiring mirrors `personal.generate_insight`'s exact shape.** `TASK_PORTS["email.detect_action"]` (`prompt_id="email.detect_action.v1"`, `eligible_tools=("email.get_thread_content",)`, `reflection_prompt_id=None`); tool input/output models registered into the shared `_TOOL_INPUT_MODELS`/`_TOOL_OUTPUT_MODELS` maps; `_render_thread_content_block` wraps the thread's own content via `_wrap_untrusted_data` unconditionally (an email body is exactly the externally-authored, potentially adversarial content this module's own threat model exists for -- Decision 6's "grounding check against the source email's own content" requirement is precisely because of this, unlike `_render_insight_source_block`'s workspace-internal `domain_records`); `_prepare_email_detect_action_request` registered into `_PREPARE_REQUEST`; grounding dispatch wired into `_check_grounding`/`_evidence_of`.

**`router.py`/`prompts.py`.** `TASK_REQUIREMENTS["email.detect_action"]` (`capability="extraction"`, `requires_structured_output=True`, `timeout_seconds=40.0`, `max_output_tokens=600`) -- a conservative initial value with no live-model history yet, the same posture `personal.generate_insight`'s own entry documents for itself. `"email.detect_action.v1"` added to `prompts.py`'s `_GATED_PROMPT_IDS`/`_GATED_PROMPT_ID_TASK_TYPES`, so the prompt cannot be activated without a passing evaluation run, same as every other task type's own prompt.

**`email_action_tools.py` (new): the deterministic `email.get_thread_content` tool.** Workspace/owner-scoped read of `email_threads`/`email_messages` (a thread belonging to a different workspace, or a different owner in the same workspace, is `ToolNotFound` -- the same non-disclosing convention every other read tool in this runtime follows), decrypting each message's `body` (the model must reason about real content, not a redaction marker, mirroring `insight_tools.py`'s identical reasoning), catching `InvalidToken` per-message with a placeholder rather than failing the whole call. A message whose `body` is still `NULL` (not yet fetched -- true of every message in a thread except the one that triggered this call, since Task 1/2's `gmail.metadata`-only sync never populates it) is omitted entirely, not rendered as an empty body a small model might mistake for "this message has no content." Messages ordered oldest-first (`sent_at ASC`), the same order a human reads a thread in.

**`gmail_adapter.py`: real body fetch + `pkos_evidence` + sync hook.** `email_messages.body`/`body_fetched_at` were always `NULL` before this task -- Task 1/2's sync only ever fetched `gmail.metadata` (headers). `detect_actions_since(context, since, ollama_adapter=None)` (called from `connector_accounts.sync_connector_endpoint`'s success path via a duck-typed `getattr(adapter, "detect_actions_since", None)` hook -- not a module-level import; kept duck-typed to avoid an engineering-depending-on-personal domain-layering back-reference and to keep `connector_accounts.py` provider-agnostic, not because of an actual circular import -- round 9 review found `ecc.domains.engineering.connectors` itself imports neither `connector_accounts.py` nor `gmail_adapter.py`, so a module-level import here would not in fact cycle) is feature-flagged off by default and, per newly-synced inbound message still missing a body: fetches it (`GET .../messages/{id}?format=full`, `gmail.readonly`), stores it encrypted, resolves the sender via the existing entity-resolution helper, registers a fresh `pkos_evidence` row citing the specific message (`gmail:detect_action:<id>`, deliberately namespaced apart from `_resolve_or_create_person`'s own bare `gmail:<id>` source_ref so the two never collide), and runs `execute_run("email.detect_action", "restricted", ...)`. A `has_action: true` result becomes a `create_recommendation` call; every other outcome (`has_action: false`, a schema/grounding failure, a transient error) is a no-op for that one message, and one message's own failure never stops the batch (`except Exception: continue`, the same "skip only this one, keep going" discipline `_process_message`'s own participant-resolution loop already established). An `ollama_adapter: OllamaAdapter | None = None` parameter threads through to `execute_run` purely so tests can inject a mocked transport (`ai_insights.py`'s own `OllamaAdapterDep` shape); `None` in production is `execute_run`'s own default, a real network-hitting adapter.

**`recommendation_mutations.py`: `create_recommendation` extracted, `synthetic_request` added.** `create_recommendation(session, auth, payload, request, idempotency_key)` is `generate_recommendation`'s full prior body, factored out so a non-HTTP caller can create a recommendation exactly the way `POST /api/v1/recommendations` does; the HTTP endpoint is now a thin wrapper. Called with a bare (not `with session.begin():`-wrapped) session -- `execute_run`/`create_recommendation` each manage their own transaction boundaries and commit internally, the identical "deliberately not wrapped" contract `ai_insights.py:generate_insight_endpoint`'s own docstring already documents for `execute_run`. `record_event`'s `Request` dependency only ever reads `request.state.request_id`/`.correlation_id` -- solved with a new public `synthetic_request(request_id, correlation_id) -> Request` helper (a `SimpleNamespace`-based duck-typed stand-in, `cast` to `Request` for mypy), reusable by any future non-HTTP caller. Verified behavior-preserving: the full pre-existing `tests/test_recommendations_postgres.py` suite (14/14) passes unchanged after the refactor.

**`evaluation.py`: floors + synthetic-source branch.** `email.detect_action` added to `_LATENCY_P95_CEILING_SECONDS_BY_TASK_TYPE`/`_OUTPUT_TEXT_FIELD`; `EvaluationRunCreateRequest.task_type` Literal widened; `_insert_synthetic_email_thread`/`_delete_synthetic_email_thread` wired into `run_evaluation`'s per-task-type branch dispatch, matching every other task type's own synthetic-source pattern. **A real-Postgres FK gap found only by actually running the migration, not by static inspection**: `email_threads` carries a 3-column FK to `personal_domains(workspace_id, owner_id, domain_key)` (migration `0069`) that the first cut of the synthetic-source helper omitted, surfacing as `ForeignKeyViolation` the first time the evaluation ran against real Postgres -- fixed by inserting a `personal_domains` row (`ON CONFLICT ... DO NOTHING`, since multiple examples in one run share the same synthetic owner/workspace) ahead of the thread insert, and deleting it in the matching cleanup helper.

**`config.py`: `email_action_detection_enabled`.** Defaults to `False` (`ECC_EMAIL_ACTION_DETECTION_ENABLED`) -- Tasks 1-4's own sync/OAuth/create-path machinery is entirely unaffected either way; `detect_actions_since` no-ops immediately when unset, verified by a dedicated test asserting zero Gmail/Ollama calls and zero body fetches with the flag left at its real default.

**Migrations.** `0072_phase10_email_detect_action.py`: seeds `tool_definitions` (`email.get_thread_content`, `scopes=["read:email"]`), `prompt_versions` (`email.detect_action.v1`), `routing_policies` (matching `personal.generate_insight`'s own timeout/budget constraints). `0073_phase10_email_detect_eval.py`: seeds `evaluation_sets` with 10 hand-written examples -- 4 positive (a task-shaped deadline request, a commitment-shaped promise, a risk-shaped warning, a multi-message thread whose action only appears in the final message) and 6 negative (newsletter, FYI-only, already-resolved, a vague non-actionable request, a rhetorical question, an automated no-reply notification), per the plan's own explicit requirement that `has_action: false` be a required-to-clear-the-floor outcome, not merely an afterthought. Both revision ids were sized to fit `alembic_version.version_num`'s `varchar(32)` column from the start (`0073_phase10_email_detect_eval`, 30 characters), a lesson carried forward from Task 4's own round-1 migration-naming mistake. Both applied and verified against real Postgres.

**Tests, 17 new at initial implementation (+1 more in Loop 2 round 1 review, 18 total -- see "Task 5 -- Loop 2 review evidence" below), all passing against real Postgres.** `tests/test_email_action_tools_postgres.py` (5): unknown/cross-workspace/cross-owner thread all `ToolNotFound`; genuine decryption in `sent_at` order; a message with no fetched body omitted, not rendered empty. `tests/test_ai_runtime_email_detect_action_evaluation_postgres.py` (9): the seeded evaluation set matches the checked-in fixture (`tests/fixtures/phase10_evaluation_email_detect_action.py`); a fully grounded run passes every floor over the real 10-example dataset (including all 6 negatives); an ungrounded citation fails only the grounding floor; a `must_not_state` violation fails only the prohibited-fact floor; a `has_action: true` response with no citations fails schema validity specifically, not grounding (the shape-vs-grounding distinction proven above); synthetic-source cleanup leaves zero rows in every table it touches; `POST /ai/evaluations/runs` accepts the new task type; the promotion-floor gate blocks activation with no passing evaluation and allows it once one passes. `tests/test_gmail_action_detection_sync_postgres.py` (3, the sync-pipeline wiring the plan calls out by name): a `has_action: true` result creates exactly one `recommendations` row (`status='proposed'`, `source='ai'`, `target_id IS NULL`, `proposed_action->>'operation'='create'`) and zero rows in `tasks`/`commitments`/`risks`, with the triggering message's body genuinely decryptable and a `pkos_evidence` row registered citing it; a `has_action: false` result still fetches and stores the body (the deterministic half of the pipeline always runs) but creates no recommendation; the feature flag left at its real default (`False`) no-ops entirely -- a mocked Gmail/Ollama transport that raises on any request proves neither is ever called. Full regression sweep at initial implementation: `tests/test_gmail_action_detection_sync_postgres.py` + `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` + `tests/test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` + `tests/test_email_action_tools_postgres.py` + `tests/test_ai_runtime_email_detect_action_evaluation_postgres.py` + `tests/test_recommendations_postgres.py` + `tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py`, 207 passed (this same file list is 208 passed as of Loop 2 round 1's own added test -- see below). Static checks: `python -m py_compile`, `ruff check`, `ruff format --diff`, `mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend` (186 source files) all clean.

**No frontend/UX changes -- not an oversight, a scope decision the plan itself makes.** Task 5 produces exactly the same recommendation shape Task 4's `RecommendationPanel.tsx` already renders and confirms (`source="ai"`, `recommendation_type="email_action_detected"`, an `operation="create"` proposal) -- no new UX state is introduced, so `UX-STATES.md` is unchanged by this task. The plan's own Task 8 ("Executive UX and browser acceptance") is where this phase's UI surface is scoped.

### Task 5 -- Loop 2 review evidence

**Round 1 (security/correctness): found and fixed 1 HIGH issue, plus 2 LOW issues.** **HIGH**: `sync_connector_endpoint`'s own proactive-detection call site (`connector_accounts.py`) called `detect_actions(context, since=now, auth=auth)` -- but `GmailAdapter.detect_actions_since` has never accepted an `auth` parameter (it deliberately builds its own `AuthContext` from the account's own `owner_id`, per its own docstring). Every real invocation therefore raised `TypeError`, silently swallowed by the deliberately broad `except Exception: pass` immediately around the call -- the entire feature never ran in production, with no trace anywhere that it failed, independent of the feature flag. Not caught by any of the 17 tests written alongside the feature, since every one of them calls `detect_actions_since` directly, bypassing the actual production call site entirely. Fixed by dropping the stray `auth=auth` keyword; closed with a new regression test, `test_sync_invokes_proactive_action_detection_with_a_valid_call_signature` (`tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py`, item 26 in that file's own docstring), which drives the real HTTP `/sync` endpoint with a fake adapter (`_ActionDetectionSpyAdapter`) whose `detect_actions_since` signature exactly mirrors the real one -- a future call-site drift raises the identical `TypeError` and fails this test loudly rather than being swallowed. Independently mutation-tested by the orchestrating session: reintroduced the stray `auth=auth` keyword, confirmed the new test fails for exactly the predicted reason (`detect_actions_since_calls` stays empty despite the sync call still returning `201`), restored, re-confirmed the full 167-test regression sweep passes. **LOW** (both defense-in-depth, not live bugs): `detect_actions_since`'s own message-selection query had no `LIMIT` of its own, relying only on an indirect coupling to `backfill`/`incremental_sync`'s own `_MAX_MESSAGES_PER_CALL` bound rather than bounding its own per-call work the way every other loop in this file already does -- fixed by adding an explicit `LIMIT :limit` bound to the same constant. A mid-flight failure between the body-fetch commit and `create_recommendation` completing permanently skips that message on every future call (the `WHERE ... AND body IS NULL` selection guard never matches it again) -- not fixed (retrying would need a signal this method doesn't currently track), documented explicitly in `detect_actions_since`'s own docstring as a deliberate trade-off instead.

**Round 1 (architecture/quality): found and confirmed the same HIGH issue independently, plus 3 more issues.** Independently identified the identical call-site/`auth` bug above via a full read of the same code, without reference to the security lens's own finding. Additionally: stale "Tasks 1-4"/"Tasks 5-8" language survived in `docs/phases/PHASE-010-gmail-connector.md`'s own "Delivery status" sentence (not touched by this PR's original diff, only by the doc-governance-repair merge) -- fixed, now reads "Tasks 1-5"/"Tasks 6-8", matching `docs/phases/status.json`. `GroundingFailure`'s own docstring (`validator.py`) claimed `email.detect_action` has a second failure-mode reason, `missing_citation_for_action`, distinct from `ungrounded_citation` -- but `check_email_detect_action_grounding` never actually constructs a `GroundingFailure` with that reason anywhere (the real enforcement for that case is schema-level, via `EmailDetectActionOutput._validate_action_shape`, exactly as this same evidence section's own "schema-level, not grounding-level" paragraph above already documents) -- the Literal member was genuinely dead (confirmed via a repo-wide grep with zero other references) and its docstring actively misattributed a failure mode to a function that could never produce it; fixed by removing the unused Literal member and correcting the docstring to state plainly that `email.detect_action` has no second grounding-level failure mode. `_prepare_email_detect_action_request` (`runtime.py`) passed `tool_input={"thread_id": raw_thread_id}` unstringified, unlike its sibling `_prepare_explain_item_request`'s own `str()`-wrapped pattern -- harmless today only because the sole real caller (`gmail_adapter.py`) already pre-stringifies, but inconsistent with the established defensive convention; fixed to match. Also flagged (documented, not changed): a created recommendation's `evidence_ids` names only the newly-fetched triggering message, not every id the model's own `cited_message_ids` may reference from earlier, already-fetched messages in the same thread -- a deliberate scope decision (no message-id -> evidence-id index exists to look older citations back up by, and re-registering would duplicate evidence already on record), now stated explicitly as such in `_detect_action_for_message`'s own inline comment rather than left implicit.

**Full regression evidence (round 1).** `tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` + `tests/test_gmail_action_detection_sync_postgres.py` + `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` + `tests/test_ai_runtime_email_detect_action_evaluation_postgres.py` + `tests/test_email_action_tools_postgres.py` + `tests/test_ai_runtime_evaluation_postgres.py` + `tests/test_ai_runtime_personal_insight_evaluation_postgres.py`: 167 passed. Static checks: `python -m py_compile`, `ruff check`, `ruff format --diff`, `mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend` (186 source files) all clean. `scripts/docs_status.py check` and `scripts/check_docs.py` (the doc-governance gates the merged-in repair commit added) both clean.

Round 1 found real issues on both lenses, so the loop does not close here -- round 2 needs two fresh, independent, consecutive clean rounds to close.

**Round 2 (security/correctness): found and fixed 1 MEDIUM issue, plus 1 LOW.** **MEDIUM**: `detect_actions_since`'s own detection loop runs entirely synchronously, inline inside `sync_connector_endpoint`'s own HTTP request handler -- per candidate message, a live Gmail body fetch *and* a live Ollama inference call (`router.py`'s own `timeout_seconds=40.0` per call), sequentially, before the `/sync` response ever returns. `connector_accounts.py`'s own module docstring justifies synchronous (non-worker) dispatch on the premise that "a sync call is a single adapter method call" -- true for plain `backfill`/`incremental_sync`, no longer true once this hook is layered on top of it. With the feature flag on, a backfill that returns many new inbound messages could block one `/sync` request for minutes, tying up a threadpool worker (the handler is not `async def`) well past most reverse-proxy/gateway timeouts, even though the sync itself already succeeded and was durably recorded before this hook ever runs. Dormant today (`email_action_detection_enabled` defaults `False`), but a real risk the moment it's enabled. Fixed with a dedicated, much smaller bound -- `_MAX_ACTION_DETECTIONS_PER_CALL = 5` (`gmail_adapter.py`, deliberately distinct from `backfill`/`incremental_sync`'s own `_MAX_MESSAGES_PER_CALL = 200`, replacing round 1's own reuse of that larger constant as this query's `LIMIT`) -- and documented explicitly, in both `gmail_adapter.py`'s own constant comment and a new paragraph in `connector_accounts.py`'s module docstring, that this bound reduces but does not eliminate the risk; moving this hook to `ecc.domains.automation.worker`'s own durable dispatch remains the real fix and is out of scope for a review-fix round of this size. **LOW**: `crypto.py`'s own module docstring claimed `ecc.domains.personal.domains` was "the only caller" of `encrypt_field`/`decrypt_field` -- stale as of Task 5, which added two more direct callers (`gmail_adapter.py`'s body encryption, `email_action_tools.py`'s decryption for the AI runtime), the identical "stale caller-count/scope claim" class of doc drift round 1 already found once in `PHASE-010-gmail-connector.md`. Fixed by correcting the docstring to name both new callers and why they reuse this module's key rather than introducing a second one.

**Round 2 (architecture/quality): found and fixed 1 MEDIUM issue, plus 1 LOW.** Independently identified round 1's HIGH call-site bug as already-fixed via a fresh read, without reference to the security lens's own round 1 finding -- confirms the fix is genuinely in place, not just claimed. **MEDIUM**: the original "Tests, 17 new" evidence paragraph above went stale the moment round 1 added an 18th test (`test_sync_invokes_proactive_action_detection_with_a_valid_call_signature`) to a fourth file -- the round-1 subsection right below it even restates "Not caught by any of the 17 tests" in the same breath as describing that 18th test, without the running total or the dependent "207 passed" full-regression-sweep figure ever being rolled forward (that same 7-file sweep, re-run today, is 208, not 207). Fixed by adding an explicit forward pointer and corrected figures to both paragraphs. **LOW**: `_ActionDetectionSpyAdapter` (round 1's own new fake adapter in `tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py`) duplicated `_MessageCursorAdapter`'s six method bodies verbatim rather than subclassing it, unlike this same file's own established precedent for exactly this situation (`_RaisingDisconnectAdapter(_SpyDisconnectAdapter)`, overriding only what differs). Fixed by making `_ActionDetectionSpyAdapter` subclass `_MessageCursorAdapter`, keeping only the new `detect_actions_since_calls` field and `detect_actions_since` method. Also re-confirmed, via a fresh repo-wide grep, that round 1's removed `missing_citation_for_action` Literal member has zero remaining references anywhere in code, tests, or docs (only the historical narration in this evidence section's own round-1 paragraph still names it, which is correct -- it is describing what round 1 found and fixed).

**Full regression evidence (round 2).** `tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` + `tests/test_gmail_action_detection_sync_postgres.py` + `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` + `tests/test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` + `tests/test_ai_runtime_email_detect_action_evaluation_postgres.py` + `tests/test_email_action_tools_postgres.py` + `tests/test_recommendations_postgres.py`: 208 passed. Static checks: `python -m py_compile`, `ruff check`, `ruff format --diff`, `mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend` (186 source files) all clean.

Round 2 found and fixed real issues on both lenses, so the loop still does not close -- round 3 needs two fresh, independent, consecutive clean rounds to close.

**Round 3 (security/correctness): found and fixed 1 HIGH-equivalent issue, plus 1 LOW.** **HIGH-equivalent** (silent, permanent, unbounded-severity data-coverage loss, not merely a latency risk): `detect_actions_since`'s eligibility query filtered on `m.created_at >= :since` in addition to `m.body IS NULL` -- round 1's own docstring framed this as "for every inbound message newly written by this sync call." Round 2's own fix (shrinking the query's `LIMIT` from `_MAX_MESSAGES_PER_CALL=200` to `_MAX_ACTION_DETECTIONS_PER_CALL=5` to bound per-call latency) had an unintended second-order consequence neither round 1 nor round 2 caught: any candidate left over after one call (more than 5 eligible messages existed, ordinary for e.g. an initial 30-day backfill) would have its `created_at` permanently fall behind every later call's own, strictly-later `since` -- silently and permanently excluded from all future detection, not merely delayed, with no error, log, or trace anywhere. `body IS NULL` alone is a durable, monotonic backlog signal (a message's body, once fetched, never reverts to `NULL`); `created_at >= since` is not, once a per-call cap exists that can leave real candidates unprocessed. Fixed by dropping the `created_at >= since` filter entirely and instead using it only to order (not exclude) results -- `ORDER BY (m.created_at >= :since) DESC, m.sent_at ASC` prioritizes this call's own newly-synced messages first while guaranteeing any backlog is worked down over subsequent calls rather than lost. Closed with a new regression test, `test_overflow_beyond_one_calls_own_bound_is_picked_up_by_the_next_call_not_lost` (`tests/test_gmail_action_detection_sync_postgres.py`): seeds `_MAX_ACTION_DETECTIONS_PER_CALL + 2` eligible messages, asserts exactly the bound is processed on the first call, then calls again with a genuinely later `since` and asserts every message is eventually fetched -- independently mutation-tested by the orchestrating session: reverted to the original `created_at >= :since` filter, confirmed the new test fails for exactly the predicted reason (2 of 9 messages permanently unfetched after the second call), restored, re-confirmed the full 209-test regression sweep passes. This same test also closes round 3's own architecture-lens finding (below) that `_MAX_ACTION_DETECTIONS_PER_CALL`'s limiting behavior itself had no direct test coverage. **LOW**: `EmailDetectActionOutput._validate_action_shape`'s `has_action=False` branch never actually checked `cited_message_ids` is empty, despite three separate docstrings (this class's own, `GroundingFailure`'s, and `_EMAIL_DETECT_ACTION_REPAIR_INSTRUCTION`'s) all stating it's "enforced below" or "guaranteed empty." No live security/write-path consequence (`check_email_detect_action_grounding` already short-circuits to `None` for `has_action=False` regardless), but a false invariant stated three times as something other code could come to rely on. Fixed by adding the missing check, matching the pattern of the other three fields in the same branch.

**Round 3 (architecture/quality): found and fixed 1 MEDIUM issue (the same gap security's HIGH-equivalent finding closed), plus 2 non-fixes explicitly logged as reviewed-and-accepted.** Independently flagged the missing direct test coverage for `_MAX_ACTION_DETECTIONS_PER_CALL`'s own limiting behavior -- closed by the same new regression test described above, which asserts the bound is respected on the first call as its own explicit checkpoint before asserting the cross-call recovery. Also reviewed and explicitly accepted as non-issues (not fixed, because there was nothing to fix): the constant's placement a few lines away from Task 2's own thematically-related sync-tuning constants (a minor organizational note, not confusing in practice, since the constant's own comment already cross-references the different code path it governs); the constant's comment length (proportionate to this file's own established style for other bounds); the new `connector_accounts.py` docstring paragraph's tone and integration (matches every surrounding paragraph); round 2's "reduces but does not eliminate" framing (verified honest -- nothing overclaims safety the fix doesn't provide); and `docs/phases/status.json`'s `last_verified_commit` still pointing at the original Task 5 commit rather than either fix commit (flagged only for awareness, not fixed, since other phases in the same registry follow an identical batch-audit-not-every-commit convention this isn't inconsistent with).

**Full regression evidence (round 3).** `tests/test_gmail_action_detection_sync_postgres.py` (now 4 tests, +1 this round) + `tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` + `tests/test_gmail_connector_sync_postgres.py` + `tests/test_gmail_connector_postgres.py` + `tests/test_ai_runtime_email_detect_action_evaluation_postgres.py` + `tests/test_email_action_tools_postgres.py` + `tests/test_recommendations_postgres.py`: 209 passed. Static checks: `python -m py_compile`, `ruff check`, `ruff format --diff`, `mypy --config-file pyproject.toml backend` (186 source files) all clean.

Round 3 found and fixed a real, high-impact issue on the security lens (independently corroborated by the architecture lens), so the loop still does not close -- round 4 needs two fresh, independent, consecutive clean rounds to close.

**Round 4 (security/correctness): found and fixed 3 issues -- one HIGH-equivalent, two MEDIUM.** **HIGH-equivalent**: `sync_connector_endpoint`'s own Task 5 hook (`connector_accounts.py`) gated the call to `detect_actions_since` on `outcome.items_processed > 0` -- but `items_processed` counts only messages *this* `backfill`/`incremental_sync` call itself fetched (new or duplicate), so on any ordinary poll where Gmail reports no new activity since the last sync (steady-state for a normal-cadence connector, not an edge case) it stays `0` and the hook never ran at all. This silently defeated round 3's own fix -- `detect_actions_since`'s "a backlog beyond one call's own bound is worked down over subsequent calls" guarantee -- for exactly the case that guarantee exists to cover: a quiet call following a burst that left a backlog behind. Fixed by dropping the gate entirely (`detect_actions_since` is itself cheap to call when there is nothing to do -- its own eligibility query returns zero rows, then an immediate return -- so nothing is saved by skipping the call, only correctness was lost). Closed with a new regression test, `test_sync_invokes_proactive_action_detection_even_when_this_calls_own_items_processed_is_zero` (`tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py`), extending `_ActionDetectionSpyAdapter` with a caller-controlled `items_processed` field (default `1`, matching `_MessageCursorAdapter`'s own hardcoded value) so a test can prove the hook still fires with `items_processed=0`. **MEDIUM** (1 of 2): round 3's own fix made `detect_actions_since`'s eligibility query's only filter (`body IS NULL`) unbounded by time, with no supporting index -- the only existing indexes on `email_messages` (`ix_email_messages_thread_sent`, thread-scoped) do not support a cross-thread `workspace_id`/`owner_id`/`direction` scan, so the query's cost now scales with an account's entire un-fetched-body history rather than a recent window. Fixed with a new partial index, migration `0074_phase10_email_detect_idx.py` -- `CREATE INDEX ... ON email_messages (workspace_id, owner_id, direction) WHERE body IS NULL` -- partial specifically so it only ever indexes the (small, steady-state) not-yet-fetched backlog, not the account's full mail history; a message drops out of the index entirely once its body is fetched (or marked unfetchable, see next finding). Upgrade/downgrade/re-upgrade round-trip verified against real Postgres, index presence confirmed via `\d email_messages` at each step. **MEDIUM** (2 of 2): a message whose Gmail response parses fully (200, valid JSON, a dict `payload`) but has no `text/plain` part anywhere in its MIME tree (HTML-only mail) previously left `body IS NULL` forever -- with round 3's own fix making `body IS NULL` the *only* eligibility filter, such a message would be re-selected, re-fetched, and re-rejected by `detect_actions_since` on every future call indefinitely: a "poison" candidate that never leaves the backlog and consumes one of `_MAX_ACTION_DETECTIONS_PER_CALL`'s five slots on every call forever. Fixed in `_detect_action_for_message` by writing an empty-string body sentinel (rather than leaving `body IS NULL`) once the response has been fully, successfully parsed and genuinely has nothing extractable -- this is exactly `email_action_tools.py`'s own pre-existing "genuinely empty" case (that module's docstring distinguishes it from "not yet fetched"), so `get_thread_content_tool` already renders it correctly as an empty message rather than omitting it. Every earlier early-return in `_detect_action_for_message` (non-200/`None` response, unparseable JSON, non-dict body, missing/non-dict `payload`) deliberately still leaves `body IS NULL` and stays eligible for retry, since those genuinely may be transient (a rate-limited or 5xx response, a malformed proxy response) rather than a fact about the message itself. Closed with a new regression test, `test_no_extractable_plain_text_is_marked_handled_not_retried_forever` (`tests/test_gmail_action_detection_sync_postgres.py`): a first call fetches an HTML-only message and asserts its body decrypts to `""`; a second call, whose Gmail transport raises on any request, proves the message is no longer eligible. All three fixes independently mutation-tested by the orchestrating session: (1) reintroduced the `outcome.items_processed > 0` gate, confirmed the new test fails for exactly the predicted reason (`len(spy.detect_actions_since_calls) == 0`), restored, reconfirmed; (2) migration downgrade/upgrade round-trip confirmed the index is dropped and recreated correctly; (3) reintroduced an early `return` on `not plain_text` before the body-sentinel write, confirmed the new test fails for exactly the predicted reason (`body is None` after the first call), restored, reconfirmed.

**Round 4 (architecture/quality): found and fixed 2 LOW issues.** `tests/test_gmail_action_detection_sync_postgres.py`'s own module docstring "Covers" list had not been updated for round 3's own new overflow/backlog test -- fixed by adding items 5 (round 4's own new no-extractable-plain-text test) and 6 (round 3's overflow test, retroactively documented) to the list. `detection_context` and `backlog_context` (that same file's two fixtures) duplicated roughly 170 lines of identical workspace/identity/connector-account/domain/consent/thread setup, differing only in account display name, thread subject, and the message-insert loop each performs afterward -- fixed by factoring the shared setup into `_seed_base_rows(*, account_display_name, thread_subject)`, returning `(workspace_id, owner_id, account_id, thread_id, credential, now)`, and the shared teardown into `_teardown_workspace(workspace_id)`; both fixtures now insert only their own distinct `email_messages` rows. Re-verified via the full 5-test file run (unchanged pass count and behavior) plus a `ruff check --select F401,F841` pass confirming no unused imports or variables survived the refactor.

**Round 5 (security/correctness): found and fixed 1 CRITICAL issue.** The evaluation harness's own synthetic-source insert/delete pair for `email.detect_action` (`evaluation.py`) -- `_insert_synthetic_email_thread`/`_delete_synthetic_email_thread`, added at Task 5's original implementation, untouched by rounds 1-4 -- inserted a `personal_domains` row for `(workspace_id=auth.workspace_id, owner_id=auth.user_id, domain_key='email')` with `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`, deliberately preserving any real, pre-existing row rather than clobbering it, but the matching cleanup deleted `personal_domains` by that same `(workspace_id, owner_id, domain_key)` triple alone -- with no way to tell "the row this call itself created" apart from "the real row that was already there and the `ON CONFLICT` branch left untouched." `email_threads` has a 3-column FK to `personal_domains(workspace_id, owner_id, domain_key)` with `ondelete="CASCADE"` (migration `0069`), and `domain_consents` cascades from the identical FK (migration `0054`), so this cleanup would have deleted a real, already-connected user's entire Gmail sync history (every thread and message) plus their `email` domain-consent record the first time this evaluation harness ran for a caller who had already connected Gmail and enabled the `email` personal domain -- reachable by any authenticated `write`-role user calling `POST /api/v1/ai/evaluations/runs` with `task_type: "email.detect_action"`, and also as a side effect of the `email.detect_action.v1` prompt-promotion gate (which requires a passing evaluation run first), on the very first example scored, since cleanup runs per-example inside the loop. Never caught by any existing test because every one of them creates a brand-new workspace/user with no pre-existing `personal_domains` row, so the `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` branch never actually fired in any test run -- the pre-existing-row collision path had zero coverage. The sibling `personal.generate_insight` harness in the same file (`_insert_synthetic_personal_insight_sources`/`_delete_synthetic_personal_insight_sources`) never had this gap: it returns the specific `domain_ids` its own insert created and deletes `WHERE ... AND id = ANY(:ids)`, never by the shared key triple. Fixed by mirroring that pattern -- `_insert_synthetic_email_thread`'s own `personal_domains` INSERT now uses `RETURNING id`, captured via `.scalar_one_or_none()` (`None` exactly when the `ON CONFLICT` branch fired), returned alongside the function's existing values; `_delete_synthetic_email_thread` takes that id as a new `domain_id` parameter and only deletes `personal_domains` (scoped by that specific `id`) when it is not `None`, leaving a real pre-existing row completely untouched otherwise. Closed with a new regression test, `test_run_evaluation_does_not_delete_a_pre_existing_real_personal_domain_or_its_data` (`tests/test_ai_runtime_email_detect_action_evaluation_postgres.py`): seeds a real, pre-existing `personal_domains` row plus a real `email_threads`/`email_messages` row under it (exactly what an already-connected caller would have on disk), runs the evaluation harness, and asserts every one of those rows survives while the run itself still completes and passes normally. Independently mutation-tested by the orchestrating session: reverted `_delete_synthetic_email_thread`'s fix back to the original unscoped `(workspace_id, owner_id, domain_key)` delete, confirmed the new test fails for exactly the predicted reason (the real, pre-existing `personal_domains` row is gone -- `assert 0 == 1`), restored, reconfirmed all 10 tests in the file pass.

**Round 6 (security/correctness): found and fixed 1 MEDIUM issue, independently corroborated by both review agents.** Round 5's own CRITICAL fix correctly closed the case of a single `email.detect_action` evaluation run colliding with a *pre-existing real* `personal_domains` row, but left open a narrower, still-real race: two genuinely concurrent `run_evaluation` calls for the *same* `(workspace_id, owner_id)` (e.g. a retried/double-triggered evaluation request, or two evaluation requests with different `Idempotency-Key`s racing -- nothing serializes this the way `meeting.prep_summary` already is via its own dedicated lock). `_insert_synthetic_email_thread`'s `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` insert against `personal_domains` correctly ensures only one of the two runs ever "owns" that row and only that run's cleanup ever deletes it (round 5's fix, still correct) -- but *every* `email_threads` row either run creates for this caller, whichever run's own or the other's, shares that one `personal_domains` row via its `ondelete="CASCADE"` FK (migration `0069`). `_delete_synthetic_email_thread` runs per-example, not batched at the end of `run_evaluation`, so the owning run's very first per-example cleanup cascades and deletes every `email_threads` row currently matching that shared key -- including the other, still-in-flight run's own synthetic thread, mid-run, not merely at its own eventual cleanup. This produces corrupted mid-run data (an unexpected `ToolNotFound` from `email.get_thread_content`, or worse, a deadlock, per the reproduction below) for the losing run, feeding a false failure into `check_promotion_floors`. Fixed with a new `_synthetic_email_thread_serialization_lock(workspace_id, owner_id)`, mirroring `_synthetic_meeting_serialization_lock`'s own `pg_advisory_lock` pattern but for a different collision mechanism (a shared-row `CASCADE`-delete race, not a deterministic-id `INSERT` collision) and scoped by `owner_id` in addition to `workspace_id` (the collision key here is per-owner, not workspace-wide) -- wired into `run_evaluation`'s existing `serialization_lock` selection alongside the meeting lock. Closed with a new regression test, `test_run_evaluation_concurrent_same_caller_runs_do_not_corrupt_each_others_data` (mirroring `test_ai_runtime_meeting_prep_evaluation_postgres.py`'s own `test_run_evaluation_concurrent_same_workspace_runs_do_not_collide`): two genuinely concurrent `run_evaluation` calls for the same caller, asserting both complete, pass every floor, and leave zero synthetic rows behind. Independently mutation-tested by the orchestrating session, including reproducing the actual failure before trusting either review agent's own report: temporarily removed the new lock, ran the new test 15 times in a loop (a race is nondeterministic by nature, so a single run cannot be trusted either way) -- it passed most runs but failed on attempt 1 and again on attempt 7 with a genuine Postgres `DeadlockDetected` error on concurrent `DELETE FROM email_threads` (`Process ... waits for ShareLock on transaction ...; blocked by process ...`), exactly the predicted mechanism; restored the fix and reconfirmed 10/10 clean runs. Also reviewed and explicitly not fixed, flagged as out of scope: the security-lens agent found that `personal.generate_insight`'s own sibling harness (`_insert_synthetic_personal_insight_sources`, Phase 7 code, untouched by this PR) has the identical shared-`personal_domains`-row exposure across concurrent runs, but with no `ON CONFLICT` guard at all -- a losing concurrent run's plain `INSERT` would raise an uncaught `IntegrityError` rather than corrupt data, a crash instead of silent corruption, but still an unhandled-500 gap. Genuinely pre-existing (this PR did not introduce or touch it) and outside Task 5's own scope; noted here for awareness, matching this document's own established convention of flagging out-of-scope findings rather than silently absorbing unrelated work into a review-fix round.

**Round 7 (security/correctness): found and fixed 1 MEDIUM-HIGH issue, plus 1 minor doc-drift item.** **MEDIUM-HIGH**: `detect_actions_since`'s per-row batch loop never re-checked `email` domain consent before each message's own live Gmail fetch and write, unlike `_sync_messages`/`_sync_history` (both Task 2 sync paths), which explicitly re-verify `_email_consent_active` before every single message write with an inline comment citing the phase's own design contract. `SYNC-CONTRACT.md` states this as a general contract, not scoped to any one sync path: "An active `email` domain consent is checked before external fetch and again before each message write. Revocation during a call stops further writes." Task 5's own body-fetch-and-write pipeline -- a live external fetch followed by a message write -- is exactly what that sentence is written to govern, but the implementation only ever checked consent once, before selecting the batch, not before each of the batch's own writes. This mattered concretely, not just as a documentation gap: `docs/phases/phase-010/IMPLEMENTATION-STATUS.md`'s own Task 7 status confirms consent-revocation disconnect/purge is "Not started" -- today, revoking the `email` domain is a pure DB flag flip with no automatic OAuth revocation or data purge, so the per-write consent recheck is the *only* enforcement currently protecting a user who disables the domain expecting processing to stop. Without it, a user revoking consent mid-batch could still have up to `_MAX_ACTION_DETECTIONS_PER_CALL - 1` (4) further messages fetched, decrypted-and-stored, run through an AI model, and turned into a persisted recommendation after they withdrew consent -- with no future purge job to ever clean any of it up. Fixed by adding the identical per-row recheck `_sync_messages`/`_sync_history` already do, `return`ing (not `continue`-ing) on revocation to halt the whole remaining batch, matching those two paths' own "halts the call" semantics rather than merely skipping the one row. Closed with a new regression test, `test_batch_halts_when_consent_is_revoked_mid_batch` (`tests/test_gmail_action_detection_sync_postgres.py`): revokes consent as a side effect of the first message's own Gmail fetch, asserts no further message is ever fetched or stored. Also added a missing `owner_id` key to `backlog_context`'s own yielded fixture dict (it was computed by `_seed_base_rows` but never surfaced, unlike `detection_context`'s identical fixture, which already exposed it) -- needed by the new test, a minor completion rather than a defect of its own. **Minor**: `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py`'s own docstring for `test_sync_invokes_proactive_action_detection_with_a_valid_call_signature` still described the sync-hook call site as "gated only by `outcome.items_processed > 0`" -- stale since round 4 removed that gate entirely; fixed to describe the call site's current, gate-less state and cross-reference the test immediately below it that already proves the gate is gone. Independently mutation-tested by the orchestrating session: reverted the per-row consent recheck, confirmed the new test fails for exactly the predicted reason (all 5 batch messages fetched despite revocation on the first), restored, reconfirmed all 6 tests in the file pass plus the broader 3-file/142-test regression sweep.

**Round 8 (security/correctness): clean.** A genuinely adversarial, full read of every file this PR touches, focused in particular on round 7's own new per-row consent recheck in `detect_actions_since` per this round's brief. Traced the recheck's session lifecycle in full: it opens and closes its own short-lived session before any later network/DB access, queries an entirely different table (`domain_consents`) than the body-fetch `UPDATE`'s own optimistic-concurrency guard or the round-4 partial index, so there is no shared-lock or deadlock path between them. Confirmed the check-then-fetch-then-write TOCTOU window the recheck leaves (consent could theoretically be revoked between the recheck and the live Gmail fetch that follows it) is the same, already-accepted "up to one message's worth of lag" window `_sync_messages`/`_sync_history` have always had for the identical shape -- not new, not worse than established precedent. Investigated whether two concurrent `/sync` calls for the same connector account could run overlapping `detect_actions_since` batches (`uq_sync_runs_running_per_account` only blocks while a `sync_runs` row is `status='running'`, which flips to terminal *before* this best-effort hook even starts) -- possible in principle, but each concurrent call still independently rechecks consent before its own writes and the `body IS NULL` optimistic guard prevents any double-write, so this is a duplicate-work/rate-limit concern at worst, not a correctness or security bug, and not introduced by this round. Traced `_detect_action_for_message`'s three separate `SessionFactory()` uses end to end and confirmed `execute_run`'s own `_persist_terminal` always commits as its last statement on every return path, so `create_recommendation`'s own later `session.rollback()` (inside `require_active_role`) can never discard an uncommitted `ai_runs`/`ai_run_steps` write. Re-spot-checked rounds 1, 3, 4, 5, and 6's fixes and found each genuinely still in place. No new issues found.

Not a functional bug -- `_render_thread_content_block` genuinely does render `messages` in whatever order it receives them, and the caller does supply `sent_at ASC` -- but a cross-reference that points a reader to a docstring not actually making the claim cited. Fixed by rewording the cross-reference to state plainly that `_render_thread_content_block` has no ordering of its own and this module's own `ORDER BY` is the entire ordering guarantee. **Round 9 (security/correctness): found and fixed 1 real issue -- `_extract_plain_text_body`'s recursive MIME walk (and, one call site up, `get_response.json()`'s own JSON decode) has no depth cap, reopening the round-4-fixed "poison message" failure class through a new trigger.** `_extract_plain_text_body` recurses one Python stack frame per `payload.parts` nesting level with no cap of its own; a maliciously deep `multipart/mixed` structure (a sender fully controls their own outgoing MIME tree, the identical premise `_parse_address`/`_getaddresses_resilient`/`_to_header_has_grammar_defect`'s own `RecursionError` guards, added rounds 6-7, already rest on for header parsing) drives it past the interpreter's recursion limit and raises an uncaught `RecursionError` -- a type neither of `_detect_action_for_message`'s two surrounding checks anticipated (`except ValueError` around `get_response.json()`; no guard at all around the `_extract_plain_text_body` call). Independently reproduced standalone (outside the full application, since this environment's own pinned Python 3.14.6 is unavailable here -- see the local-verification note below): a pure-Python copy of `_extract_plain_text_body`, walking a ~5,000-level-deep nested structure built from the same shape Gmail's `messages.get(format=full)` returns, raises `RecursionError` exactly as predicted; a second, independent check confirmed `httpx.Response.json()`'s own `json.loads` call has an identical, uncapped recursive descent and can raise the same exception type one call site earlier, before `_extract_plain_text_body` is ever reached, depending on how deep the C-accelerated decoder (if present in a given environment) tolerates before falling back to the pure-Python path. Both are reached *before* the body-`UPDATE` that marks a message handled, so -- uncaught -- the message's `body` stays `NULL` forever; `detect_actions_since`'s own `WHERE ... AND body IS NULL` eligibility query has no way to distinguish "not yet fetched" from "fetched, but this specific structure crashes every attempt," so it would re-select, re-fetch, and re-crash on this exact message on every future call, indefinitely -- the identical symptom round 4's own fix closed for a different trigger (no extractable `text/plain` part), reopened here through a structurally deeper input. Fixed by widening `get_response.json()`'s `except ValueError` into two separate clauses (`ValueError` unchanged; a new `except RecursionError`) and wrapping the `_extract_plain_text_body` call in its own `try`/`except RecursionError`, both funneling into the same `plain_text = None` -> empty-string sentinel path round 4 already established for "structurally parsed, nothing extractable." **A refinement made during this round's own fix, beyond what review reported:** the first draft lumped the new `except RecursionError` in with the pre-existing `except ValueError` (both `return`ing, leaving `body IS NULL`) -- which would have left the `get_response.json()` trigger un-fixed in substance, only non-crashing, since a `return` here is exactly as "retried forever" as an uncaught exception once `detect_actions_since`'s own outer `except Exception: continue` catches it either way. Caught by re-tracing the two triggers' actual failure semantics rather than trusting the first draft's pattern-match to `_parse_address`'s stdlib-parsing precedent: unlike a genuinely malformed/truncated JSON body (`ValueError`, presumed a transient response glitch, correctly left `return`-and-retry), a JSON payload nested deeply enough to blow the recursion limit is a *deterministic* function of this specific message's own immutable stored MIME structure -- Gmail returns byte-identical content on every retry -- so it belongs with the "permanent property of the message" class the round-4 sentinel exists for, not with the "presumed transient" class. Restructured with `try`/`except ValueError`/`except RecursionError`/`else` so the `RecursionError` branch falls through to the sentinel write like `_extract_plain_text_body`'s own guard, while `ValueError` keeps its original `return` semantics unchanged.

**Round 10 (security/correctness): found and fixed 1 real issue -- `email.detect_action` had no prompt-injection test of its own, unlike its two sibling task types, despite `docs/phases/phase-010/TEST-PLAN.md`'s own explicit "prompt-injection ... tests" requirement.** The untrusted-content surface here is also structurally different from (and larger-population than) the two existing injection tests' own fixtures: an email body from *any* external Gmail sender the account has ever received mail from, not a workspace-internal record (a factor label, a meeting participant name) an attacker would first need some other foothold to write. This round's own security-lens review additionally concluded, in its first pass, that this task type has *no* tool-call allowlist backstop at all -- reasoning that `email.get_thread_content` is dispatched deterministically by `_prepare_email_detect_action_request` before the model ever runs, so the model itself is never in a position to request an out-of-scope tool the way the sibling tests' injected content steers a *second*, model-requested tool call. **Independently re-verified and found factually incorrect**, by reading `execute_run` itself rather than trusting the finding's own reasoning: the `_try_parse_tool_call_request`/`_dispatch_tool(..., eligible_tools=port.eligible_tools)` pair that runs immediately after every task type's *primary* model call (not `_prepare_email_detect_action_request`'s own Step 1) is fully generic across every task type, keyed by `port.eligible_tools` from the same `TASK_PORTS` dict every task type shares -- `execute_run`'s own comment states this plainly ("Step 1: this task's own deterministic required-input tool call -- dispatched through the exact same allowlist-gated path any model-requested tool call also goes through"). So a model response for `email.detect_action` shaped `{"tool_call": {...}}` hits the identical allowlist gate `attention.explain_item`/`meeting.prep_summary`'s own injection tests already prove works. What was genuinely missing was proof of that fact for this specific task type, not the mechanism itself. **Round 13 (architecture/quality): clean.** A fresh, thorough read cross-checking every claim in this document against the actual code and every "Covers" list against actual test bodies. Chased one speculative lead (whether `TEST-PLAN.md`'s "size bounds" requirement was left uncovered for email body fetch) and correctly concluded it wasn't a bug at the time -- generically bounded by the shared token-budget mechanism every task type has -- not knowing round 13's own security lens would independently find a *different*, more specific size-bound gap in the same area minutes later (see below). No other new issues found.

**Round 13 (security/correctness): found and fixed 1 real issue -- `email.get_thread_content` was the one deterministic tool in the entire AI runtime with no size bound at all, a direct gap against the design doc's own Decision 6 contract.** `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-23-phase-4-ai-runtime-design.md` Decision 6 states plainly: "every tool result is ... size-bounded (a hard cap on returned rows/characters, e.g. `attention.get_item` returns exactly one item, `knowledge.get_entity` truncates claims/evidence lists to a fixed page size)." Every sibling deterministic tool honors this: `attention/tools.py:_MAX_FACTORS=20`, `knowledge/tools.py:_MAX_CLAIMS=20`/`_MAX_EVIDENCE=20`, `insight_tools.py:_MAX_RECORDS_PER_DOMAIN=50`, `meeting_prep.py:_MAX_EVIDENCE=50` -- `email_action_tools.py:get_thread_content_tool`'s query had no `LIMIT` and no analogous constant, unnoticed by 12 prior review rounds. A long-running thread (a support/notification/reply-all thread -- realistic, and one an external sender fully controls the size of simply by replying into it) would decrypt and render every historically-fetched message every time any new message in that thread became eligible, paid *before* `execute_run`'s own token-budget check runs, and recurring on every future message in that same thread indefinitely (round 3's backlog-draining design guarantees this, not a one-off cost) -- compounding round 2's already-documented "blocks a threadpool worker" risk, since a single message's actual cost is proportional to its thread's entire historical size, not O(1) as previously modeled. Fixed by adding `_MAX_THREAD_MESSAGES = 50` (matching the `_MAX_EVIDENCE`/`_MAX_RECORDS_PER_DOMAIN` precedent) and capping the query to the most recent `_MAX_THREAD_MESSAGES` messages (`ORDER BY sent_at DESC LIMIT`, in a subquery), then re-sorting that capped set back to the same oldest-first order the rest of this tool's output already guarantees -- deliberately not a naive `LIMIT` on the existing `sent_at ASC` query, which would have silently dropped the *newest* messages first on an oversized thread, exactly backwards, since the newest message is normally the one that triggered the call in the first place. Mutation-tested directly against real Postgres (60 messages in one thread, a throwaway workspace, rolled back): the capped, re-sorted query returns exactly 50 rows, the oldest surviving message is message 11 of 60 (not message 1), the newest (message 60, the realistic trigger) is always the last row, and the original unbounded query is confirmed to return all 60 for comparison. New regression test `test_thread_with_more_than_the_cap_returns_only_the_most_recent_messages_in_order` (`tests/test_email_action_tools_postgres.py`) mirrors this exact mutation-tested shape.

The capped-query fix and its exact predicted row counts/ordering independently mutation-tested against real Postgres directly (see above), including the pre-fix behavior for comparison. `scripts/docs_status.py check` / `scripts/check_docs.py` clean.
