**Round 14 (architecture/quality): found and fixed 1 real issue -- migration `0072_phase10_email_detect_action.py`'s own docstring became stale the moment round 13's fix landed.** Item 1 of that migration's docstring described `email.get_thread_content` as reading "every fetched (`body IS NOT NULL`) message in the requested thread" -- true when the migration was originally written, but no longer accurate once round 13 added the `_MAX_THREAD_MESSAGES` cap in the same PR; no round since (round 13 itself, focused entirely on `email_action_tools.py` and its own test file) had circled back to the migration's own docstring, the same kind of drift rounds 4, 5, 8, and 9 have each caught in a different file. Fixed by rewording item 1 to state the tool reads "up to the most recent `_MAX_THREAD_MESSAGES` fetched ... messages," with an inline note crediting round 13 for the cap. Otherwise clean: independently re-verified round 13's own `email_action_tools.py` fix directly against the code (the subquery's `ORDER BY sent_at DESC LIMIT` shape, the outer re-sort back to `sent_at ASC`, the `_MAX_THREAD_MESSAGES = 50` constant matching the `_MAX_EVIDENCE`/`_MAX_RECORDS_PER_DOMAIN` sibling precedent cited), re-verified the new round-13 test's mutation-tested row counts against the actual query, and re-checked test-count arithmetic and every prior round's fix across the full rounds 1-13 history -- all still accurate. No other new design inconsistencies, doc drift, duplication, naming mismatches, or test-coverage gaps found.

**Round 14 (security/correctness): found and fixed 1 real issue -- round 13's own cap can silently exclude the one message that triggered the call, disconnecting a created recommendation's evidence from what the model was actually shown.** `_detect_action_for_message`'s `execute_run("email.detect_action", ..., {"thread_id": str(thread_id)}, ...)` call never told `email.get_thread_content` which message it was being run for. `detect_actions_since`'s own eligibility query (`gmail_adapter.py`) orders `(m.created_at >= :since) DESC, m.sent_at ASC` -- `body IS NULL` is the sole eligibility filter (durable across calls, the round 3 backlog-worked-down-over-time design), but `since` determines which *eligible* messages a given call prioritizes, deliberately favoring an account's freshly-synced messages over older same-thread backlog. On a busy, long-running thread (the same realistic shape round 13's own finding already established an external sender fully controls the size of), an old backlog message can still be waiting its turn long after more than `_MAX_THREAD_MESSAGES` *newer* messages in that same thread have already had their bodies fetched and now occupy every slot round 13's "most recent N" cap allows -- so by the time that old message is finally processed, `email.get_thread_content`'s own capped output would never include it at all. The model would then reason over thread content that never contains the very email the run exists to evaluate; if grounding then passed on some other, visible message in the thread, `_register_message_evidence`'s own recommendation-evidence citation (which always points at the *triggering* message specifically, not whatever the model happened to cite) would end up pointing at a message the model was never actually shown -- a silent grounding/evidence integrity gap, not merely a missed detection. Independently re-verified the mechanism before accepting it: read `detect_actions_since`'s actual `ORDER BY` clause, `_detect_action_for_message`'s actual `execute_run` call (confirmed it passed no message identifier), `_prepare_email_detect_action_request`'s actual `input.get(...)` handling, and `_register_message_evidence`'s own citation logic -- all matched the finding's claims exactly. Fixed by threading the triggering message id through end to end: `_detect_action_for_message` now passes `"message_id": str(message_id)` in the `execute_run` input dict; `_prepare_email_detect_action_request` (`runtime.py`) reads `input.get("message_id")` and, when present, passes it to `_dispatch_tool` as `tool_input["trigger_message_id"]`; `_EmailGetThreadContentInput` gained the new optional `trigger_message_id: UUID | None = None` field; `get_thread_content_tool` (`email_action_tools.py`) gained a `trigger_message_id` parameter and its query was rewritten as a `UNION ALL` of (a) the most recent `_MAX_THREAD_MESSAGES - 1` messages excluding the trigger (only reduced by one, and only when a trigger is given, so a call with no trigger -- the evaluation harness's own existing call site, which has no message-level concept -- is completely unaffected) and (b) the trigger message itself when given, with a single outer `ORDER BY sent_at ASC` so the existing oldest-first reading-order guarantee is preserved regardless of which branch supplied which row. The field is optional throughout specifically so the evaluation harness's own `{"thread_id": ...}`-only call site (`evaluation.py:run_evaluation`, which has no per-message concept at all) needed no change. Mutation-tested directly against real Postgres, twice: a 61-message thread with `trigger_message_id` set to the oldest message confirmed the trigger survives, sorts first, the newest message is always last, and the total is still exactly 50 rows (never 51); a second run with `trigger_message_id=NULL` on the same data confirmed the query still reproduces round 13's original 50-row, message-11-through-60 behavior exactly, unchanged. New regression test `test_trigger_message_id_is_always_included_even_when_older_than_the_cap_window` (`tests/test_email_action_tools_postgres.py`) mirrors this exact mutation-tested shape (60 messages, trigger set to the oldest, asserts 50 total, the trigger first, and the remaining 49 are the most recent 49 excluding the trigger). Migration `0072`'s own docstring (already touched by this round's architecture-lens fix, above) was updated a second time to credit this mechanism as well.

**Full regression evidence (round 14).** Full Postgres-backed suite not run locally this round for the same reason as rounds 9-13 (this container's pinned Python 3.14.6 remains unavailable; CI is the authoritative gate). `python -m py_compile`/`ruff check` clean on all five files this round touched (`backend/ecc/domains/personal/email_action_tools.py`, `backend/ecc/domains/ai_runtime/runtime.py`, `backend/ecc/domains/personal/gmail_adapter.py`, `backend/migrations/versions/0072_phase10_email_detect_action.py`, `tests/test_email_action_tools_postgres.py`). The new `UNION ALL` query's exact predicted row counts, ordering, and trigger-inclusion guarantee independently mutation-tested against real Postgres directly, twice (trigger set and trigger `NULL`; see above). `scripts/docs_status.py check` / `scripts/check_docs.py` clean.

Round 14 found and fixed real issues on both lenses (one doc-drift, architecture/quality; one real security/correctness gap in round 13's own fix), so the loop still does not close -- round 15 needs two fresh, independent, consecutive clean rounds to close.

**CI-discovered correction to round 14's own commit (`df5b085`): `:trigger_message_id::uuid` was never actually substituted by SQLAlchemy, breaking every call to `get_thread_content_tool`.** Round 14's `UNION ALL` query used `(:trigger_message_id::uuid IS NULL OR id <> :trigger_message_id)` to null-check the new parameter before casting it for the `IS NULL` comparison. This was never caught by round 14's own real-Postgres mutation testing because that testing was done by hand-writing the exact SQL with literal values via `psql` (this session's own established substitute-verification workaround for the broken local Python interpreter, documented since round 9) -- which never exercises SQLAlchemy's own `text()` bind-parameter substitution at all. CI's `backend` job, which does run the real application code path through SQLAlchemy, failed on commit `df5b085` with a repeated Postgres `syntax error at or near ":" at character 304`, and the failing `STATEMENT` logged by Postgres showed exactly one occurrence of `:trigger_message_id` -- the one immediately followed by `::uuid` -- left completely unsubstituted in the text sent to the database, while every other occurrence (including the second, bare `:trigger_message_id` two words later in the same line) was correctly replaced with its positional placeholder. Root cause: SQLAlchemy's `text()` bind-parameter regex deliberately excludes a parameter name when it is immediately followed by a second colon, specifically so that Postgres's own `::type` cast syntax can pass through a `text()` string as literal SQL without being mistaken for `:name` followed by another bind reference -- so `:trigger_message_id::uuid` was never recognized as a bind parameter in the first place, not merely mis-substituted. Independently confirmed this exact mechanism (not merely inferred from the CI log) via a standalone script using this project's own actual dialect (`postgresql+psycopg`, `sqlalchemy` 2.0.51, both importable directly without going through the broken `ecc` package import chain): running `SELECT (:trigger_message_id::uuid IS NULL)` through a real `engine.execute()` against real Postgres reproduces the identical `psycopg.errors.SyntaxError: syntax error at or near ":"` on the first try. Fixed by replacing the inline `::uuid` cast with the already-established codebase convention for exactly this situation, `CAST(:trigger_message_id AS uuid)` (the same pattern `search.py`, `knowledge/retrieval.py`, and `governance/recommendation_queries.py` all already use for a bind parameter compared against a `uuid` column) -- confirmed correct through the identical standalone-script method: the same `SELECT (CAST(:trigger_message_id AS uuid) IS NULL)` now returns `True` for `None` and `False` for a real UUID, through the app's real dialect. Beyond the isolated cast expression, the full production query (copy-pasted verbatim from `email_action_tools.py`) was then run end to end through SQLAlchemy against a real, FK-satisfying `workspace`/`account`/`user`/`personal_domains`/`connector_account`/`email_thread`/61-`email_messages` fixture (built and rolled back in the same script, mirroring round 14's own data shape exactly): the trigger-set case again returned exactly 50 rows with `message-0` (the trigger) first and `message-60` last, and the trigger-`NULL` case again returned exactly 50 rows spanning `message-11` through `message-60` -- both outcomes byte-identical to what round 14's `psql`-based mutation test had already established, confirming the *query's logic* was always correct and only its *SQLAlchemy bind-parameter syntax* was broken. This is a durable gap in this session's own substitute-verification strategy going forward, not just a one-off miss: **direct-`psql` mutation testing proves a query's row-level logic but cannot prove its `text()` bind-parameter syntax is valid, since `psql` never goes through SQLAlchemy's own parameter-substitution layer** -- any future `text()` query in this PR (or session) that pairs a bind parameter directly with an inline `::type` cast needs either this end-to-end-through-SQLAlchemy verification method or the `CAST(:x AS type)` form used everywhere else in this codebase, not a `psql`-only check. No test-visible behavior change versus round 14's own intent -- the fix is purely to the SQL syntax, not the logic -- so no new test was needed beyond the existing `test_trigger_message_id_is_always_included_even_when_older_than_the_cap_window`, which will now itself pass under CI's real interpreter (it could never have passed before this fix, since the tool it exercises could not execute at all).

**Round 15 (security/correctness): clean.** A fresh, adversarial read focused in particular on round 14's own new `trigger_message_id` mechanism and its interaction with the surrounding code, per this round's own brief. Manually traced the `UNION ALL` query's SQL semantics (the outer `ORDER BY sent_at ASC` legitimately applies to the whole compound query; `most_recent_limit`'s one-less-when-a-trigger-is-given arithmetic cannot double-count or overflow the cap; every branch stays scoped by `workspace_id`/`owner_id`/`thread_id`/`body IS NOT NULL`, so the new parameter adds no cross-tenant leak) and checked boundary cases (zero non-trigger messages, exactly at the cap, a trigger not present in the thread at all) all degrade correctly. Confirmed `_detect_action_for_message`'s body-fetch `UPDATE` always commits before `execute_run` is called, so the trigger message is guaranteed `body IS NOT NULL` by the time `get_thread_content_tool` runs -- no race that could make the "always included" guarantee silently false. Confirmed the model-requested ("Step 2") tool-call path is unaffected: any `email.get_thread_content` call the model itself requests still always resolves to `fail(...)`, unchanged by round 14. Confirmed `_register_message_evidence` has no positional/index coupling to `messages` -- it identifies the triggering message by `external_message_id`, not by list position, so the trigger's place in the returned ordering is irrelevant to evidence correctness. This review pass, run entirely by static reading rather than execution, is the same reason it could not catch the SQLAlchemy bind-parameter bug documented immediately above -- consistent with that finding's own closing observation that this class of bug needs execution-based verification, not code reading, from either a human reviewer or CI. ## Task 6 evidence

**On-demand thread reading and per-thread "forget this"** (`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-04-phase-10-gmail-connector.md`, Task 6). Per that task's own three-item scope: a `gmail.readonly` fetch for a specific thread on explicit user open, in addition to Task 5's own proactive sync-time fetch; a per-thread "forget this" action extending Phase 7's existing domain-wide deletion granularity to a single thread; and tests for both.

**"Matching Task 5's own fetch-and-store path rather than a second one" required extracting a real helper, not writing a parallel implementation.** `gmail_adapter.py:_detect_action_for_message` (Task 5) had its own body-fetch-and-store logic (the live Gmail `messages.get(format=full)` call, JSON/`RecursionError` handling, `_extract_plain_text_body`, `encrypt_field`, and the `UPDATE ... WHERE body IS NULL` write) fused inline with AI-detection logic that Task 6's own on-demand open must not run (a user opening a thread to read it should not trigger an LLM call or a possible recommendation as a side effect). Extracted the reusable ~110-line block into `GmailAdapter.fetch_and_store_body` (not underscore-prefixed, unlike every other single-caller helper in this file -- the one deliberate exception, since this is now a genuine two-caller method: `_detect_action_for_message` calls it and continues into detection; the new `gmail_threads.py:get_thread_endpoint` calls it and stops there), verified behaviorally unchanged for Task 5's own path by construction (the extraction moved code, it did not rewrite any of the `RecursionError`/empty-string-sentinel logic rounds 4/9 already fixed).

**New module `gmail_threads.py`, registered at the existing `/api/v1/personal/gmail` prefix** (`gmail_oauth.py`'s own prefix, the established Gmail-specific-routes convention -- confirmed via research that no thread/message-reading HTTP endpoint existed anywhere in this codebase before this task, so both new routes are genuinely new surface, not extensions of an existing one):
- `GET /threads/{thread_id}`: workspace/owner-scoped thread lookup (non-disclosing `404`, matching every existing read endpoint's own convention), `_email_consent_active` check (the identical check Task 5's own per-message recheck uses -- `403 EMAIL_CONSENT_NOT_ACTIVE`, not `require_enabled_domain`, since a live Gmail fetch is exactly what `domain_consents` governs and Task 5 never separately checked `personal_domains.enabled` either), fetches every still-`body IS NULL` message in the thread via `fetch_and_store_body` (best-effort per message, matching `detect_actions_since`'s own "one message's failure never stops the rest" discipline), then returns the thread's content via `email_action_tools.get_thread_content_tool` -- the exact same tool `email.detect_action`'s own AI runtime call uses, reused here for the human-facing HTTP surface for the identical "one rendering of what this thread contains" reason Task 6's own fetch-reuse instruction gives for the fetch mechanism. No `trigger_message_id` is passed (that field exists solely for `email.detect_action`'s own single-triggering-message guarantee, which has no analogue for an explicit human thread-open).
- `POST /threads/{thread_id}/forget`: idempotency-key gated exactly like `export_deletion.py:delete_domain_endpoint` (same rationale -- the underlying `UPDATE` is individually idempotent, the `deletion_jobs` audit `INSERT` is not). Nulls `snippet`/`body`/`body_fetched_at` for every message in the targeted thread, back to exactly the state Task 2's own metadata-only sync leaves a never-opened message in.

**A genuine design decision, not resolvable from the plan text alone, made and documented rather than left implicit: "forget this" nulls message content, it does not delete `email_messages` rows or the `email_threads` row itself.** The plan says "deletes the cached body/message content for that thread only" -- read literally, this could mean deleting the `email_messages` rows entirely. Rejected that reading for two concrete reasons, both now stated in `gmail_threads.py`'s own module docstring: (1) `email_messages.external_message_id` is this table's own dedup/idempotency key for future incremental syncs (`uq_email_messages_thread_external_id`) -- deleting the row would make a future sync re-insert it as if new, corrupting the thread's own chronology and re-triggering any create-time side effects; (2) `export_deletion.py`'s own established Phase 7 precedent for exactly this "existence vs. content" tension retains the parent `personal_domains` row (disabled, not deleted) specifically so `deletion_jobs`/`domain_consents`' own FK integrity and a later re-enable both have something to point at -- the identical reasoning applies one level down here: `email_threads` (the existence record) is kept, `deletion_jobs.resource_id` FKs nothing but still needs a stable id to reference, and a "forgotten" thread must remain something Task 6's own `GET` endpoint can still resolve (404 would be wrong -- the thread still exists, its content is merely uncached) and re-fetch on a later open. Verified this reasoning holds via `test_reopening_a_forgotten_thread_refetches_its_content`: a forgotten thread's `GET` re-fetches exactly like a never-opened one.

**Migration `0075_phase10_thread_forget.py` widens `deletion_jobs` for the first sub-domain-granularity deletion this codebase has ever performed.** `deletion_jobs.scope` was `CHECK`-constrained to the single literal `'domain'` since Phase 7 (migration `0054`), and the table had no column identifying *which* resource within a domain a job targeted -- correct for every deletion before this task, all domain-wide. Widened the constraint to `scope IN ('domain', 'thread')` (drop-and-recreate, not a second constraint) and added a nullable `resource_id UUID` column (`NULL` for every existing/future `'domain'`-scope row; only the new thread-forget endpoint populates it). No FK from `resource_id` to `email_threads` -- a `deletion_jobs` row is a permanent audit record of an action that happened, and must not itself be deletable-or-blocking via an FK to a row this task deliberately never deletes. Mutation-tested directly against real Postgres: `scope='thread'` insert succeeds with `resource_id` populated; an invalid `scope` value is still rejected by the widened constraint; full upgrade -> downgrade (constraint and column both revert) -> re-upgrade round-trip verified, matching every prior migration in this PR's own established verification discipline.

**Tests: `tests/test_gmail_threads_postgres.py` (11 tests)**, driven through the real HTTP endpoints via `TestClient` (unlike Task 5's own method-level tests, since Task 6's surface is genuinely new HTTP routes, not an internal method): fetch-and-cache round-trip (a still-unfetched message is fetched, stored encrypted, and returned decrypted); one message's live-fetch failure (a transient `httpx.ConnectError`) does not fail the whole request -- the other, still-fetchable message in the same thread is still returned, and the failed one simply stays `body IS NULL` (Loop 2 round 2's own architecture-lens finding: the fetch loop's `try`/`except` was missing entirely until this round); a disconnected connector account skips the live-fetch attempt entirely, cached content (if any) still renders (Loop 2 round 3's own security-lens finding: unlike `sync_connector_endpoint`, this endpoint had no equivalent to that endpoint's own `409 CONNECTOR_DISCONNECTED` gate before this round) -- proven with a call-spy directly on `fetch_and_store_body`, not a raising transport, since the endpoint's own per-message `except Exception: continue` (the round-2 fix above) would otherwise silently mask a raising transport's exception too, making "skipped" and "attempted, then masked" indistinguishable by response shape alone (Loop 2 round 4's own finding -- the first round-3 version of this test used a raising transport and did not actually prove what its own docstring claimed); a second `GET` on an already-fetched thread makes no Gmail call at all (proven with a transport that raises `AssertionError` if invoked, not merely asserting the response shape); consent-not-active rejection; not-found for both a nonexistent thread and one belonging to a different workspace; forget-removes-only-the-targeted-thread's-content (two threads seeded, one forgotten, the other's content asserted unchanged -- the plan's own explicit "not the whole `email` domain" requirement); exactly one `deletion_jobs` row with the correct `scope`/`resource_id`/`status`; idempotency-key replay inserts no second job (comparing only the cached business fields, not the full response body -- `response_contract_middleware` stamps a fresh `request_id`/`correlation_id` onto every response, including a replayed one); forget-on-nonexistent-thread is `404`; and the reopen-after-forget round-trip described above.

Loop 2 round 2 also found that all four of this file's error-response assertions had checked the wrong JSON shape (`resp.json()["detail"]` instead of the real `resp.json()["error"]["code"]` this app's `response_contract_middleware` actually produces) and that the cross-workspace test's second identity was missing a required `personal_domains` row -- both were masked until round 1's own fixture fix let these tests execute their real bodies in CI for the first time; see "Task 6 -- Loop 2 review evidence" below for the full account.

**Local verification.** `python -m py_compile`/`ruff check` clean on all touched files (`gmail_adapter.py`, `gmail_threads.py` (new), `main.py`, migration `0075`, `test_gmail_threads_postgres.py` (new)). Full Postgres-backed test suite not run locally for the same reason every Task 5 review round documents (this container's pinned Python 3.14.6 remains unavailable at PR-open time -- confirmed the exact same `budgets.py:RunBudget` forward-reference `NameError` reproduces on a plain `import ecc.main`, unrelated to anything this task touches); CI is the authoritative gate. In its place: the migration's own upgrade/downgrade/re-upgrade round-trip and constraint-rejection behavior verified directly against real Postgres (above), and the "forget" endpoint's own core `UPDATE`/`INSERT` logic mutation-tested directly against real Postgres with two threads seeded, confirming the targeted thread's `snippet`/`body`/`body_fetched_at` are nulled while the untouched thread's content and a correctly-scoped single `deletion_jobs` row both survive exactly as `test_forget_thread_removes_only_targeted_threads_content` itself asserts. (By round 2, this sandbox's own Python became 3.14.0rc2 -- close enough to unblock `ruff`/`py_compile`/direct-SQL mutation testing against real Postgres for every round's own fix, but still not full Postgres-backed `pytest` collection, which needs the pinned 3.14.6 final CI itself runs; the real, authoritative pytest failures every round below responds to came from reading CI's own job logs directly, not from a local run.)

### Task 6 -- Loop 2 review evidence

**Round 1 (security/correctness): found and fixed 1 HIGH issue, plus 1 MEDIUM (docstring/code mismatch).** **HIGH**: `GET /threads/{thread_id}`'s eligibility query for still-uncached messages had no `LIMIT` of its own -- unlike Task 5's own proactive path, which `_MAX_ACTION_DETECTIONS_PER_CALL` already bounds for exactly this reason. A thread an external sender can grow arbitrarily long (a reply-all/notification thread) could make one `GET` trigger one sequential live Gmail round trip per unfetched message, blocking the request for minutes. Fixed by bounding the query at `MAX_THREAD_MESSAGES` (renamed from `_MAX_THREAD_MESSAGES` in `email_action_tools.py`, since `gmail_threads.py` is now a genuine second caller) -- fetching more would be wasted work anyway, since the response itself never renders more than that many messages. **MEDIUM**: the module docstring claimed both endpoints check `_email_consent_active`, but `forget_thread_endpoint` never did. Not a live vulnerability (both endpoints stay correctly scoped to the caller's own `workspace_id`/`owner_id` regardless), but a false claim; resolved by correcting the docstring to explain the asymmetry is deliberate -- a deletion action should stay available even without an active consent grant.

**Round 1 (architecture/quality): found and fixed 1 issue (doc staleness across three sibling docs).** `docs/phases/PHASE-010-gmail-connector.md`, `docs/phases/README.md` (via `docs/phases/status.json`, the generated-status source of truth), and `phase-010/DATA-MODEL.md` had all been missed by Task 6's own initial doc pass -- still saying "Tasks 1-5 complete"/"Tasks 6-8 open" after Task 6 shipped. Fixed all three (`status.json` first, then regenerated `README.md`/`docs/ROADMAP.md`/`docs/phases/README.md` via `scripts/docs_status.py render`), the identical "sibling doc drifted stale after a task shipped" defect class Task 5's own round 16 already found and fixed once.

**Separately, this same push also fixed a real CI-blocking bug** (found by directly reading CI's own job logs, not by either review lens): `tests/test_gmail_threads_postgres.py`'s fixture created a global `accounts` row with a hardcoded literal email on every test, but `_cleanup_workspace` never deleted it (`accounts` is not workspace-scoped) -- the first test passed, every test after it failed setup with a duplicate-key `IntegrityError`. Fixed by adding the same `DELETE FROM accounts WHERE email = ...` step the established sibling pattern (`test_gmail_action_detection_sync_postgres.py`'s own `_teardown_workspace`) already uses.

Round 1 found real issues on both lenses (plus the CI-blocking bug above), so the loop does not close here -- round 2 needs two fresh, independent, consecutive clean rounds to close.

**Round 2: the round-1 fixture fix let this file's tests execute their own bodies in CI for the first time ever -- surfacing 3 further latent test bugs, all previously masked.** Every one of this file's 9 (at the time) tests had failed at fixture setup before its own body ever ran, so none of their actual assertions had ever been exercised against real CI. With setup now succeeding: (1) four tests asserted `resp.json()["detail"]` for error responses, but this app's `response_contract_middleware` (`main.py`) reshapes every 4xx/5xx body into `{"error": {"code": ..., "message": ..., "request_id": ..., "details": {...}}, "correlation_id": ...}`, not a bare `{"detail": ...}` -- fixed to check `resp.json()["error"]["code"]`, matching the pattern already used correctly in `test_gmail_connector_postgres.py`. (2) `test_get_thread_belonging_to_a_different_workspace_is_404` never gave its second identity a `personal_domains` row before inserting a thread for it, violating `email_threads`' own composite FK -- fixed. (3) `test_forget_thread_idempotency_replay_does_not_insert_a_second_job` compared full response bodies, but the middleware stamps a fresh `request_id`/`correlation_id` onto every response including cached replays, so two calls' bodies never matched exactly -- fixed to compare only the specific business fields, matching `test_collaboration_delegations_postgres.py`'s own established idempotency-replay pattern.

**Round 2 (architecture/quality): found and fixed 1 real gap, independent of the CI investigation above.** `get_thread_endpoint`'s fetch loop had no per-message `try`/`except`, despite its own comment claiming to mirror `detect_actions_since`'s "one message's failure never stops the rest" discipline -- a genuine transient `httpx` error fetching one message would 500 the whole request, including content already fetched for every other message in the thread. Fixed to match the established sibling pattern exactly, closed with a new regression test (`test_get_thread_one_message_fetch_failure_does_not_fail_the_request`, using a new `_gmail_transport_with_one_failure` helper). Also corrected an `API-SCHEMAS.md` overclaim that the `GET` response includes "participants"/"snippets" (it doesn't -- sender only, no snippet field).

Round 2 found and fixed real issues (a CI-breaking bug and a genuine production-code gap), so the loop still does not close -- round 3 needs two fresh, independent, consecutive clean rounds to close.

**Round 3: both lenses independently caught the same doc staleness** -- `IMPLEMENTATION-STATUS.md` and `TEST-PLAN.md` still said "9 tests" after round 2 added a 10th. Fixed both.

**Round 3 (security/correctness): found and fixed 1 real gap.** `GET /threads/{thread_id}` never checked `connector_accounts.status` before decrypting the stored credential and attempting a live Gmail call, unlike the established sibling pattern (`sync_connector_endpoint`'s own `409 CONNECTOR_DISCONNECTED` gate in `connector_accounts.py`, which checks status before ever touching the credential). Fixed by widening `_thread_connector_account_id` into `_thread_connector_account` (now also returns `status`) and skipping the live-fetch loop entirely when disconnected -- without failing the whole request, since a disconnected connector should still let an owner read a thread's already-cached content. Closed with a new regression test.

Round 3 found and fixed real issues on both lenses, so the loop still does not close -- round 4 needs two fresh, independent, consecutive clean rounds to close.

**Round 4 (security/correctness): found and fixed 1 issue -- the round-3 test didn't actually prove its own claim.** `test_get_thread_with_disconnected_connector_skips_live_fetch` used a raising transport to prove "no Gmail call," but round 2's own `except Exception: continue` (added for a different, legitimate reason) would silently swallow that transport's `AssertionError` too -- making "skipped" and "attempted, then masked" indistinguishable by response shape alone. A regression that quietly reintroduced round 3's own bug would have passed this test undetected. Fixed by replacing the raising-transport approach with a call-spy directly on `fetch_and_store_body`, upstream of the endpoint's own per-message exception handling.

**Round 4 (architecture/quality): found and fixed 2 doc-coherence issues, no code issues.** This document's own Task 6 evidence had accumulated two dangling `"see below"`/`"see the Loop 2 review evidence section"` cross-references pointing at a subsection that (unlike Task 4's and Task 5's own) did not yet exist -- fixed by adding this subsection. Separately, `tests/test_gmail_threads_postgres.py`'s own numbered "Covers" docstring had drifted out of sync with the file's actual physical test order (rounds 2 and 3's new tests were inserted near the top, not appended where their list numbers implied) -- the identical "Covers"-list-vs-physical-position drift this same document already diagnosed once for a sibling file (Task 3's own round 7, `test_attention_email_awaiting_reply_postgres.py`) -- fixed by renumbering the list to match physical order.

**Round 12 (decisive round): clean on both lenses.** Security/correctness re-derived tenancy scoping, SQL parameterization, credential handling, the consent-check asymmetry, the disconnected-connector skip, per-message exception handling, idempotency-key hashing, and migration `0075` from scratch, additionally verifying the migration's applied effect directly against a live Postgres instance (`alembic heads` resolves to a single head; `\d deletion_jobs` matches the migration exactly). Architecture/quality re-verified every doc cross-reference across all 15 touched/related docs, confirmed the `MAX_THREAD_MESSAGES` rename is complete everywhere except the two deliberately-preserved historical references, confirmed the "Covers" list still matches physical test order, and explicitly re-verified every fix from rounds 1-11 against current file state to rule out regression -- none had regressed. No findings on either lens.

**Loop 2 closes as of round 12: rounds 11 and 12 are two consecutive rounds, both clean on both lenses.** Twelve rounds total ran on this PR; production-code bugs were found and fixed in rounds 1-4, documentation-only issues in rounds 5-10, and rounds 11-12 were clean, closing the loop per the governing rule.

## Task 7 evidence

**Consent revocation cascade** (`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-04-phase-10-gmail-connector.md`, Task 7: "Revoking the `email` domain's `domain_consents` row calls `GmailAdapter.disconnect()` ... and runs Phase 7's existing deletion-job pipeline ... -- one action, both effects, no partial 'disconnected but data remains' state reachable through this path").

**Why `email` disabling/revoking now purges data, unlike every other domain -- a deliberate, disclosed asymmetry.** For every other domain, `domains.py:_disable_domain` only flips `personal_domains.enabled` and closes the open `domain_consents` row; the underlying records stay intact, and a separate, explicit `POST .../delete` (`export_deletion.py`) is required to purge anything. `email`'s data is a synced mirror of a live, externally-connected Google account, not a self-reported record -- leaving a full inbox mirror and an active OAuth grant behind after "disabling" the domain would defeat consent revocation's entire purpose for this domain specifically. `PRIVACY-CONSENT-CONTRACT.md`'s own "Unsupported" section had named exactly this gap as a production blocker since Task 1.

**New module `gmail_revocation.py`, called from both existing write paths, not a new endpoint.** `cascade_email_revocation(session, auth, now)` runs entirely inside the caller's own already-open transaction: purges `attention_items` (`entity_type='email_thread'`, scoped by the real `owner_id` column `attention.py`'s own email-thread branch sets explicitly), redacts-in-place-or-deletes `email_action_detected` recommendations (see below), purges the owner's own `gmail_sync`-sourced `pkos_evidence` (except a row whose id collides with a different owner's own message, left unpurged -- see "What is deliberately NOT deleted" below), deletes `email_messages` then `email_threads`, and marks every one of the owner's `gmail` connector account(s) `disconnected` -- returning pending Google-side revoke info for the caller to pass to `finish_gmail_revocation` only *after* that transaction commits and the session's connection is released, matching `connector_accounts.py:disable_connector_endpoint`'s own established "decrypt inside the transaction, network call after" split (that endpoint's own round-23 review comment explains why: a blocking outbound HTTPS call must never run while holding a DB connection/lock). Called from `domains.py:_disable_domain` (the single write path both `disable_domain_endpoint` and `revoke_consent_endpoint` already funnel through, per this module's own "one write path per transition" convention) when `domain_key == "email"` and a real enabled -> disabled transition is happening, and from `export_deletion.py:_delete_domain_data` when `domain_key == "email"` -- every code path that disables or deletes the `email` domain reaches the identical cascade.

**A genuine design decision on each of three tables whose own `owner_id` column (Phase 8's migration `0063` authz widening) turned out not to be trustworthy for scoping, discovered by reading each write site rather than assumed from the column's mere presence.** `attention_items.owner_id` is set explicitly at write time from the underlying entity's own owner and is reliable; `recommendations.owner_id` is backfilled/defaulted from `created_by`, and Task 5's own `email.detect_action` always creates under the run's own owner-scoped `AuthContext`, so it is reliable too. `pkos_evidence.owner_id`, by contrast, is *never* set at write time (`gmail_adapter.py`'s own two `INSERT INTO pkos_evidence` sites list no `owner_id` column at all) and so falls back to migration `0063`'s generic "workspace's earliest-created user" default -- unrelated to which owner's Gmail sync actually produced a given evidence row. Scoping `pkos_evidence` deletion by that column would have been silently wrong in any multi-owner workspace; instead, `external_message_id`s are captured (before `email_messages` is deleted) and matched against the exact `source_ref` strings `gmail_adapter.py`'s two write sites deterministically construct (`f"gmail:{external_message_id}"`, `f"gmail:detect_action:{external_message_id}"`).

**What is deliberately NOT deleted, and why.** `pkos_nodes` (the resolved person entities) survive: `_resolve_or_create_person` deduplicates at *workspace*, not owner, scope, so a node this owner's Gmail sync first created may be the same node a different owner's sync, or an entirely different domain's entity resolution, has since reused -- deleting it here could destroy state another owner or domain still depends on; only the `gmail_sync`-sourced evidence pointing at it is removed, leaving (if orphaned) a bare `canonical_name` with no content and no privacy exposure of its own. `recommendations` already `status = 'executed'` are not deleted either: Task 4's own create-path confirms these into a real, independent `tasks`/`commitments`/`risks` row via the same internal creation function every other caller uses, so that downstream row is the owner's own work product, not Gmail data. The recommendation row itself is instead redacted in place -- `rationale`/`proposed_action`/`evidence_ids` nulled -- matching `gmail_threads.py:forget_thread_endpoint`'s own "null the content, keep the audit skeleton" convention exactly; the fact that a recommendation once existed, was confirmed, and led to that task remains visible, only the Gmail-derived narrative behind it does not. Every other `email_action_detected` recommendation (not yet `executed`) is deleted outright, since it represents unactioned Gmail-derived content with no independent value.

**No new migration -- an explicit, reasoned decision, not an oversight.** `TEST-PLAN.md`'s "Required Task 7-8" bullet named "retry, partial failure" as needed evidence, and `export_deletion.py`'s own module docstring predicted this task as the natural point to introduce a real `pending -> worker -> completed` `deletion_jobs` lifecycle. On inspection that machinery was not actually needed: every write in the cascade runs inside the *same* transaction `_disable_domain`/`_delete_domain_data` already opens, so a failure at any point rolls back everything written so far -- there is no reachable partially-purged state, and no `deletion_jobs` row is ever written for a failed attempt. The existing idempotency-key guard in each caller means a client retry with the same `Idempotency-Key` simply re-attempts the whole cascade from scratch, since every `DELETE`/`UPDATE` in it is independently idempotent. `deletion_jobs.status` therefore still only needs `'pending'`/`'completed'` (migration `0054`'s original shape) -- building new `'failed'`/`'retrying'` states now, with nothing that would ever populate them for longer than one transaction, would be exactly the kind of premature machinery this codebase's own precedent (this same docstring) warns against.

**Tests: `tests/test_gmail_revocation_postgres.py` (13 tests as of Loop 2 round 5) plus 2 tests in `tests/test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py`**, driven through the real HTTP endpoints via `TestClient`: disabling `email` disconnects the connector and purges the owner's own threads/messages/attention-items/evidence (subject to the ambiguous-id evidence carve-out below) and non-`executed` recommendations in one request; an already-`executed` recommendation is redacted in place, not deleted, with its confirmed `target_id` verified still present; `pkos_nodes` survive disable while their `pkos_evidence` is removed; `revoke_consent_endpoint` and `delete_domain_endpoint` both reach the identical cascade (verified by seeding the same fixture and asserting the same purge, plus `delete_domain_endpoint`'s own `deletion_jobs` row with `scope='domain'`/`status='completed'`); disabling an already-disabled domain does not re-run the cascade (a thread inserted between two disable calls survives the second call); a fresh domain-disable's own idempotency-key replay does not re-run the cascade either (the identical proof, via a replayed request instead of a second fresh one); disabling an unrelated domain (`habits`) leaves the `email` connector and its data completely untouched. Rounds 1-5 added: an owner with two connector accounts has both disconnected; both call sites that reach `finish_gmail_revocation` release the pool connection/row lock before the blocking Google revoke call; a second owner's own Gmail data is untouched; two owners' colliding `external_message_id`s do not leak `pkos_evidence` across the ownership boundary, while a non-colliding id for the same owner is still purged normally; the generic engineering disable endpoint rejects an active `gmail` account but stays an idempotent no-op for an already-disconnected one.

**Local verification.** `python -m py_compile`/`ruff check` clean on all touched files (`gmail_revocation.py` (new), `domains.py`, `export_deletion.py`, `test_gmail_revocation_postgres.py` (new)). Full Postgres-backed test suite not run locally for the same reason every prior task's own review rounds document (this container's pinned Python 3.14.6 remains unavailable at PR-open time; confirmed the identical `fastapi`/`pydantic` `_typing_extra.eval_type_backport` `AssertionError` reproduces on a plain `import ecc.auth` alone, since `AuthContext` itself pulls in FastAPI -- unrelated to anything this task touches, blocking even a standalone-script bypass of the full app import this session's prior tasks sometimes used). Every SQL statement in `gmail_revocation.py` was instead verified by hand against the actual current schema (each table's real migration, not assumed column shapes) before being written, including the Phase 8 `owner_id`-reliability investigation described above; CI is the authoritative gate for runtime behavior.

**Task 7 -- Loop 2 review evidence**

**Round 1 (security/correctness and architecture/quality, launched concurrently): two CRITICAL issues found and fixed, corroborated independently by both lenses.** First (both lenses independently): `connector_accounts` has no per-owner uniqueness on `provider = 'gmail'` (only `uq_connector_accounts_workspace_provider_external_id`, scoped to `external_account_id`, migration `0044`), and `gmail_oauth.py`'s OAuth callback places no restriction on a second, distinct Google account for the same owner -- an owner who connected two Gmail accounts has two simultaneously non-`disconnected` `connector_accounts` rows. `cascade_email_revocation`'s original `SELECT ... .one_or_none()` raised `MultipleResultsFound` in that case, uncaught, aborting the *entire* cascade transaction (a 500, no purge, domain never disabled) for any such owner. Fixed by fetching every matching row (`.all()`, not `.one_or_none()`) and disconnecting each independently, matching the task's own "connector_accounts row(**s**)" (plural) language; a `FOR UPDATE` clause was added to the same `SELECT` at the same time (round 1 architecture finding: the docstring claimed to mirror `connector_accounts.py:get_connector_account(..., for_update=True)`'s locking "exactly" but the lock itself was missing). `PendingGmailRevoke` return types widened from `| None` to `list[...]` throughout (`gmail_revocation.py`, `domains.py`, `export_deletion.py`); `finish_gmail_revocation` now iterates and revokes every entry independently (one failing does not block the rest). New regression test `test_disable_domain_with_multiple_connector_accounts_disconnects_all` seeds a second connector row and asserts both are disconnected.

Second (architecture lens): a genuine third, cascade-bypassing write path existed. `connector_accounts.py`'s generic, provider-agnostic `POST /connectors/{account_id}/disable` could disconnect a `gmail`-provider row -- including a real best-effort Google `/revoke` call -- without ever touching `personal_domains`/`domain_consents` or purging any Gmail-derived data, since that endpoint has no per-provider special-casing at all. This was exactly the "disconnected but data (and domain consent) remains" state the module's own docstring and `PRIVACY-CONSENT-CONTRACT.md` claimed was unreachable "through this path" -- true of the path Task 7 built, false of a pre-existing sibling path neither the plan nor the original implementation considered. Fixed by rejecting `gmail`-provider disables at that endpoint outright (`409 GMAIL_DISABLE_REQUIRES_DOMAIN_ENDPOINT`), directing callers to the domain-level endpoints that actually reach the cascade -- restoring the "one real write path per state transition" property this module has claimed since it was written. New test `test_disable_rejects_gmail_provider_account` (`test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py`) asserts the rejection and that the account row is left untouched.

Security lens additionally flagged (not independently fixed this round): tests exercised a real, unmocked outbound HTTPS call to Google's live `/revoke` endpoint via `finish_gmail_revocation`, unlike `test_gmail_threads_postgres.py`'s own established `monkeypatch.setattr(module, "_adapter", GmailAdapter(transport=...))` pattern -- fixed by installing a shared `httpx.MockTransport` once in the `gmail_revocation_context` fixture (every test in this file needs identical revoke-success behavior, unlike `test_gmail_threads_postgres.py`'s per-test varying transports, so one shared fixture-level patch was the right scope rather than seven duplicated per-test ones). Security lens also flagged the pre-existing, unlogged `except Exception: pass` around credential decryption as lacking observability -- reviewed and left as-is: this codebase's domain layer has no logging framework in use anywhere (`grep` for `logging`/`getLogger` across `ecc/domains/personal/` and `ecc/domains/engineering/connector_accounts.py` returns nothing), and the identical unlogged swallow already exists in `connector_accounts.py:disable_connector_endpoint`, which this module's own docstring explicitly says it matches "exactly" -- adding logging only here would be inconsistent with an established, codebase-wide convention, not a fix specific to this task.

**Round 2 (security/correctness and architecture/quality, launched concurrently): round 1's fixes verified correct; three new MEDIUM/LOW issues found and fixed, one left as an accepted low-risk gap.** Both lenses independently re-verified round 1's fixes as correct and complete (multi-row `FOR UPDATE`/`.all()` loop, `PendingGmailRevoke` list-widening threaded consistently through `gmail_revocation.py`/`domains.py`/`export_deletion.py`, the `409` gmail-provider guard, the mocked-transport fixture) before looking for new issues, and both independently converged on the same regression: the round-1 `409` guard in `disable_connector_endpoint` was unconditional, silently breaking every other provider's own established contract that disabling an already-`disconnected` connector is an idempotent `200` no-op -- an already-disconnected `gmail` row has nothing left to bypass, so rejecting it too was an unintended, untested behavior change. Fixed by gating the guard on `account.status != "disconnected"` (moved inside that existing conditional, immediately before the adapter lookup) -- an already-disconnected gmail account now correctly falls through to the same no-op path every other provider uses. New test `test_disable_already_disconnected_gmail_account_is_still_an_idempotent_noop` asserts the restored no-op.

Security lens additionally found (MEDIUM): the round-1 `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` over potentially-multiple `connector_accounts` rows had no deterministic order, risking a lock-order deadlock between two genuinely concurrent cascades for the same multi-account owner (e.g. a racing `disable_domain_endpoint` and `revoke_consent_endpoint` call) -- confirmed the loser would surface as an unhandled 500 rather than silently serializing, though no data-integrity risk (the connector-row lock itself still prevents a duplicate real Google revoke). Fixed with `ORDER BY id` on that `SELECT`, guaranteeing every concurrent caller acquires the same locks in the same sequence.

Architecture lens additionally found (MEDIUM): `TEST-PLAN.md`'s "Current automated evidence" table for Task 7 had not been updated for round 1's new coverage, unlike `IMPLEMENTATION-STATUS.md`/`PRIVACY-CONSENT-CONTRACT.md` -- in particular it didn't mention `test_engineering_connectors_postgres.py` at all, a cross-domain test file added for this Phase 10 task. Fixed by updating the Task 7 row and adding a changelog entry. Architecture lens also flagged (MEDIUM) that the round-1 error detail `GMAIL_MUST_DISABLE_VIA_DOMAIN_ENDPOINT` mixed two of this codebase's own naming conventions (every other `..._MUST_...`-phrased detail in this codebase pairs with `422`, while `409`-coded details are noun/state descriptions, not imperative phrasing) -- renamed to `GMAIL_DISABLE_REQUIRES_DOMAIN_ENDPOINT` across code, tests, and docs to match the `409` noun-description convention.

Left as an accepted, disclosed low-risk gap (security lens, LOW): an idempotency-cache replay of a *pre-fix* successful gmail disable (via `disable_connector_endpoint`, cache lifetime 365 days) would return the old cached `200` rather than the new `409` for that one specific stale key. Not exploitable by a new request (no way to mint such a cached success post-fix), and Gmail remains explicitly "internal-development only" per `PRIVACY-CONSENT-CONTRACT.md`'s own "Unsupported" section with no production deployment to have produced such a cached record -- noted rather than engineered around.

**Round 3 (security/correctness and architecture/quality, launched concurrently): a clean round -- no new findings on either lens.** Both lenses re-verified rounds 1-2's fixes as correct before doing a genuinely fresh pass (per-lens, targeted at classes of bug already proven real in this PR -- e.g. re-checking every `.json()["error"]["code"]`/`["status"]`/`["detail"]` assertion in both touched test files for the same response-shape-mismatch defect class CI had just caught once). Neither lens found anything new.

**Round 4 (security/correctness and architecture/quality, launched concurrently, explicitly instructed to bring genuinely fresh scrutiny rather than confirm round 3): one new MEDIUM finding per lens, both fixed.** Architecture lens found a real stale doc claim invalidated by round 1's own fix: `API-SCHEMAS.md`'s "Current reused connector endpoints" table still described `POST .../connectors/{id}/disable` as unconditionally revoking-and-disconnecting, contradicting this PR's own round-1 change for `gmail` specifically (and self-contradicting within the same file, since that file's own "Consent revocation cascade" section two paragraphs below already assumed the reader knew disable no longer did that for `gmail`) -- fixed, with a changelog entry. Architecture lens also found two structural test-coverage gaps: no test proved the pool-connection/row-lock release ordering (`session.close()` before the blocking Google revoke call) for the two call sites that actually reach `finish_gmail_revocation` for `gmail` (`_disable_domain`/`delete_domain_endpoint`) -- unlike `disable_connector_endpoint`'s own dedicated concurrency test (round 23's own fake-and-`ThreadPoolExecutor` pattern) -- and no test seeded a second owner to prove cross-owner isolation. Both closed with new tests (`test_disable_domain_releases_pool_connection_and_row_lock_before_revoke_call`, `test_disable_domain_does_not_affect_a_different_owners_gmail_data`).

Security lens found a genuine, if low-probability, architectural gap: `email_messages.external_message_id` is only uniqueness-constrained per `(workspace_id, thread_id)` (migration `0069`), not per-workspace -- nothing in the schema rules out two different owners' own connected Gmail accounts each producing a message that happens to share the same raw id. `pkos_evidence` purge matches by `source_ref` string alone, with no owner qualifier (unlike every other statement in `cascade_email_revocation`), so such a collision would silently purge a different owner's evidence too. Fixed in `gmail_revocation.py`: before deleting, any candidate id that also belongs to another owner's own `email_messages` row in the same workspace is excluded from the purge -- an ambiguous id is left alone rather than risk deleting across the ownership boundary (a strictly safer failure mode: one row not purged, versus purging the wrong owner's data). New test `test_disable_domain_does_not_purge_evidence_with_a_colliding_external_message_id` proves it. (In practice this collision requires two independent real Google accounts to be assigned the identical opaque message id by Google's own infrastructure -- not something any workspace member can engineer deliberately, since neither their own nor another owner's Gmail-assigned ids are caller-controlled -- but the fix costs one query and closes the gap regardless of how unlikely.)

**Round 5 (security/correctness and architecture/quality, launched concurrently, explicitly instructed to bring fresh scrutiny rather than confirm round 3's earlier clean pass): findings on both lenses, all fixed.** Architecture lens found the same class of doc-staleness issue round 4 itself had just fixed once (in `API-SCHEMAS.md`) was still present elsewhere: `PRIVACY-CONSENT-CONTRACT.md` and `DATA-MODEL.md` both still claimed "every Gmail-sourced `pkos_evidence` row" / "every derived ... row" is purged, overclaiming completeness against round 4's own deliberate ambiguous-id carve-out -- `DATA-MODEL.md` in particular had never been revisited across any of the five review rounds despite the cascade's actual behavior changing twice since its one edit. Both corrected, along with the identical overclaim in two in-code docstrings (`domains.py:_disable_domain`, `export_deletion.py`'s own inline comment).

Security lens found two test-coverage gaps in round 4's own new tests: `test_disable_domain_does_not_purge_evidence_with_a_colliding_external_message_id` only ever seeded one message, so it exercised the "every candidate id is ambiguous" branch and never proved the mixed case (`safe_ids` still purging normally alongside a non-empty `ambiguous_ids`) -- a future refactor collapsing that branch structure would have passed unnoticed. Fixed by seeding a second, non-colliding message/evidence pair for the same owner and asserting it is still purged. Second, the round-4 concurrency test only covered `disable_domain_endpoint`; `delete_domain_endpoint`'s own identical `session.close()`-before-blocking-revoke sequence (the OTHER call site that ever reaches `finish_gmail_revocation` for `gmail`) had no equivalent proof, despite the shared test fake's own docstring claiming both were covered. Fixed with a mirrored `test_delete_domain_releases_pool_connection_and_row_lock_before_revoke_call`.

Both new round-5 cross-owner tests (and one round-4 test) initially failed CI with `ForeignKeyViolation` on `email_threads_workspace_id_owner_id_domain_key_fkey`: `email_threads` has a real FK to `personal_domains(workspace_id, owner_id, domain_key)` (migration `0069`), and the second owner seeded in each test had no such row. Fixed with a new `_insert_enabled_email_domain` test helper, used before inserting any second owner's own thread.

**Round 6 (security/correctness and architecture/quality, launched concurrently): security lens clean; architecture lens found the same doc-staleness class a third time, now in the two remaining docs no round had touched, plus a stale test count and a docstring ordering mismatch -- all fixed, plus a real CI-caught bug in round 5's own new tests.** Security lens did a genuinely thorough pass (hand-traced every SQL statement in the new round-4/5 tests, the `ambiguous_ids`/`safe_ids` filtering logic, and the interaction between the round-1 multi-connector-account fix and the round-4 evidence-collision fix) and found nothing new.

Architecture lens found the "every ... row" overclaim pattern a third time: `SYNC-CONTRACT.md` (the one Task-7-touched doc no round had ever revisited) and a second, separate sentence in `PRIVACY-CONSENT-CONTRACT.md` that round 5's fix had missed two sentences earlier in the same paragraph. Also found `API-SCHEMAS.md`'s `disable` table row never disclosed round 2's already-disconnected idempotent-no-op carve-out (unconditionally claimed rejection), and a second overclaim nearby in the same file's cascade description. All four fixed. Also found this document's own Task 7 test-count claim was stale ("8 tests", actual 13 as of round 5) and that the test file's own "Covers" docstring, which explicitly claims to list items "in the same order these tests physically appear below," had items 11-13 transposed relative to actual physical order (round 5's new delete-domain concurrency test was inserted physically right after round 4's disable-domain one without reordering the docstring). Both fixed.

CI on the round-5 push then caught a real bug in round 5's own new cross-owner tests: both failed with `RestrictViolation` on `fk_users_account` -- the tests tried to delete a second owner's `accounts` row inline, before the fixture's own teardown had deleted that owner's `users` row (which `RESTRICT`-references `accounts`). Fixed by widening `_cleanup_workspace` to accept a list of emails and having tests register their extra owner's email via a new `ctx["extra_owner_emails"]` list instead of deleting inline, so cleanup order is always: workspace-scoped `users` first (existing loop), then `accounts` for every registered email.

**Round 7 (security/correctness and architecture/quality, launched concurrently): security lens clean after a thorough hand-trace (plus one low-severity test-hygiene gap it flagged, fixed); architecture lens ran an exhaustive sweep for the recurring doc-overclaim pattern across all six docs and every touched docstring, finding four more instances -- all fixed.**

Security lens traced the `_cleanup_workspace(emails=...)` fix end-to-end (correct order, both call sites correctly register their email), re-verified all 13 test functions' SQL and response-shape assertions, and traced the `idempotency_records` table's `(workspace_id, actor_id, key)` + `request_hash` collision-safety design against the round-2 gmail-guard placement -- confirmed fail-closed (`409 IDEMPOTENCY_CONFLICT` on a hash mismatch, never a wrong cached body) and unaffected. It did flag one low-severity test-hygiene gap: the two cross-owner tests only registered their second identity's email for cleanup as the LAST statement in the test body, so an earlier assertion failure would skip registration and leave an orphaned `accounts` row. Fixed by registering each email immediately after it's generated, before any assertion can short-circuit the test.

Architecture lens's exhaustive sweep (grepping all six docs plus every docstring in `gmail_revocation.py`/`domains.py`/`export_deletion.py`/`connector_accounts.py` for totalizing language near delete/purge/disconnect/reject) found: `gmail_revocation.py`'s own `cascade_email_revocation` function docstring was the one place in that file rounds 4-6 never revisited, still claiming to purge "every email-derived record" (the module docstring four lines above it already discloses the carve-out, but the function's own summary didn't); `IMPLEMENTATION-STATUS.md`'s top summary-table row for this feature was unedited since the original round-1 commit (confirmed via `git log -p`) and still read "Loop 2 review round 2 in progress" -- five rounds stale, and omitted both the ambiguous-id evidence carve-out and the round-2 idempotent-no-op carve-out; two more sentences in this same document's own "Task 7 evidence" prose (also unedited since the original commit) had the identical unqualified "purges ... every" phrasing. All four corrected. Also confirmed clean: `PRIVACY-CONSENT-CONTRACT.md`, `API-SCHEMAS.md`, `DATA-MODEL.md`, `SYNC-CONTRACT.md`, `TEST-PLAN.md`, and every other docstring -- no fifth instance found. Separately, architecture lens flagged the round-6 `_cleanup_workspace` fix's `emails: list[str] | None = None` fallback as dead code (the function's one call site always passes `emails=` explicitly) -- simplified to a required `emails: list[str]` parameter.

**Round 8 (security/correctness and architecture/quality, launched concurrently, architecture explicitly told not to re-sweep the doc-overclaim pattern round 7 had just closed): one architecture finding, two new security findings, all fixed.** Architecture lens found one instance of a related-but-distinct pattern: `gmail_revocation.py`'s module docstring (round 1's own "an owner can hold more than one `gmail` connector account" paragraph) and an inline comment immediately above the `account_rows` `SELECT` (the `.all()`, not `.one_or_none()` comment) restated the identical multi-connector-account narrative in full a second time. Trimmed the inline comment to a cross-reference back to the module docstring.

Security lens found two new, genuine MEDIUM issues, both unrelated to documentation. First: the round-4 cross-owner ambiguity check only ever defended against a *concurrent* collision (both owners' `email_messages` rows still live at cascade time) -- not a *sequential* one. Owner A disables first: A's own check correctly sees owner B's still-live colliding row and leaves the shared `pkos_evidence` row alone, but A's own `email_messages` row for that id is still deleted (correctly -- it's A's own data), erasing the only signal that made the id ambiguous. Owner B disables independently afterwards: B's own check now finds no other owner's live row left and wrongly concludes the id is safe, purging the shared evidence row B's own ambiguity check should have preserved -- destroying data A's own earlier cascade had specifically protected. Fixed with a new table, `email_message_id_purge_log` (migration `0076`): every cascade logs every `external_message_id` it processes, unconditionally, before that owner's own `email_messages` rows are deleted; the ambiguity check now additionally treats an id as ambiguous if a *different* owner has a log entry for it, not only a still-live row. New test `test_sequential_disables_by_two_colliding_owners_do_not_leak_the_first_owners_evidence` reproduces the exact sequence above (owner A disables via the HTTP endpoint, owner B's cascade is then invoked directly) and proves the shared evidence row survives both.

Second: `domains.py:revoke_consent_endpoint`'s own `domain_consents` lookup did not filter `revoked_at IS NULL`, so a stale/already-revoked `consent_id` -- a replayed request, a double-submitted form, or a client retry landing after the owner already re-granted `email` consent and resynced Gmail -- would still resolve to `domain_key = "email"` and trigger a fresh disable-and-purge cascade against the *current*, freshly re-granted state, using an identifier for a grant that no longer exists. Harmless for every other domain (disable/re-enable is fully reversible); newly destructive for `email` specifically, since Task 7 made disabling it cascade. Fixed by requiring `revoked_at IS NULL` in that lookup -- a stale id now gets the same non-disclosing `404 CONSENT_NOT_FOUND` every other unresolved lookup in this module already returns. New test `test_revoke_consent_endpoint_rejects_a_stale_already_revoked_consent_id` revokes once, re-grants consent, seeds fresh cascade data under the new grant, replays the original (now-stale) `consent_id`, and asserts both the `404` and that the freshly re-granted data survives untouched.

This is the first migration Task 7 has ever needed (`0076_phase10_email_id_purge_log.py`) -- `DATA-MODEL.md`'s and this document's own prior "no new migration needed" claims are now stale in that one respect and have been corrected alongside the rest of this round's doc updates (`PRIVACY-CONSENT-CONTRACT.md`, `API-SCHEMAS.md`, `DATA-MODEL.md`, `TEST-PLAN.md`).

**Round 9 (security/correctness and architecture/quality, launched concurrently): one finding per lens, both fixed -- one a genuine regression round 8's own fix introduced.** Architecture lens re-verified round 8's ledger fix (atomicity, `unnest` parameterization, index coverage, UNION-query structure) and the new tests as correct, then found two accuracy issues in round-8's own new prose: migration `0076`'s docstring claimed an "owner-removal... cascade" as an existing removal path for `email_message_id_purge_log`, when no such mechanism exists (member removal never hard-deletes a `users` row, only flips `workspace_memberships.status`) and the migration's own DDL gives `owner_id` no FK at all -- contradicting `DATA-MODEL.md`'s own, more careful round-8 wording ("a `workspace_id` `ON DELETE CASCADE` FK is its only removal path") written in the same pass. Fixed by aligning the migration docstring with that accurate claim. Separately, the new sequential-leak test's own docstring justified invoking owner B's cascade directly (bypassing the HTTP layer) with "there is no second `sessions` row/login flow for a second owner in this fixture" -- not actually true, since the test already creates a full second identity a few lines earlier; the real reason is deliberate test-scope isolation, and the shortcut does mean that leg of the test doesn't also exercise `_disable_domain`'s own commit/`session.close()`/revoke sequencing (already proven generically elsewhere). Fixed by correcting the docstring rather than restructuring the test.

Security lens re-derived the round-8 ledger fix from first principles (transaction atomicity of the INSERT and the `email_messages` DELETE, `unnest(:candidate_ids::text[])` parameterization against SQL injection and empty/duplicate-id inputs, concurrent-cascade lock ordering, tenancy scoping in every new statement) and confirmed it all held up. It then found a genuine regression in round 8's *other* fix: the plain `AND revoked_at IS NULL` added to `revoke_consent_endpoint`'s consent lookup ran *before* `_disable_domain`'s own idempotency-cache check ever got a chance to fire, so a legitimate same-`Idempotency-Key` retry of a revoke that had already succeeded (no re-grant in between) would 404 instead of returning the cached response -- the first successful call itself sets `revoked_at`, so the identical retry's own pre-check now (wrongly) rejected it. Fixed by replacing the blanket `revoked_at IS NULL` check with `revoked_at IS NULL OR NOT EXISTS (a later grant for the same domain)`: a revoked consent with nothing superseding it still resolves normally (the true idempotent-retry case), while a revoked consent a later grant actually superseded is still rejected (round 8's original threat case) -- verified against real Postgres for all three cases (active, revoked-no-regrant, revoked-then-regranted) before landing.

**Round 10 (security/correctness and architecture/quality, launched concurrently, explicitly told the bind-parameter sweep that caught round 9's CRITICAL bug needed to be more rigorous, not less): one MEDIUM and one LOW security finding, three doc/test-quality findings, all fixed.** Security lens re-traced every `:name` bind parameter in the diff for the same class of substitution bug round 9 found (none found -- the round-9 fix is the only cast-adjacent bind param in the whole diff), then found a genuine TOCTOU race: `revoke_consent_endpoint`'s round-8/9 staleness check ran in its own transaction, committed *before* `_disable_domain` was even called -- a concurrent re-grant could commit in the gap between that check and `_disable_domain`'s own `FOR UPDATE` lock, and the disable would proceed against the freshly re-granted state anyway using a `consent_id` that had already gone stale by the time the mutation actually ran (owner-scoped, not a cross-tenant issue, but a real reintroduction of the exact bug rounds 8-9 were built to prevent). Fixed by threading `consent_id` through to `_disable_domain` and re-validating the `NOT EXISTS (a later grant)` check *inside* that function's own transaction, immediately after it acquires the same `personal_domains` row lock `_enable_domain`'s own re-grant path also acquires -- whichever transaction gets the lock first is authoritative. New test `test_revoke_consent_endpoint_serializes_against_a_concurrent_regrant` proves the underlying mechanism: while a raw connection holds that row's lock, a concurrent `POST /consents` call for the same domain genuinely blocks rather than racing ahead. Security lens also found a LOW same-timestamp blind spot in the `>` comparison (coarse clock resolution could tie a regrant's `granted_at` with a revoke's `revoked_at`); widened to `>=` to fail closed on a tie.

Architecture lens re-verified round 9's bind-parameter fix and the ledger/seed-script changes as correct, then found: `TEST-PLAN.md` was never updated for round 9's own consent-lookup refinement (a doc-staleness recurrence of the exact class six prior rounds already fixed elsewhere) -- corrected; `_cleanup_workspace`'s explicit table list omitted `email_message_id_purge_log` (harmless today, since it's `ON DELETE CASCADE` from `workspaces`, but breaks the file's own "list every workspace-scoped table it touches" convention) -- added; and the round-9 idempotency-replay test's sole assertion (response-body equality) wasn't by itself proof of a real cache hit versus a coincidentally-identical recompute -- strengthened with a direct `audit_events` row-count assertion (`write_side_effects` has no dedup, so a genuine second execution would leave two rows).

**Round 11 (security/correctness and architecture/quality, launched concurrently): two MEDIUM findings and one doc-dedup finding, all fixed; a fourth issue -- a CI-caught bug in this session's own new test, not the application -- found and fixed separately.** Security lens confirmed round 10's TOCTOU fix in `_disable_domain` was correct and complete, then found the identical class of gap still open one call site over: `export_deletion.py:delete_domain_endpoint`'s own `get_domain(session, auth, domain_key)` call had no `for_update=True`, so a concurrent `POST /consents` re-grant could commit while this endpoint's own trailing unconditional `UPDATE personal_domains`/`UPDATE domain_consents` writes were in flight, silently clobbering the fresh grant with no error surfaced to the caller who just re-granted. Fixed by adding `for_update=True`, mirroring `_disable_domain`'s own round-10 fix and cross-referencing its docstring rather than duplicating the reasoning.

Architecture lens found round 10's own TOCTOU narrative had been written in full twice -- once in `_disable_domain`'s docstring, once again in `revoke_consent_endpoint`'s inline comment above the `domain_key` lookup, ~90% overlapping -- the same duplicated-narrative pattern round 8 already fixed once elsewhere in this file. Trimmed the inline comment to a short cross-reference, keeping the sole full copy in `_disable_domain`'s own docstring. Architecture lens also flagged the round-10 concurrency test's connection handling as non-idiomatic and leak-prone: `holder = engine.connect()` plus raw `text("BEGIN")`/`text("COMMIT")` executed before a `try` block (a setup failure would skip the `finally` and leak the connection), and bypasses SQLAlchemy's own autobegin tracking -- confirmed in practice via a subsequent CI log tail showing a harmless-but-confusing `WARNING: there is already a transaction in progress`. Security lens separately noted the same test never actually exercised `revoke_consent_endpoint`'s own code path under real concurrency, only generic `_enable_domain` lock contention via an unrelated `POST /consents` call -- a regression in the `consent_id` re-check itself would not have failed it. Both closed together by rewriting `test_revoke_consent_endpoint_serializes_against_a_concurrent_regrant`: it now performs a real initial revoke, holds the `personal_domains` row lock via `with engine.connect() as holder:` (proper context-manager scoping, relying on autobegin), submits a *replay* of the now-superseded `consent_id` via `revoke_consent_endpoint` in a background thread (which blocks acquiring the same lock inside `_disable_domain`), performs the exact regrant `_enable_domain` would over the SAME held connection while the replay is blocked, releases the lock, and asserts the replay observes the fresh grant and rejects with `404 CONSENT_NOT_FOUND` rather than silently disabling the just-re-granted domain.

Separately, CI on the round-10 push caught a real bug in round 9's own idempotency-replay test, `test_revoke_consent_endpoint_idempotency_key_replay_without_regrant_still_succeeds`: its `replay_resp.json() == first_resp.json()` assertion failed because `request_id`/`correlation_id` -- per-HTTP-call tracing fields injected into every response envelope by outer middleware, not part of `DomainResponse`'s own field list -- legitimately differ between the two calls even on a genuine cache hit. Fixed by excluding those two keys from the comparison; the `audit_event_count == 1` assertion immediately after was already, and remains, the actual proof of a cache hit.

Loop 2 review round 12 in progress.
